Symmetry-enriched topological order in tensor networks: Defects, gauging and anyon condensation
Dominic J. Williamson, Nick Bultinck, Frank Verstraete

Abstract
We study symmetry-enriched topological order in two-dimensional tensor network states by using graded matrix product operator algebras to represent symmetry induced domain walls. A close connection to the theory of graded unitary fusion categories is established. Tensor network representations of the topological defect superselection sectors are constructed for all domain walls. The emergent symmetry-enriched topological order is extracted from these representations, including the symmetry action on the underlying anyons. Dual phase transitions, induced by gauging a global symmetry, and condensation of a bosonic subtheory, are analyzed and the relationship between topological orders on either side of the transition is derived. Several examples are worked through explicitly.



I
Introduction

Symmetry plays a fundamental role in the classification of phases of matter.
From spontaneous symmetry breaking in the Landau-Ginzburg theory of second-order phase transitions
Landau and Lifshitz 1965
to the homeomorphism invariance of topological quantum field theories (TQFTs)
Segal 1988
;
Witten 1988
;
Atiyah 1988
.
Even in the absence of a global symmetry, equivalence classes of gapped Hamiltonians under adiabatic deformation break up into inequivalent topological phases
Wegner 1971
;
Kosterlitz and Thouless 1973
;
Wen 1989
;
Einarsson 1990
;
Wen 1990
. These phases have topological symmetries inherited from the TQFTs that describe their low energy behavior, which are often realized by nontrivial string operators.
Enforcing a global symmetry drastically refines the classification of phases that is found. Even the trivial phase splits into a set of symmetry-protected topological phases, which cannot be adiabatically connected while preserving the symmetry
Haldane 1983
;
Gu and Wen 2009
;
Pollmann et al. 2010
;
Chen et al. 2013
.
Nontrivial topological phases also split into symmetry-enriched topological phases which are distinguished by the interplay of the global symmetry with their topological superselection sectors
Turaev 2000
;
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Kitaev 2006
;
Drinfeld et al. 2010
;
Etingof et al. 2009
;
Bombin 2010
;
Hung and Wen 2013
;
Mesaros and Ran 2013
;
Barkeshli et al. 2014
;
Tarantino et al. 2016
;
Teo et al. 2015
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
.

The interplay of a global symmetry with topological degrees of freedom has also received attention from the quantum information community due to its relevance for topological quantum codes and computation
Beigi et al. 2011
;
Bombin 2010
;
Brown et al. 2013
;
Yoshida 2017
;
Pastawski and Yoshida 2015
;
Bravyi and König 2013
;
Beverland et al. 2016
;
Yoshida 2015
;
Bridgeman et al. 2017
;
Webster and Bartlett 2017
.
Transversal gates on topological codes are generated by the action of on-site symmetries on superselection sectors, and symmetry defects have been used to alter the code properties of a topological order.
In the quantum information approach to many-body condensed matter systems, tensor networks
Verstraete et al. 2009
;
Orús 2014
;
Bridgeman and Chubb 2017
are used to describe ground states and excitations.
These provide efficient representations that faithfully capture the local entanglement structure of the many-body states.
In one spatial dimension, matrix product states (MPS) have been used to classify gapped phases by studying the way a global symmetry acts on the auxiliary entanglement degrees of freedom
White 1992
;
Östlund and Rommer 1995
;
Dukelsky et al. 1998
;
Affleck et al. 1987
;
Fannes et al. 1992
;
Klümper et al. 1993
;
Pérez-García et al. 2007
;
Chen et al. 2011a
;
Schuch et al. 2011
;
Cirac et al. 2017
.
In two spatial dimensions, projected entangled pair states (PEPS) have been used to study topological phases
Nishino et al. 2001
;
Verstraete and Cirac 2004
;
Schuch et al. 2010
;
Buerschaper 2014
;
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
;
Bridgeman et al. 2016
;
Williamson et al. 2016a
;
Bultinck et al. 2017b
;
Bridgeman et al. 2017
. A classification of nonchiral phases has been obtained from the algebra of matrix product operator (MPO) symmetries on the entanglement degrees of freedom of a local PEPS tensor that satisfy a pulling through equation. The resulting topological phases are given by Morita equivalence
Müger 2003
classes of MPO symmetry algebras.
This class of tensor networks includes all the string-net states
Levin and Wen 2005
, for which explicit PEPS and MPO representations have been found
Buerschaper et al. 2009
;
Gu et al. 2009
;
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
.
Chiral phases have also been studied
Yang et al. 2015
;
Dubail and Read 2015
;
Wahl et al. 2013
, however a complete coherent theoretical framework is lacking.

In this work, we study PEPS representations of symmetry-enriched nonchiral topological phases. We focus on the interplay of a global symmetry with an MPO algebra symmetry on the entanglement degrees of freedom. To this end we consider the generalized symmetry-enriched pulling through equation introduced in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
.
The resulting SETs are classified by graded Morita equivalence classes of MPO symmetry algebras.
We demonstrate that this class of tensor networks includes the recently introduced symmetry-enriched string-net models
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
;
Chang et al. 2015
by constructing the local PEPS and MPO tensors.
We generalize Ocneanu’s tube algebra
Ocneanu 1994
;
Evans and Kawahigashi 1995
;
Evans and Kawahigashi 1998
to include nontrivial defect sectors, from which we construct the superselection sectors of the emergent SET order.
We explain how the physical data of the SET can be extracted from this construction.
We calculate the effects of gauging the global symmetry
Wilson 1974
;
Kogut and Susskind 1975
;
Levin and Gu 2012
;
Haegeman et al. 2015a
, and the relation of the SET to the resulting topological phase. We also find an anyon condensation phase transition
Bais and Slingerland 2009
— dual to gauging — that is induced by breaking a graded MPO symmetry algebra, and calculate the relation of the topological order to the resulting SET.
The nonchiral case of many of the general results shown in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
follow directly from our construction.
The mathematical content of our work includes an explicit construction of the automorphism induced on the Drinfeld center (double) by a given extension of a UFC
Etingof et al. 2009
, which forms part of our more general construction of the defect tube algebra and graded Drinfeld center from a graded UFC
Gelaki et al. 2009
.
Several examples are provided to illustrate various aspects of our formalism.

The paper is organized as follows:
In Section
II
, we present a detailed analysis of the electromagnetic duality symmetry-enriched toric code: including a summary of relevant background results from Refs.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
and
Bultinck et al. 2017a
, a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauging procedure that maps it to the doubled Ising model, and a dual
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
anyon condensation phase transition.
In Section
III
, we write down explicit tensor network representations of the symmetry-enriched string-net ground states and their
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry algebras, and show that they satisfy the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation.
In Section
IV
, we summarize the results of Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
including a derivation of Ocneanu’s tube algebra from the MPO symmetry algebra of a tensor network, a construction of the emergent topological superselection sectors from the tube algebra, and the extraction of physical data from superselection sectors thus constructed.
In Section
V
, we describe the theory of
\mathcal{G}
-graded matrix product operator algebras in terms of
\mathcal{G}
-extensions of an underlying MPO algebra, we draw an analogy to the theory of
\mathcal{G}
-graded unitary fusion categories, and define the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation for a tensor network.
In Section
VI
, we generalize the tube algebra to include nontrivial
\mathcal{G}
-defect sectors, which are constructed from a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry algebra of a tensor network, we describe a construction of the emergent SET order, and the extraction of its physical data.
In Section
VII
, we calculate the effect that gauging a global
\mathcal{G}
symmetry has upon a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry algebra, derive the relationship between the emergent SET and the topological order that results from gauging, and demonstrate the gauging procedure explicitly for SPT phases.
In Section
VIII
, we describe the
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
anyon condensation phase transitions that are induced by
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra symmetry breaking, these phase transitions are dual to gauging a global
\mathcal{G}
symmetry, we derive the relation between the emergent topological order and the SET that results from the condensation.
In Section
IX
, we present several examples that demonstrate different aspects of our formalism, including the interplay between Morita equivalence and anyon condensation phase transitions induced by MPO algebra symmetry breaking.
In Section
X
, we present conclusions and future directions.
In Appendix
A
, we describe how to sequentially gauge a global symmetry group, possessing a nontrivial normal subgroup, on the lattice.

II
A motivating example

We begin with a concrete example that demonstrates the methods developed throughout the paper. In this example we study the relation between the
doubled Ising model
Levin and Wen 2005
and the
toric code
Kitaev 2003
enriched by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
electromagnetic duality symmetry
Bombin 2010
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
. These models are related by the dual processes of gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
electromagnetic duality symmetry or condensing a
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
subtheory, as follows

Toric code
\scriptstyle{\text{Add }\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ defects}}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry-enriched

toric code

\scriptstyle{\text{Gauge }\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ symmetry}}
\scriptstyle{\text{Confine }\mathbb{Z}_{2}\text{ defects}}

Doubled

Ising model.

\scriptstyle{\text{Condense Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})}

Each of the steps in this diagram can be calculated concretely in the language of tensor networks. To achieve this we summarize the mathematical objects, known as
unitary fusion categories
, from which the toric code and doubled Ising model are built
Etingof et al. 2005
;
Etingof et al. 2015
.
Using this data, we recount the tensor network description of the toric code and doubled Ising model string-net ground states
Buerschaper et al. 2009
;
Gu et al. 2009
and the algebras of matrix product operator symmetries they posses
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
.
We present an extension of the fixed point tensor network construction to the recently defined class of symmetry-enriched string-net models
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
, and demonstrate the interplay of the MPO symmetries with the physical symmetry. This extension is based upon the notion of graded unitary fusion categories
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Turaev 2000
;
Etingof et al. 2009
. By viewing the Ising fusion category as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion category the authors of Refs.
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
were able to construct a local commuting projector Hamiltonian, in two spatial dimensions, with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-enriched toric code topological order where an on-site group action implements the electromagnetic duality of the toric code anyons.

To calculate the action of the symmetry explicitly with the tensor network approach we build upon the anyon ansatz presented in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
, which is summarized in Section
IV
.
In that work, a second algebra — corresponding to
Ocneanu’s tube algebra

Ocneanu 1994
;
Evans and Kawahigashi 1995
;
Evans and Kawahigashi 1998
— was derived from the matrix product operator symmetry algebra of the local tensors. By diagonalizing this tube algebra into irreducible blocks, the topological sectors are constructed. From the projectors onto these blocks, the full set of topological data — characterizing the emergent anyonic excitations — can be extracted. This set of data forms a mathematical object known as a
modular tensor category
Bakalov and Kirillov 2001
;
Etingof et al. 2005
;
Kitaev 2006
;
Drinfeld et al. 2010
;
Etingof et al. 2015
which includes an underlying set of anyons, their fusion rules and
F
-symbols,
R
matrices which describe braiding processes, as well as the gauge invariant Frobenius-Schur indicators, and the modular
S
and
T
matrices.

We extend the tube algebra to a set of
|\mathcal{G}|

defect tube algebras
whose irreducible blocks describe the definite topological
\mathcal{G}
-defect sectors.
Similar to the anyon tubes, all topological data of the emergent symmetry-enriched theory can be extracted from the defect tubes. This data constitutes a
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular tensor category
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Turaev 2000
;
Etingof et al. 2009
;
Barkeshli et al. 2014
;
Gelaki et al. 2009
, and includes the underlying set of topological defects, their fusion rules and
F
-symbols, the
\mathcal{G}
action on the set of defects, the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
R
matrices which describe the braiding of defects, the projective representation carried by each defect and its 2-cocycle
{\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}
, and the projective representation on the fusion and splitting spaces
U_{{\text{\bf{{$\text{\bf{g}}$}}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}}})
, as well as the Frobenius-Schur indicators, and the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular
S
and
T
matrices.

Rather than calculating the topological defect sectors of the EM duality enriched toric code directly, we develop an extremely simple recipe to find the effect of
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})

anyon condensation
on the tubes of the doubled Ising model. This approach yields the pair of inequivalent topological defect sectors, known as
Bombin 2010

\sigma_{+}
,
\sigma_{-}
. We also describe a recipe to calculate the effects of
gauging
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
EM duality symmetry on the defect tubes to recover the tubes of the doubled Ising model.
We derive relations between the topological data of the models related by the dual gauging and condensation procedures.

II.1
Background: Constructing topological sectors in a tensor network

II.1.1
Toric code

The toric code is based on a very simple unitary fusion category
\mathcal{C}=\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
built from the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
. We denote the simple objects
\{0,\psi\}
with abelian multiplication

\displaystyle 0\times a=a,\quad\psi\times\psi=0,

(13)

for
a\in\{0,\psi\}
.
The
F
-symbols are trivial, containing only the fusion constraints

\displaystyle{F^{abc}_{def}}=\delta_{ab}^{e}\delta_{ec}^{d}\delta_{bc}^{f}\delta_{af}^{d},

(14)

which are defined by

\displaystyle\delta^{c}_{ab}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{when $a$ appears in the fusion product }b\times c\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

A tensor network representation of the toric code ground state on a trivalent lattice can be constructed from these
F
-symbols, see Eq.(
II.1.1
) left. This is a specific instance of the more general tensor network representation of string-net ground states
Buerschaper et al. 2009
;
Gu et al. 2009
;
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
. This tensor network specifies a state in a Hilbert space where the qubit degrees of freedom that usually live on edges live in the diagonal subspace of a pair of qubits living on the neighbouring vertices

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{e}\cong\text{span}\{\ket{00},\ket{11}\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{u}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]_{v},

(17)

where
\partial e=\{u,v\}
. This convention is used as it avoids the need to define separate tensors on the edges of the tensor network.

This tensor network has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
MPO symmetry specified by the tensor on the right of Eq.(
II.1.1
).

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCsplitting}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPO}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}{F^{a\mu i}_{\beta\alpha\nu}}\,,

where all labels lie in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Here
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\{0,\psi\}
controls which element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
the MPO represents

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{a}:=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{smallTCmpo}\end{array},

where the vertical dashed lines denote periodic boundary conditions and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOclosure}\end{array}:=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}\delta_{b,a}.

We will also make use of the following short hand notation for the projector onto the
a
block

\displaystyle\raisebox{5.69046pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOclosure1}\end{array}$}:=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOclosure2}\end{array}.

These MPOs reproduce the fusion algebra in Eq.(
13
)

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{0}\text{MPO}_{a}=\text{MPO}_{a},

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{\psi}\text{MPO}_{\psi}=\text{MPO}_{0}.

(24)

In fact, the individual MPO tensors satisfy the fusion rules locally (left), up to a fusion/splitting tensor (right)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOmultLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c}\delta_{ab}^{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOmultRHS}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCMPOfusion}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}{F^{ab\gamma}_{\alpha c\beta}}.

This MPO representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
falls into the framework develop in Refs.
Schuch et al. 2010
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
.

The local order of fusion/splitting does not matter in this case

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCFsymbolLHS}\end{array}=\sum_{f}{F^{abc}_{def}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCFsymbolRHS}\end{array},

as the
F
-symbols are trivial and simply enforce the fusion constraint
\delta_{bc}^{f}\delta_{af}^{d}
.

The toric code tensor network and its
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
MPO symmetry satisfy the pulling through equation (left), as well as a constraint that allows one to remove a contractible loop of the MPO from the tensor network (right)

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{smallTCPullthruLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\raisebox{11.38092pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{smallTCPullthruRHS}\end{array}$},

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCBubbleLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}^{k}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCBubbleRHS}\end{array}.

Furthermore the toric code tensor network is MPO-injective, in the terminology of Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
, with respect to the MPO projector onto the symmetric subspace:
\frac{1}{2}(\text{MPO}_{0}+\text{MPO}_{\psi})
.

The framework developed in Refs.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
;
Aasen et al. 2016
demonstrated that calculations involving an algebra of MPO symmetries on the virtual level of an MPO-injective tensor network follow the same rules as the planar isotopy invariant, diagrammatic calculus for unitary fusion categories described in Refs.
Joyal and Street 1991
;
Joyal and Street 1993
;
Kitaev 2006
and elsewhere.
In other words, the properties of an MPO algebra on the virtual level of an MPO-injective tensor network can be abstracted to the diagrammatic calculus for anyons (without braiding).

To see this from another perspective, recall that the vector space associated to a manifold by the Turaev-Viro
topological quantum field theory
(TQFT)
Turaev and Viro 1992
;
Barrett and Westbury 1996
— for a given unitary fusion category — is defined to be the space of allowed pictures modulo local relations
Walker 2006
;
Freedman et al. 2011
(this is isomorphic to the ground space of the string net model on that manifold).
In this way a tensor network on the sphere satisfying MPO-injectivity, together with its MPO symmetry algebra, can be thought of as an explicit representation of all pictures that can be reduced to the empty diagram via local relations.
In this representation the local relations are implemented by the pulling through equation, together with the local conditions satisfied by the MPOs.
We remark that one can construct the full set of different ground states by closing the tensor network using different MPOs, as described below.

The upshot of the previous paragraph is: for the purposes of calculations involving elements of the MPO algebra within an MPO-injective tensor network, we can work at the level of the diagrammatic UFC calculus
Joyal and Street 1991
;
Joyal and Street 1993
;
Kitaev 2006
. The MPO-injective tensor network itself can be thought of as a sort of background, and MPOs on the virtual level will become loops on top of this background. For example

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCPEPS}\end{array}

=

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCpsiPEPS}\end{array}

\mapsto

\mapsto

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCbackground}\end{array}

\sim

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCpsibackground}\end{array}

where the
\psi
loop is depicted as a squiggly line in the diagrammatic calculus. As demonstrated by this example local relations in the diagrammatic calculus correspond to equalities in the tensor network representation. For this reason we will work with the vector space of diagrams modulo local relations, hence the equivalence above becomes an equality.

As was mentioned above, on a topologically nontrivial manifold the vector space of the Turaev-Viro TQFT may have dimension greater than one. Equivalently the string-net model may have a ground state degeneracy
Koenig et al. 2010
.
This appears in the tensor network formalism when one considers closing a disc to form a nontrivial topology. It was shown in Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
that the MPO-injectivity property is stable under concatenation. That is, one can contract arbitrarily many MPO-injective tensors on a disc and the resulting tensor will maintain the MPO-injectivity property.
The resulting tensor network is a PEPS, and hence comes equipped with a frustration free, local, parent Hamiltonian for which it is a ground state
Pérez-García et al. 2008
.
It was also shown in Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
, that the ground space of this Hamiltonian on a disc is spanned by the tensor network on the disc contracted with an arbitrary boundary tensor, see Eq.(
II.1.1
), LHS.
We remark that the local relations in the bulk give rise to an action of the MPO algebra on the boundary tensor which project it onto the symmetric subspace.
A generalization of this story also holds in higher spatial dimensions
Walker 2006
;
Sahinoglu et al.
.

The local tensor in Eq.(
II.1.1
) can be used to build a unique state on a closed manifold.
However there may be linearly independent states on the same manifold that are constructed from the same tensor on any disc, but also contain virtual level MPOs that are free to move through the tensor network. As a matter of terminology, we will refer to a spanning set of ground states of the local parent Hamiltonian on a given manifold as the set of tensor networks assigned to that manifold by the local tensor.

To form a closed manifold one can consider adding handles to a disc. The simplest choice is to close the disc into a sphere. Even in this case one finds a closure tensor, located on a single link, that is free to move throughout the tensor network
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
. The different choices for this closure tensor pick out different ground states in an unstable TQFT. Here unstable means that the TQFT has a ground state degeneracy on the sphere. For a stable TQFT, which is always the case when the MPO algebra yields a unitary fusion category, there is a single ground state on the sphere and the closure tensor is trivial. Since an unstable TQFT can be written as a sum of stable TQFTs we will only consider the stable case from here on.

The next example we consider is attaching a 1-handle to a disc to form a cylinder. Before the closure, the class of states we are considering corresponds to the tensor network with arbitrary boundary conditions
A
, depicted below on the left. After taking the closure we are guaranteed that any disc must look like the original tensor network. Hence the only boundary conditions along the attaching edge that survive are the ones that can be freely deformed through the tensor network. This implies that these boundary conditions must be a linear combination of the irreducible MPOs, shown below on the right. This argument was given explicitly in Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCpuncturedsphere0}\end{array}=\sum_{p\mu}c_{p}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCpuncturedsphere2}\end{array},

where the dotted lines indicate a periodic identification of the boundaries and the solid line connecting the caps indicates that they are part of the same tensor.
Hence all boundary conditions on the cylinder can be divided into an arbitrary tensor
A^{\prime}:=\sum_{\mu}B_{\mu}\otimes C_{\mu}
, acting on the open left and right boundaries, joined by a linear combination of MPOs along the closure edge. More specifically, the virtual indices of the MPO are contracted with indices of
A^{\prime}
.

Similarly, when closing a disc to form a torus, the most general boundary condition is given by a linear combination of MPOs along the cycles of the torus. Furthermore the tensor at their intersection point can be resolved into a linear combination of fusion and splitting vertices, shown below on the right.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCdiscclosure0}\end{array}=\sum_{xyz}c_{xy}^{z}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTorus}\end{array}=\sum_{xyz}c_{xy}^{z}\ket{xyz}.

For the toric code, a basis for the ground space on the torus is given by

\displaystyle\ket{000}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTorus0}\end{array}\,,\quad\ket{0\psi\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTorus1}\end{array}\,,\quad\ket{\psi 0\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTorus2}\end{array}\,,\quad\ket{\psi\psi 0}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTorus3}\end{array}\,.

To find the superselection sectors within this tensor network, we consider a well separated pair of pointlike excitations on a sphere. This sphere can be stretched out to a cylinder with an excitation at each end. Away from these excitation points the tensor network locally looks the same, hence the whole tensor network is of the form in Eq.(
II.1.1
) with
A^{\prime}
now also containing physical indices located around each excitation.
The local relations in the bulk of the tensor network on the cylinder give rise to an MPO action on each boundary.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCCylinder0}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCCylinder1}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCCylinder2}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q}\delta_{rs}^{q}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCCylinder3}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{qr}\delta_{rs}^{q}\delta_{sq}^{p}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCCylinder4}\end{array}\,.

This defines an algebra acting on each of the punctures. In fact, this is Ocneanu’s celebrated
tube algebra
.
We remark that the tube algebra contains a copy of the fusion algebra, generated by
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{0s0}
, but is generally distinct from the fusion algebra.
The irreducible superselection sectors within a puncture are given by the irreducible blocks of the tube algebra.
Projecting onto a superselection sector fixes a definite anyon flux through the puncture.
A particular sector can be constructed by projecting onto the corresponding block of the tube algebra using the
irreducible central idempotent
(ICI) that acts as the identity within that block.

We use the following labeling convention for basis elements of the tube algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tube0}\end{array}\,,

corresponding to the minimal tensor network:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCtensortube}\end{array},

note
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
is only nonzero when
\delta_{qs}^{r}\delta_{sq}^{p}=1
, hence the dimension of the algebra is given by

\sum\limits_{pqrs}\delta_{qs}^{r}\delta_{sq}^{p}<|\mathcal{C}|^{4}\,,

where
|\mathcal{C}|
is the number of simple objects in the fusion algebra, in this case
|\mathcal{C}|=2
.

Multiplication of tubes is defined by the stacking operation

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}r^{\prime}}=\delta_{rp^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTubemult}\end{array}=\delta_{rp^{\prime}}\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}s^{\prime\prime}}{F^{sqs^{\prime}}_{q^{\prime}rq^{\prime\prime}}}{F^{q^{\prime\prime}s^{\prime}s}_{pqs^{\prime\prime}}}{F^{s^{\prime}sq^{\prime\prime}}_{r^{\prime}q^{\prime}s^{\prime\prime}}}\ \mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{pq^{\prime\prime}r^{\prime}}\,,

where the only tubes
\mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{pq^{\prime\prime}r^{\prime}}
that appear on the RHS with nonzero coefficients satisfy
\delta_{ss^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime\prime}}
.
Hermitian conjugation is given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTubedagger}\end{array}=\sum_{q^{\prime}}{F^{srs}_{pqq^{\prime}}}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{rq^{\prime}p}\,.

With this notation Eq.(
II.1.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{0}_{rrr}

\displaystyle=\sum_{pq}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{ppp}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}.

(57)

We have verified that the tube algebra is closed under multiplication and Hermitian conjugation. Hence the tube algebra is a
C^{*}
algebra, spanned by the tubes in Eq.(
II.1.1
) and can be block diagonalized.

The toric code tube algebra is spanned by the following basis elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTube0}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTube1}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTube2}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TCTube3}\end{array}\,.

We remark that this tube algebra splits into two copies of
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{2}]
and hence is diagonalized by a set of projectors onto the irreducible representations (irreps) of each
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
These projectors are summarized in the following table

Anyons

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0}

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}

0

1

1

e

1

-1

m

1

1

em

1

-1

which contains the coefficients of each ICI. In this table — and for all superselection sector tables throughout the paper — we suppress a normalization by the quantum dimension squared
\mathcal{D}^{2}
, which gives
2
in this case.
Hence the ICIs of the toric code are given by

\displaystyle\underline{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})\,,\quad\underline{e}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}-\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})\,,\quad\underline{m}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi})\,,\quad\underline{em}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}-\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi})\,.

(67)

We remark that the vacuum
\underline{0}
superselection sector is always given by the projector onto the symmetric subspace of the fusion algebra generated by
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{0s0}
.
To justify the labeling of the other superselection sectors, we need a method to extract the physical data characterizing the anyon theory from the ICIs.
The
S
and
T
matrices of the emergent topological theory are independent of any gauge choice in defining the input fusion category.
These invariants prove very useful for the classification and identification of non-chiral theories, which are precisely those arising from the double construction we have described. In the current example
S
and
T
turn out to uniquely specify the anyon theory amongst other possibilities with the same number of anyons, however they are known to be incomplete invariants in general
Mignard and Schauenburg 2017
.

On the tube algebra
S
and
T
are defined as

\displaystyle S(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})

\displaystyle:=\delta_{pr}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{STube0}\end{array}=\sum_{q^{\prime}}{F^{sps}_{pq^{\prime}q}}\mathcal{T}^{p}_{sq^{\prime}s}\,,

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}^{p}_{p1p}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

\displaystyle S(\underline{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underline{b}}S_{ab}\,\underline{b}\,,

\displaystyle T(\underline{a})

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}\,\underline{a}\,,

(69)

in writing the second formula for
S
we have assumed that all idempotents have dimension one, which is true for the toric code example. The general formula is given in Section
III
, Eq.(
IV.3
).
We remark that
S
corresponds to changing basis on the torus from a definite flux through the
y
-cycle to a definite flux through the
x
-cycle. Similarly
T
corresponds to a Dehn twist along the
y
-cycle of the torus with definite flux through the
y
-cycle, or equivalently a
2\pi
rotation of a superselection sector.

For toric code
S
and
T
are given by

\mathcal{D}^{2}S

We remark the normalization of the
S
matrix by
\mathcal{D}^{2}
refers to the quantum dimension of the input fusion category which satisfies
\mathcal{D}^{2}=\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}
. In this example the normalization is
\mathcal{D}^{2}=2
.
The
T
matrix uniquely identifies the
em
sector as it is the only fermion in the theory. However one can see that the choice of
e
and
m
sectors is arbitrary as the data is invariant under swapping
e
with
m
. This is an EM duality
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry of the toric code. In the next section we will construct the symmetry enriched toric code, including the EM duality defects.

In fact we can also extract the full set of gauge-variant data, the
F
and
R
symbols, that define the emergent topological theory from the ICIs. The ICIs can be used to construct a realization of the emergent theory, which may be of use for topological quantum computation in wavefunctions that admit a tensor network description.

Throughout this subsection all explanations were given with the toric code in mind and hence many subtleties have been left out. In particular we neglected orientation dependence, factors of the quantum dimension, pivotal phases, Frobenius-Schur indicators and possible fusion degeneracy. These subtleties are covered in Section
III
.

II.1.2
Doubled Ising model

The Ising fusion category is based on an algebra of three objects which we denote
\{0,\psi,\sigma\}
with commutative multiplication rules

\displaystyle 0\times a=a,\quad\psi\times\psi=0,\quad\sigma\times\psi=\sigma,\quad\sigma\times\sigma=0+\psi,

(80)

for
a\in\{0,\psi,\sigma\}
. The quantum dimensions are
d_{0}=1,d_{\psi}=1,d_{\sigma}=\sqrt{2}
, hence
\mathcal{D}^{2}=4
. The nontrivial
F
-symbols are given by

\displaystyle{[F^{\sigma\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma}]_{i}^{j}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\begin{matrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{matrix}\right],\quad{[F^{\sigma\psi\sigma}_{\psi}]_{\sigma}^{\sigma}}={[F^{\psi\sigma\psi}_{\sigma}]_{\sigma}^{\sigma}}=-1,

(81)

for
i,j\in\{0,\psi\}
.
The remaining
F
-symbols are either
0
or
1
, which is completely determined by the convention that
{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}
contains the constraint
\delta^{e}_{ab}\delta^{d}_{ec}\delta^{f}_{bc}\delta^{d}_{af}
, defined in Eq.(
II.1.1
). One can check that these
F
-symbols satisfy the pentagon equation.
We remark that
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is contained as a subcategory of Ising, generated by the objects
\{0,\psi\}
. This observation is the basis of the construction in Section
II.2.2
.

The tensor network and MPO algebra for the Ising model are defined in a similar fashion to the toric code example. Both examples fall under the general case that covers all string-net models
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
, which is described in Section
III
. Here we will skip directly to the tube algebra.
The construction of the tube algebra proceeds similarly to the toric code, but one must also deal with the appearance of quantum dimensions.
For an explanation of the explicit details we have skipped over see Section
III
.

The Ising tube algebra is twelve dimensional and is spanned by the tubes in Eq.(
II.1.1
), together with the following

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\sigma\sigma\sigma}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube0}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\sigma\sigma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube1}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{0\sigma 0}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube4}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube6}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\sigma 0\sigma}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube2}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\sigma\psi\sigma}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube3}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma 0}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube7}\end{array}\,,\quad\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{0\sigma\psi}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{IsingTube5}\end{array}\,.

The coefficients of the ICIs that determine the superselection sectors of the tensor network are given by

.
To be explicit, the table should be read as follows

(100)

where

\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
differs slightly from that in Ref.

by a factor
\sqrt{d_{s}}
, after adjusting for this difference our results match those reported in Ref.

.

In the Ising tube algebra the
\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}
block is two dimensional. The full block, including off diagonal elements, is given by

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}-\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}_{01}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{0\sigma\psi}

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma 0}

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}_{11}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}).

(101)

To justify the labeling of the particles we have calculated the
S
and
T
matrices from the ICIs using the formula in Section
III
, Eq.(
IV.3
). Note that since
\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}
is a two dimensional block, the
S
matrix is defined in terms of its action on the normalized ICI
\frac{1}{2}\underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{2}S=\ \begin{tabular}[]{ |c | c c c | c | c | c c c|}\cline{1-4}\cr\cline{6-9}\cr&$0$&$\psi$&$\sigma$&&&${\overline{0}}$&${\overline{\psi}}$&${\overline{\sigma}}$\\

\cline{1-4}\cr\cline{6-9}\cr\end{tabular}

\cline{1-4}\cr\cline{6-9}\cr\end{tabular}

We remark the
S
and
T
matrices have a tensor product structure since the input Ising fusion category admits a modular braiding. This implies
Muger 2003

\mathcal{Z}({\text{Ising}})\cong\text{Ising}^{(1)}\boxtimes{\overline{\text{Ising}}}^{(1)}
, where
\text{Ising}^{(1)}
denotes the Ising theory equipped with a modular braiding following the convention of Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
.

II.2
Symmetry-enriched tensor networks from anyon condensation

II.2.1
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-paramagnet from the toric code

To demonstrate the condensation procedure we note the toric code trivially has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}=\{0\}\oplus\{\psi\}
, where we use the following presentation
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\cong\left\langle{\text{\bf{x}}}\ |\ {\text{\bf{x}}}^{2}={{\text{\bf{1}}}}\right\rangle
. We make use of the notation
\psi_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}
to indicate
\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}
and
\lfloor{\psi}\rfloor={\text{\bf{x}}}
to indicate the sector containing
\psi
. In this example each sector contains only a single simple object, so there is no need to keep track of the sector label separately. However, we will explicitly keep track of the sectors to prepare the reader for more complicated examples.

The tensors for the symmetry-enriched model
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
are given by a simple modification of the string-net tensors, which amounts to promoting the sector label of the virtual indices to a physical degree of freedom.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCsplitting}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}}\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\lfloor{\beta}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\lfloor{\gamma}\rfloor}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}},

The set of MPOs for the SET string-net tensor are still given by Eq.(
II.1.1
), we simply reinterpret them in light of the additional
\mathcal{G}
-grading structure.
Hence the
1
sector of the MPO algebra is given by
\text{MPO}_{0}
and the
x
sector is given by
\text{MPO}_{\psi}
.
Since we are using the same MPOs they still satisfy the zipper condition,
F
-move, and the bubble popping relation introduced in Section
II.1
.

The pulling through equation must be modified in accordance with the
\mathcal{G}
-grading. That is, pulling through an MPO in sector
g
results in a
g
action on the physical indices

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCPullthruLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\raisebox{17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCPullthruRHS}\end{array}$}\,,

where

\displaystyle\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor0}\end{array}$}:=\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor1}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor2}\end{array}$}.

Similar to the purely topological case, the calculations involving MPOs within a tensor network that satisfies the SET pulling though condition can be abstracted to a diagrammatic calculus. This calculus additionally involves discs that are labeled by group elements, which represent the application of the on-site symmetry to the tensor network within the labeled region. For example

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCbackground1}\end{array}\sim\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCpsibackground1}\end{array}\,,

alternatively,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCbackground0}\end{array}\sim\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCpsibackground0}\end{array}\,,

where the
1
label, corresponding to the action of the trivial element, remains implicit. This corresponds to a diagrammatic calculus for a 2-category
Barkeshli et al. 2016
;
Cui and Wang 2017
where the set of objects is
\mathcal{G}
, the morphisms from
g
to
h
are given by the objects in
\mathcal{C}_{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}
, and the 2-morphisms are given by the 1-morphisms of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

The emergent SET order of the tensor network is described by a unitary
\mathcal{G}
-crossed braided fusion category. This mathematical structure captures the topological properties of extrinsic monodromy defects as they braid and fuse. The inequivalent monodromy defects for a given
{\text{\bf{g}}}\in\mathcal{G}
correspond to the superselection sectors at the end point of a
g
domain wall.
The defect superselection sectors can be calculated using a modification of the tube algebra, which is described in Section
VI
.

The SET is intimately related to a topological order obtained by gauging the
\mathcal{G}
symmetry
Gelaki et al. 2009
;
Barkeshli et al. 2014
;
Teo et al. 2015
, which can be done directly on the lattice
Haegeman et al. 2015a
;
Williamson et al. 2016b
;
Williamson 2016
. The superselection sectors of the gauged topological order can be derived from the defect superselection sectors and the action of the group upon them.
Alternatively, the defect sectors of the SET can be found via a
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation
Bais and Slingerland 2009
procedure on the gauged topological order.

Here we demonstrate the effect of condensing Rep
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
on the toric code tubes.
This is implemented by dividing the tube algebra into
1
and
x
sectors, according to the group element
\lfloor{p}\rfloor=\lfloor{r}\rfloor
in
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
. Next we simply drop the basis elements
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
with
\lfloor{s}\rfloor={\text{\bf{x}}}
, corresponding to a
\psi
line wrapping the tube, from the tube algebra.
The ICIs in the
1
sector correspond to anyons or
1
-defects, while those in the
x
sector correspond to
x
-defects.
Since the defect tube algebra in each sector becomes one dimensional, we find only the vacuum anyon and a single
x
-defect,
\Delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}
.

Anyons

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0}

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}

0

1

1

e

1

-1

m

1

1

em

1

-1

\mapsto

Sectors

Defects

Condensed

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}

\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action

anyons

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0}

\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}

1

0

0

1

1

e

1

x

\Delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}

m

1

1

em

1

In the right table we retain the implicit normalization of
\mathcal{D}^{2}=2
, from the toric code, in the coefficients of the ICIs. However, we also have multiple contributions to each defect ICI from the different anyon ICIs that condense into it. In this case the factors cancel and we simply have

\displaystyle\underline{0}=\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}

\displaystyle\underline{\Delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}=\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}.

(131)

From the table we see that the Rep
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
boson which condensed was the
e
particle of the toric code. By picking a different tensor network representation of the toric code one can also realize the equivalent condensation of
m
. The result is a trivial topological order with a single
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defect. Since the underlying topological order is trivial, the model is an SPT which is classified by the cohomology class of the associator for the defects. This label lies in
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
and for this example it is trivial.
Hence we have found a trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-paramagnet as expected.

The tubes that were dropped to make the defect tube algebra can now be used to realize the action of the symmetry on the defect sectors. This is achieved by projecting the collection of discarded tubes onto each defect ICI. Hence we find the action
\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0}
on the vacuum, as expected, and
\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi}
acting on the
\Delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}
defect. These actions generate linear representations of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, note the identity in each sector is given by the corresponding defect ICI.

With the representation of the symmetry for each defect we can also calculate the effect of gauging the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry. Specifically there is a simple recipe to calculate the full set of anyon ICIs of the gauged theory.
For each defect the post-gauging anyon ICIs are given by the projectors onto the irreps of the group action on that defect.
In the toric code example this implies

\displaystyle\underline{0}\ \mapsto\left.\begin{array}[]{c c c}\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})&=&\underline{0}\\
\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}-\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})&=&\underline{e}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\underline{\Delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}\ \mapsto\left.\begin{array}[]{c c c}\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi})&=&\underline{m}\\
\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{\psi\psi\psi}-\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{\psi 0\psi})&=&\underline{em}\end{array}\right..

We remark that there are much simpler ways to analyze the condensation / gauging procedure for the toric code, we have included it here to demonstrate our general approach on a familiar example.

II.2.2
Symmetry-enriched toric code from the doubled Ising model

The Ising fusion category has a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded structure
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}=\{0,\psi\}\oplus\{\sigma\}
. In this case there are two simple objects in the trivial sector, so it is necessary to keep track of the object and sector index separately.

A tensor network representation of the recently introduced SET string-net models
Cheng et al. 2016
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
is given by the following tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingsplitting}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}}\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\lfloor{\beta}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\lfloor{\gamma}\rfloor}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}}{\sqrt{d_{\beta}d_{k}}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingfusion}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}}\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\lfloor{\beta}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\lfloor{\gamma}\rfloor}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}^{*}}{\sqrt{d_{\beta}d_{k}}}

where the
F
-symbols are those of the input
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion category. For the current example they are given in Eq.(
81
). We remark that the tensors are related by a combination of reflection and complex conjugation.

The MPOs for this tensor network are the same as those for the usual string-net
Levin and Wen 2005
, which are described in Section
III
.
However, the MPOs in the
g
-sector now represent
g
-domain walls. The multiplication of these MPOs is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded, and in particular
\{\frac{1}{2}(\text{MPO}_{0}+\text{MPO}_{\psi}),\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\text{MPO}_{\sigma}\}
forms a representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

These domain walls require a group action to be moved through the lattice.
This is captured by the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation

\displaystyle\raisebox{-25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruLHS}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruRHS}\end{array}$},

\displaystyle\raisebox{-17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruRHS2}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruLHS2}\end{array}$}.

We have used the notation defined in Eq.
II.2.1
for the group action on the physical indices. The equation on the right is obtained from the left via complex conjugation and reflection. All other pulling through configurations can be derived from Eq.(
II.2.2
) by using properties of the string-net MPO given in Section
III
.

The defect tube algebra is spanned by the subset of Ising tubes
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
, given in Section
II.1.2
, with
s\in\{0,\psi\}
.
The algebra is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded, with the
1
sector containing tubes with
\lfloor{p}\rfloor=\lfloor{q}\rfloor={\text{\bf{1}}}
, and the
x
sector containing those with
\lfloor{p}\rfloor=\lfloor{q}\rfloor={\text{\bf{x}}}
.
The ICIs can be found by condensing the
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
boson in doubled Ising, corresponding to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading of the input Ising fusion category.
First the — possibly non-central — irreducible idempotents of the defect tube algebra are derived from those of the doubled Ising model, given in Eq.(
II.1.2
), by simply dropping the elements
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
with
s=\sigma
.
In this case all the resulting idempotents turn out to be central in the defect tube algebra. In particular the two non-central irreducible idempotents in the
{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}
block of doubled Ising split into distinct sectors after condensation.
The results are summarized in the following table.

The left table carries over the implicit normalization of
\mathcal{D}^{2}=4
from Eq.(
II.1.2
), but there are also two contributions to each of the condensed defects, except
e
and

. For example we have
\underline{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{T}^{0}_{000}+\mathcal{T}^{\psi}_{0\psi 0})
.

Each defect sector,
\underline{a}
, carries a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action,
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{a}}
. This is found by projecting the Ising tubes that were thrown away onto the ICI of each sector and normalizing the results so that they satisfy the unitarity condition
(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{a}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{a}}=\underline{a}
.

\displaystyle K\,(c_{0}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{0\sigma 0}+c_{1}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma\psi}+c_{2}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\psi\sigma 0}+c_{3}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{0\sigma\psi}+c_{4}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\sigma 0\sigma}+c_{5}\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{\sigma\psi\sigma})\,\underline{a}=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{a}},

(158)

for arbitrary constants
c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
and some positive constant normalization
K
that depends on the
c_{i}
. We remark that certain specific choices of
c_{i}
can result in a zero projection, but any generic choice will lead to a nonzero result.
There is a gauge freedom in the definition of
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{a}}
corresponding to multiplication by a coboundary. In particular, one can always choose
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{a}}=\underline{a}
for all
\underline{a}
.

The resulting
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{a}}
for the symmetry-enriched toric code are contained on the right of Eq.(
II.2.2
), note there is an implicit normalization by
\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}=2
there.
We remark that the tubes which were thrown away correspond to an
x
domain wall, hence projecting them onto a defect captures the action of the
x
domain wall.

The nontrivial group element
x
may permute each defect
\underline{a}
, resulting in a defect
{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\underline{a}
, this permutation is found by considering

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{a}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{a}})^{\dagger}={}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\underline{a}.

(159)

In our example we find the only nontrivial permutation corresponds to the EM duality
{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\underline{e}=\underline{m},{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\underline{m}=\underline{e}
as expected. This is summarized in the following table

\rho_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}

For each defect, the action of the subgroup that does not permute it may form a projective representation. For our example we find

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{0}})^{2}=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{0}}\,,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{em}})^{2}=-\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{em}}\,,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}})^{2}=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}}\,,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{\sigma_{-}}})^{2}=e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\sigma_{-}}}.

(167)

Note, the symmetry groups preserving
\underline{e}
and
\underline{m}
are trivial. In this case the projective phases of the
x
-defect sectors can be trivialized by a gauge transformation.

The
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
domain walls determine a basis of minimally entangled states for each symmetry twisted sector on the torus. The state
\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
is given by a linear combination of states derived from basis elements of the tube algebra

\displaystyle\ket{p_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,s_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,q_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{h}}$}}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Torus}\end{array}.

The coefficient of each state is determined by those of
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
expanded in the tube basis. For a more detailed description see Eq.(
529
).
For this state to be nonzero we must have
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{a}=a
, as the periodic boundary conditions imply that the following tubes yield the same state

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,\underline{a}\,\sim\,\underline{a}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\delta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{a}\,a}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}.

(169)

Hence the dimension of the
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})
-twisted sector is equal to the number of
h
-invariant
g
-defects.

The
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
domain walls, satisfying
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{a}=a
, give rise to
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
and
T
matrices. These matrices are defined by their action on tube basis elements, which is described in Eq.(
IV.3
). They satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D_{a}}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\frac{S_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}{D_{b}}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}_{{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}},

(170)

\displaystyle S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]}\,,

\displaystyle\theta_{a}\delta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]}\,,

(171)

where we have assumed that the defect ICIs are one dimensional for the top left equality for
S
, which is true for the current example. We remark that the trace used above is the conventional matrix trace applied to elements of the tube algebra.

For the current example the
\mathcal{G}

We remark that these matrices are not gauge invariant quantities. However, they do contain gauge invariant data, in particular the
S
and
T
matrices of the underlying toric code, and the quantum dimensions of the defects.
Unitarity of the
S
-matrix implies that the number of
h
-invariant
g
-defects equals the number of
g
-invariant
h
-defects, in particular the number of
g
-defects equals the number of
g
-invariant anyons
Barkeshli et al. 2014
. It also allows one to use the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed Verlinde formula to calculate the fusion rules
Barkeshli et al. 2014
.

The ICIs of the gauged model can be recovered from the full set of domain walls, the projective representations they form, and their permutation action on the defects. First, a set of idempotents
\underline{{(a,\mu)}}
are constructed by forming projectors onto the projective irreps of the stabilizer group of each defect.
The ICIs
\underline{{[a,\mu]}}
are then found by taking a sum over projectors in the orbit of each defect

\displaystyle\underline{{(a,\mu)}}=\frac{d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{a}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\in{Z}_{a}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{h}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a}}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{{[a,\mu]}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}}\underline{{({}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a,{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\mu)}}

(195)

For example

\displaystyle\underline{\sigma_{+}}\ \mapsto\left.\begin{array}[]{c c c}\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}}+e^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{$\text{\bf{x}}$}}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}})&=&\underline{\sigma}\\
\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}}-e^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{$\text{\bf{x}}$}}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}})&=&\underline{\sigma{\overline{\psi}}}\end{array}\right.\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c }\underline{e}\\
\underline{m}\end{array}\mapsto\ \mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{e}}+\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{m}}\ =\ \underline{\sigma{\overline{\sigma}}}\,.

note the projective irreps
\chi^{\pm}_{{\sigma_{+}}}
of the representation
\{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}},\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}_{{\sigma_{+}}}\}
are given by
\chi^{\pm}_{{\sigma_{+}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})=1,\,\chi^{\pm}_{{\sigma_{+}}}({\text{\bf{x}}})=\pm e^{-\frac{\pi i}{8}}
.

The full set of gauged ICIs is once again given by Eq.(
II.1.2
), and the resulting topological order is the doubled Ising model.
In this case, knowing the topological order post-gauging is enough to diagnose that the SET must be the EM duality enriched toric code
\{0,e,m,em\}\oplus\{\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}\}
where
\sigma_{\pm}
have trivial FS indicator.

The
S
and
T
matrix of the gauged theory can be derived from their
\mathcal{G}
-crossed counter parts
Barkeshli et al. 2014
by using the the gauged ICIs, see Section
VII
,

\displaystyle S^{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Ising}})}_{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{b}\end{subarray}}{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}$h}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{b}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}})\,S_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle T^{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Ising}})}_{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}}\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\theta_{a},

(200)

where
S_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}

denotes an element of the SET
S
matrix, and
\theta_{a}
an element of the SET
T
matrix.
We have also used the notation

\displaystyle{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}\chi^{\mu}_{{a}}:=\chi^{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}\mu}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}a}},

(201)

to denote the action of an element
{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}
, which permutes
a
, on a character of
{Z}_{a}
. Note the permutation action induces a fixed choice of irrep
{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}\mu
on
{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}a
which specifies the aforementioned character.
By using Eq.(
200
), one can diagnose the gauged theory once the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
and
T
matrices have been calculated, without needing to carry out the gauging procedure explicitly. In the current example we recover the
S
and
T
matrices of the doubled Ising model.

We remark that one can go further, and use the defect ICIs and domain walls to construct a realization of the full emergent U
\mathcal{G}
xBFC, including the full gauge variant,
\{N,F,R,\rho,U,\eta\}
data. For the purposes of diagnosing the SET this does not appear to be particularly useful, but we anticipate that it may have applications to analyzing topological quantum computation
Walter Ogburn and Preskill 1999
;
Kitaev 2003
;
Freedman et al. 2003
;
Nayak et al. 2008
;
Wang 2010
with defects in a tensor network.

III
Symmetry-enriched string-net tensor networks

In this section we present the PEPS and MPO tensors for the class of symmetry-enriched string-net examples
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
. These include the conventional string-nets as a subcase
Levin and Wen 2005
. We first introduce the unitary fusion category data
Etingof et al. 2005
;
Etingof et al. 2015
that is the input for the string-net construction. We then present the MPOs that are used in both the string-nets and their symmetry-enriched counterparts. The local tensors for the (symetry-enriched) string nets are then given, along with the (symmetry-enriched) pulling through equation that they satisfy. The global symmetry of the symmetry-enriched string-net tensors is explicitly gauged, which is shown to recover the regular string-net tensor.

III.1
Input (graded) unitary fusion category data

We give a pedestrian description of the algebraic data
Moore and Seiberg 1988
;
Moore and Seiberg 1989
of a unitary fusion category (UFC)
\mathcal{C}
that is taken as input to the string-net construction.
The skeleton of this data is given by a fusion algebra of a finite set of simple objects
a\in\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle a\times b=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}N_{ab}^{c}c\,,

(202)

where
N_{ab}^{c}\in\mathbb{N}
are a set of fusion coefficients. We define
\delta_{ab}^{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
to be
0
when
N_{ab}^{c}=0
and
1
otherwise.

For a
\mathcal{G}
-graded UFC
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
the simple objects are organized into sectors
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
and the fusion respects the
\mathcal{G}
-grading

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}=\bigoplus_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}\,,

\displaystyle N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}=\delta_{{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{gh}}}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}\,,

(203)

where
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
is shorthand for
a\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
.

There is a distinguished unit element
0
, corresponding to the vacuum in a physical theory, that satisfies
N_{0a}^{b}=\delta_{a,b}=N_{a0}^{b}
.
Each simple object
a
has a unique conjugate simple object
{\overline{a}}
that satisfies
N_{ab}^{0}=\delta_{{\overline{a}},b}=N_{ba}^{0}
, and
{\overline{{\overline{a}}}}=a
. For a graded fusion category
0\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
and
{\overline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.

Each simple object
a
is assigned a quantum dimension
d_{a}
, given by the Perron-Frobenius eigenvalue of the matrix obtained by fixing
a
in
N_{ab}^{c}
. These quantum dimensions satisfy

\displaystyle d_{a}\,d_{b}=\sum_{c}N_{ab}^{c}\,d_{c}\,,

\displaystyle d_{0}=1

and

\displaystyle d_{a}=d_{{\overline{a}}}\,.

(204)

The total quantum dimension
\mathcal{D}
of a UFC
\mathcal{C}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{2}=\sum_{a}d_{a}^{2}\,.

(205)

For
\mathcal{G}
-graded UFCs the quantum dimension of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\sum_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}^{2}\,,

(206)

they satisfy
\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
for all
{\text{\bf{g}}}\in\mathcal{G}
, and hence
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}=|\mathcal{G}|\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}
.

The following sum is a projector known as the
\omega_{0}
-loop of
\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\omega_{0}:=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{d_{a}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}a\,.

(207)

For a
\mathcal{G}
-graded UFC the
\omega_{0}
-loop of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
plays the role of the unit element in a representation of
\mathcal{G}
given by

\displaystyle\pi_{\text{\bf{g}}}:=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}}\frac{d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\,{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,,

(208)

hence the
\omega_{0}
-loop of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
is the projector onto the symmetric subspace of this representation.

In a UFC the fusion coefficients are promoted to
N_{ab}^{c}
-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{C}
. These vector spaces
V_{ab}^{c}
(and their duals
V^{ab}_{c}
) appear on fusion (and splitting) vertices. Basis states for
V^{ab}_{c}
are denoted

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{stringnetsplittingvertex}\end{array}\,,

where
\mu\in\{1,\dots,N_{ab}^{c}\}
.

The fusion algebra is associative and the fusion coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in\mathcal{C}}N_{ab}^{e}N_{ec}^{d}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{C}}N_{bc}^{f}N_{af}^{d}\,.

(210)

However, the composition of fusion or splitting vertices is not strictly associative. Rather, two different orders of fusion or splitting are related by an associator matrix known as the
F
-symbol.

At this point, for clarity of exposition, we restrict our discussion to multiplicity free UFCs. That is, we assume
N_{ab}^{c}=\delta_{ab}^{c}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
It is straightforward, although cumbersome, to extend the whole construction to include fusion multiplicity, but we will not cover that here.

The
F
-symbol associators satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{StringFLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f}{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{StringFRHS}\end{array}\,.

The
F
-symbols are only defined upon the allowed splitting vertices, we use the convention that they are
0
outside of this subspace

\displaystyle{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}=\delta_{ab}^{e}\delta_{ec}^{d}\delta_{bc}^{f}\delta_{af}^{d}{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}\,.

(213)

The
F
-symbols are invertible matrices on the splitting spaces for which they are defined

\displaystyle\sum_{f}{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}{[(F^{abc}_{d})^{-1}]_{f}^{\widetilde{e}}}=\delta_{e\widetilde{e}}\,\delta_{ab}^{e}\,\delta_{ec}^{d}\,.

(214)

The quantum dimension and Frobenius-Shur indicator can be extracted from the
F
-symbol

\displaystyle d_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|{F^{a{\overline{a}}a}_{a00}}|}\,,

\displaystyle\varkappa_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{{F^{a{\overline{a}}a}_{a00}}}{|{F^{a{\overline{a}}a}_{a00}}|}\,.

(215)

Since the associativity constraint of an algebra was relaxed to a nontrivial associator, we find a higher order constraint on the composition of associators known as the pentagon equation. The pentagon equation can be derived by equating two inequivalent paths of associators that relate a pair of splitting diagrams on four objects.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Pentagon}\end{array}

Which can be written in terms of matrix elements as

\displaystyle{F^{pcd}_{eqr}}{F^{abr}_{eps}}=\sum_{x}{F^{abc}_{qpx}}{F^{axd}_{eqs}}{F^{bcd}_{sxr}}\,,

where

\displaystyle{F^{abc}_{def}}:={[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e}^{f}}\,.

(217)

A result known as Maclane’s coherence theorem
Mac Lane 2013
implies that the pentagon equation alone is enough to ensure that any path of associators between a pair of splitting diagrams are consistent.
Another fundamental result known as Ocneanu rigidity
Etingof et al. 2005
;
Kitaev 2006
, implies that a continuous deformation of a solution to the pentagon equation can be absorbed into a gauge transformation of the associator.

The fusion categories that lead to Hermitian string-net Hamiltonians have unitary
F
-symbols on the relevant splitting subspace

\displaystyle{[(F^{abc}_{d})^{-1}]_{f}^{e}}=[(F^{abc}_{d})^{\dagger}]_{f}^{e}=\left({F^{abc}_{def}}\right)^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\sum_{f}{F^{abc}_{def}}\left({F^{abc}_{d\widetilde{e}f}}\right)^{*}=\delta_{e\widetilde{e}}\,\delta_{ab}^{e}\,\delta_{ec}^{d}\,.

(218)

III.2
Matrix product operator tensors and their fusion

The string-net examples considered here are defined on oriented lattices, which give rise to both left and right handed MPO tensors. These tensors are related by a combined reflection and complex conjugation. A factor of the quantum dimension must be included for each variable that is summed over to achieve a correct overall normalization. The variables being summed over appear as closed loops in the tensor network diagrams and we use the convention established in Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
, that such a sum over a variable
s
is weighted by the quantum dimensions
d_{s}
. We remark that the weighting by quantum dimension can also be incorporated into the local tensors directly, by making use of the planar geometry, which was done in Refs.
Buerschaper et al. 2009
;
Gu et al. 2009
;
Bultinck et al. 2017a
. We do not take this approach here.

The MPOs described in this section are for both the string-nets and their symmetry-enriched variants. The only difference in the symmetry-enriched case is the inclusion of a
\mathcal{G}
-grading on the objects of the input fusion category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

The left and right handed MPO tensors are given by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNRHMPO}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{{F^{a\mu i}_{\beta\alpha\nu}}}{\sqrt{d_{\alpha}d_{\nu}}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNLHMPO}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{{F^{a\alpha i}_{\nu\mu\beta}}^{*}}{\sqrt{d_{\mu}d_{\beta}}}\,,

where the indices take values in the set of simple objects of the input category, and
d
and
F
are also taken from the input category. Each index of the tensor is
|\mathcal{C}|^{3}
dimensional, and red is used to indicate the virtual indices. The MPO tensors have some redundancy, as they are only nonzero on the subspace of the bond indices that satisfy
\delta_{\beta^{\prime}a^{\prime}}^{\nu}
and
\delta_{\alpha a}^{\mu^{\prime}}
, they are injective once projected onto this subspace. Here
a
corresponds to the block label of the MPO.

These MPO tensors satisfy an equation that corresponds to dragging an MPO string off a tensor network bond, this leads to a bubble popping equation that replaces a loop of MPO
a
with the quantum dimension
d_{a}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNUnitarityLHS}\end{array}=\frac{1}{d_{i}}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{ik}^{j}\delta_{al}^{i^{\prime}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNUnitarityRHS}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNBubbleLHS}\end{array}=d_{a}\delta_{ik}^{j}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNBubbleRHS}\end{array}\,.

The factor of
\frac{1}{d_{i}}
in the left equation corrects for an excess quantum dimension that arises when two separate loops are merged. This can be seen by imagining the equation as part of a larger, closed, tensor network diagram in which the
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}
condition becomes redundant.
Reflecting and conjugating the above equations, leads to analogous relations with reversed orientations.

The matrices constructed by contracting a periodic chain of
L
local tensors, with block label
a
and virtual index pointing let to right, are denoted
\text{MPO}^{L}_{a}
. These matrices form an MPO representation of the input fusion algebra

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{L}_{a}\,\text{MPO}^{L}_{b}=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}}N_{ab}^{c}\,\text{MPO}^{L}_{c}\,.

(229)

In the symmetry-enriched case this algebra is
\mathcal{G}
-graded.

For open boundary conditions the MPOs only obey the algebra multiplication up to boundary fusion and splitting tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNMPOsplitting}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\frac{d_{a}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{b}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{ab\gamma}_{\alpha c\beta}}}{d_{c}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNMPOfusion}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\frac{d_{a}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{b}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{ab\gamma}_{\alpha c\beta}}^{*}}{d_{c}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,.

These tensors are again related by a combined reflection and complex conjugation.
We have chosen a particular normalization of the fusion and splitting tensors such that the following equations hold

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{smallMPOmultLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{c}\delta_{ab}^{c}\sqrt{\frac{d_{c}}{d_{a}d_{b}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{smallMPOmultRHS}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNFusionIsometryLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{c,c^{\prime}}\delta_{ab}^{c}\delta_{c\beta}^{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNFusionIsometryRHS}\end{array}\,,

where the right equation reduces to bubble popping for
b={\overline{a}}
and
c=0
.

While the multiplication of closed MPOs is clearly associative, as they are matrices, for open boundary conditions the multiplication is not strictly associative. Rather, two different orders of multiplication are related by an associator, that is given by the
F
-symbol of the input category

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{MPOFsymbolLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f}{F^{abc}_{def}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{MPOFsymbolRHS}\end{array}\,.

An analogous equation for the fusion tensors is obtained by a combined reflection and complex-conjugation.
The resulting
F
symbols satisfy the pentagon equation, as the input was a consistent UFC.
Since we are working with the tensor product of tensors over
\mathbb{C}
there is no difference between taking a left or right trace, which is analogous to the
spherical
property of UFCs.

In our examples we assume that the
F
symbols have been brought into a gauge where

\displaystyle{F^{0bc}_{def}}={F^{a0c}_{def}}={F^{ab0}_{def}}=1\,.

(238)

We remark that, in general, these elements may be arbitrary complex phases.
This constraint is equivalent to the following
triangle equations
, which allow a vacuum line to be added or removed at fusion and splitting vertices

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{leftcap}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{rightcap}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{leftcup}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{rightcup}\end{array}=\delta_{ab}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{a\beta}^{\alpha}\raisebox{6.40204pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{aidentity}\end{array}$}\,.

In the above equations we have used the following states to terminate a vacuum line

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{onecup}\end{array}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{a0}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{onecap}\end{array}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{a0}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}\,.

Terminating a vacuum line that splits into (results from the fusion of) a particle-antiparticle pair leads to an orientation reversing “flag” matrix, corresponding to a cup (cap)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNcupRHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle:=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNcupLHS}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNcapRHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle:=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNcapLHS}\end{array}\,.

The absolute orientation chosen for the flag matrices is not important, as they already define their oppositely oriented variants via transposition and swapping
a\leftrightarrow{\overline{a}}
.

The closed MPOs satisfy
\text{MPO}^{\dagger}_{a}=\text{MPO}_{{\overline{a}}}=\text{MPO}^{-}_{a}
, where
-
indicates orientation reversal.
The orientation reversing flag matrices implement this at the level of the local tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{MPOreversalLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{MPOreversalRHS}\end{array}\,,
