\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ReversalIdentity}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{a\beta}^{\alpha}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ReversalIdentityRHS}\end{array}\,.

From the above we see that the relative orientation of the flags does matter. Changing this orientation leads to the FS indicator
\varkappa_{a}\in\mathsf{U}(1)
, which is a piece of gauge invariant physical data — given by
\varkappa_{a}=\pm 1
— for
a={\overline{a}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{FSIdentity}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\varkappa_{a}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{a\alpha}^{\beta}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ReversalIdentityRHS}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{FSIdentityLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\varkappa_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{FSIdentityRHS}\end{array}\,.

The orientation reversing flag matrices can be used to derive a relation between the fusion and splitting spaces

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNlbendLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=A^{ab}_{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNlbendRHS}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNrbendLHS}\end{array}

\displaystyle=B^{ab}_{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNrbendRHS}\end{array}\,,

with the normalization we have chosen these maps are unitary. They can be used to show a version of the
pivotal
identity satisfied by UFCs.
For the case of no fusion multiplicity these maps are simply phases

\displaystyle A^{ab}_{c}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}{F^{{\overline{a}}ab}_{b0c}}^{*}\,,

\displaystyle B^{ab}_{c}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}{F^{ab{\overline{b}}}_{ac0}}\,.

(262)

Furthermore, the flag matrices allow one to derive a version of Eqs.(
III.2
), and (
III.2
) with the orientation of only the red lines reversed.

III.3
String-net tensors and the pulling through equation

The string-net models are defined on a trivalent lattice that is dual to a triangulation endowed with branching structure. A PEPS representation of the ground state is specified by the local tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNsplitting}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{d_{i}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{j}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}}{d_{k}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNfusion}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{d_{i}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{j}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}^{*}}{d_{k}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,,

which are related by a combined reflection and complex conjugation.
Each of the indices is
|\mathcal{C}|^{3}
dimensional, the physical indices are depicted within the tensors. In our representation of the string-nets each edge degree of freedom appears twice on the adjacent triangles. This convention, although redundant, is used for convenience and is equivalent to other conventions where the redundancy is removed.

The string-net PEPS was found to be MPO-injective in Ref.
Şahinoğlu et al. 2014
with respect to the MPO defined by the same UFC
\mathcal{C}
. The pulling through equations for the string-nets are given by

\displaystyle\raisebox{-25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNPullthruLHS}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNPullthruRHS}\end{array}$}\,,

\displaystyle\raisebox{-17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNPullthruRHS2}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SNPullthruLHS2}\end{array}$}\,.

We emphasize the inclusion of a factor of the quantum dimension
d_{a}
on the index that is summed over, which appears as a closed loop. These equations are implied by the pentagon equation for the
F
-symbol used to define both the PEPS and the MPO tensors. The two equations are related by a combined reflection and complex conjugation. All other pulling through equations, involving different combinations of indices and orientations, can be derived from the above by using Eqs.(
III.2
), and (
III.2
).

III.4
Symmetry-enriched string-net tensors, pulling through, and gauging

For the
\mathcal{G}
symmetry-enriched string-nets the simple objects of the input UFC are equipped with a
\mathcal{G}
-grading. We use the shorthand notation
\lfloor{a}\rfloor={\text{\bf{g}}}
to denote the sector
a\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
, i.e.
\lfloor{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\rfloor={\text{\bf{g}}}
.
The PEPS tensors only differ from the ungraded string-nets via the inclusion of the sector information of the virtual indices in the physical index

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingsplitting}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}}\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\lfloor{\beta}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\lfloor{\gamma}\rfloor}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{d_{i}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{j}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}}{d_{k}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingfusion}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}}\lfloor{\alpha}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\lfloor{\beta}\rfloor}\delta_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\lfloor{\gamma}\rfloor}\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\\
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\gamma\gamma^{\prime}}\end{array}\frac{d_{i}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{j}^{\frac{1}{4}}{F^{\alpha ij}_{\gamma\beta k}}^{*}}{d_{k}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\beta}}}\,.

The internal tensor that copies the sector information is depicted as a circle. Again the tensors are related by combined reflection and complex conjugation. Similar tensors were described in Refs.
Barkeshli et al. 2016
;
Cui and Wang 2017
;
Luo et al. 2017
.

These tensors are MPO-injective with respect to the MPO constructed from
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
. They further satisfy the symmetry-enriched pulling through equations defined by the MPOs constructed from
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\raisebox{-25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruLHS}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruRHS}\end{array}$},

\displaystyle\raisebox{-17.07182pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruRHS2}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{25.6073pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETIsingPullthruLHS2}\end{array}$}\,,

where the shaded circle containing
g
denotes the application of the on-site left regular group action

\displaystyle\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor0}\end{array}$}:=\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor1}\end{array}$}=\raisebox{-4.26773pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCgtensor2}\end{array}$}\,.

Again a factor of the quantum dimension is included on the summed variable, which appears as a closed loop. These equations are implied by the pentagon equation, and the
\mathcal{G}
-grading of the fusion algebra for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
. The two pulling through equations are related by a combined reflection and Hermitian conjugation. All other instances of the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation can be derived from the above by using Eqs.(
III.2
), and (
III.2
).
The symmetry-enriched pulling through equation
Williamson et al. 2016b
, together with the properties of the MPOs, imply that the PEPS is symmetric under the on-site left regular representation of
\mathcal{G}
.

III.4.1
Gauging the
\mathcal{G}
symmetry

The state gauging procedure defined in Ref.
Haegeman et al. 2015a
can be applied to the PEPS tensors of a fixed-point model, such as the symmetry-enriched string-nets, to produce PEPS tensors for the gauged model. This was explained in detail in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
, which focused on SPT tensor networks, but the results generalize straightforwardly.
For a system of
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{G}]
degrees of freedom on the vertices of a directed graph, in state
\ket{\psi}_{V}
, and a global symmetry given by a tensor product of the left regular representation on every vertex, the gauging map is given by

\displaystyle G\ket{\psi}_{V}:=\prod_{v}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}_{v}}L_{v}({\text{\bf{g}}}_{v})\bigotimes_{e_{v}^{+}}L_{e_{v}^{+}}({\text{\bf{g}}}_{v})\bigotimes_{e_{v}^{-}}R_{e_{v}^{-}}({\text{\bf{g}}}_{v})\right)\ \ket{\psi}_{V}\bigotimes_{e}\ket{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{e}\,,

(286)

where
e_{v}^{\pm}
is a neighboring edge that points away from (towards) vertex
v
. The resulting states
G\ket{\psi}_{V}
are only defined on the subspace that satisfies the local gauge constraints, which does not have an immediately obvious tensor product structure.

For the on-site left regular representation the following isometry disentangles the gauge constraints, and maps back to a tensor product Hilbert space

\displaystyle Y:=\left(\bigotimes_{v}\bra{{\text{\bf{+}}}}_{v}\right)\prod_{v}\,\prod_{e_{v}^{+}}{CL}^{-1}_{{v,e_{v}^{+}}}\prod_{e_{v}^{-}}{CR}^{-1}_{{v,e_{v}^{-}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\ket{{\text{\bf{+}}}}:=\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\ket{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,,

(287)

and
CL\,(R)
is the controlled left (right) multiplication. Hence
YG\ket{\psi}_{V}
is a state on the edge degrees of freedom alone.

The composition of the global gauging projector followed by the disentangling isometry,
CG
, can be implemented directly on the local tensors

\displaystyle\bra{{\text{\bf{+}}}}_{0}\bra{{\text{\bf{+}}}}_{1}\bra{{\text{\bf{+}}}}_{2}{CL}^{-1}_{{0,01}}{CL}^{-1}_{{0,02}}{CL}^{-1}_{{1,12}}{CR}^{-1}_{{1,01}}{CR}^{-1}_{{2,02}}{CR}^{-1}_{{2,12}}\ket{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{01}\ket{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{12}\ket{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{23}\raisebox{5.69046pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETSNsplittinggaugedLHS}\end{array}$}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETSNsplittinggauged}\end{array}\,,

where
0,\,1,\,2
labels the closest
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{G}]
degree of of freedom, and
01,\,12,\,02
labels the newly introduced
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{G}]
degree of of freedom on the relevant edge.

Every edge degree of freedom of the tensor network now carries a redundant copy of the sector information i.e.
\ket{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\ket{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
.
A further local isometry
W
can be applied to each edge to disentangle and project out this redundancy

\displaystyle W_{01}W_{12}W_{02}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETSNsplittinggauged}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETSNsplittinggaugedDisentangled}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle W\ket{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\ket{{\text{\bf{h}}}}=(\openone\otimes\bra{{\text{\bf{1}}}})\ket{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\ket{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,.

We find that this results in the string-net tensor for the full UFC
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, given in Eq.(
III.3
), left. A similar equation holds for the positively oriented tensor given in Eq.(
III.3
), right.

In summary, for fixed point models

•

The effect of gauging can be captured by simply projecting each local tensor onto the trivial representation of the on-site symmetry, in this case
\ket{{\text{\bf{+}}}}
. This explains some of the results observed in Ref.
Fernández-González et al. 2016
.

•

The inverse operation to gauging
\mathcal{G}
corresponds to a
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
anyon condensation. This can simply be implemented on the string-net tensors by copying the sectors of the virtual degrees of freedom to the physical index. This effectively ungauges the flat
\mathcal{G}
-connection defined by the
\mathcal{G}
-grading of the virtual indices.

•

Similarly, confining the
\mathcal{G}
-defects of the SET can be implemented directly on the local tensors by projecting the sector information onto the
\ket{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
state.

•

The inverse operation corresponds to allowing
\mathcal{G}
-defects and can be implemented on the local tensors by allowing the sector degree of freedom to fluctuate freely.

These relations are summarized as follows

\textstyle{\text{SNTN}(\mathcal{C})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Fluctuate sector d.o.f.
\textstyle{\ \text{SESNTN}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Project sector d.o.f. onto
+
Project sector d.o.f. onto
1
\textstyle{\ \text{SNTN}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Copy
\lfloor{a}\rfloor
to phys. index

where SNTN
(\mathcal{C})
refers to the string-net tensor network constructed from the UFC
\mathcal{C}
, and SESNTN
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}})
refers to the symmetry-enriched string-net tensor network.

IV
Ocneanu’s tube algebra and emergent topological order from matrix product operators

In this section, we describe a construction known as Ocneanu’s
tube algebra
Evans and Kawahigashi 1995
;
Evans and Kawahigashi 1998
;
Muger 2003
;
Ocneanu 1994
. The derivation of the tube algebra from MPOs was first explained in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
, similar lattice constructions of the tube algebra have appeared in Refs.
Lan and Wen 2014
;
Haah 2016
;
Aasen et al. 2017
;
Aasen et al.
.
By block diagonalizing this tube algebra we find tensor network representations of the emergent superselection sectors. We are able to extract gauge invariant observable physical data by using these representations to construct minimally entangled states, this includes the modular
S
and
T
matrices as well as the topological entanglement entropies of the superselection sectors. Furthermore we outline how the full emergent anyon theory, which is known to be the Drinfeld center (or double)
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
of the MPO UFC
\mathcal{C}
, can be constructed by finding the gauge variant fusion and braiding,
F
and
R
symbols.
The tube algebra construction can be performed for the string-net examples tensors of the previous section.

IV.1
Definition of the tube algebra

Throughout this section we work on the level of abstraction explained in Eq.(
II.1.1
), where the specifics of the underlying lattice are neglected. We use a diagrammatic notation in which grey shaded areas denote the PEPS tensor network, and black lines denote MPOs contracted with the virtual indices of the PEPS.

The tube algebra is found by considering a puncture in the tensor network and calculating the action of the topological MPO symmetry on the virtual indices of the puncture.
To this end we consider a cylinder (or twice punctured sphere) as there is a global constraint that forces the charge in a unique puncture on a sphere to be trivial.
As explained in Eq.(
II.1.1
) the MPO-injective tensor network states on the cylinder are found by closing the tensor network with an MPO. Hence it suffices to consider the action of the topological MPO symmetry algebra on the states

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder0}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{s}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder1}\end{array}=\frac{1}{d_{s}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder2}\end{array}=\sum_{q}\sqrt{\frac{d_{q}}{d_{r}d_{s}^{3}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder3}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{pq}\frac{1}{d_{s}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{p}}{d_{r}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder4}\end{array}=\sum_{pq}B^{r{\overline{s}}}_{q}(B^{r{\overline{s}}}_{q})^{\dagger}\frac{1}{d_{s}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{p}}{d_{r}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Cylinder5}\end{array}\,,

where we have used Eqs.(
III.2
),
(
III.2
),
(
III.2
),
and
(
III.2
), or rather, the analogous equations for a general MPO algebra. The dashed lines indicate periodic boundary conditions and the white triangles denote cup and cap matrices, see Eq.(
III.2
).

Hence the basis elements that span the tube algebra are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tube}\end{array}\,,

corresponding to the minimal tensor network

\displaystyle d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{tensornetworktube}\end{array}\,,

we will often refer to these basis elements simply as tubes. There is some gauge freedom in the definition of these tubes which is similar to a 1-cochain.
Eq.(
IV.1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{0}_{rrr}=\frac{1}{d_{s}^{2}}\sum_{pq}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{ppp}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

and hence

\displaystyle\openone:=\sum_{r}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{rrr}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

(302)

where
\openone
denotes the identity matrix on a relevant subspace corresponding to virtual indices of a cylinder PEPS.

Multiplication of tubes is defined by the stacking operation

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}r^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{rp^{\prime}}d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r^{\prime}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tubemult}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\delta_{rp^{\prime}}\sum_{q^{\prime\prime}s^{\prime\prime}}\varkappa_{s}\sqrt{\frac{d_{s}d_{s^{\prime}}}{d_{s^{\prime\prime}}}}B^{q^{\prime}{\overline{s}}}_{q^{\prime\prime}}(B^{qs}_{r})^{\dagger}{F^{s^{\prime}q^{\prime}{\overline{s}}}_{qrq^{\prime\prime}}}{({F^{q^{\prime\prime}ss^{\prime}}_{r^{\prime}s^{\prime\prime}q^{\prime}}})}^{*}{({F^{ss^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}}_{pqs^{\prime\prime}}})}^{*}\ \mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{r^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}p}\,,

(304)

only tubes,
\mathcal{T}^{s^{\prime\prime}}_{pq^{\prime\prime}r^{\prime}}
, satisfying
\delta_{ss^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime\prime}}\neq 0
have nonzero coefficients in this expansion.
The tube algebra is a
C^{*}
algebra as Hermitian conjugation is given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tubedagger}\end{array}=\sqrt{\frac{d_{p}}{d_{r}}}\sum_{q^{\prime}}B^{ps}_{q^{\prime}}(A^{sq}_{p})^{\dagger}{F^{{\overline{s}}ps}_{rqq^{\prime}}}\ \mathcal{T}^{{\overline{s}}}_{pq^{\prime}r}\,.

Each tube defines a state on the torus, which is identically zero when
p\neq r

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}}:=\delta_{pr}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Torus}\end{array}=:\delta_{pr}\ket{psq}.

As explained in Eq.(
II.1.1
), the above states span the ground space on the torus.

These states are not always independent, due to the relation induced by growing an
\omega_{0}
-loop over the whole torus and fusing it into the MPOs along the 1-skeleton

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{T}^{s}_{rqr}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}\left|\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{rqr}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}\right\rangle.

(307)

IV.2
Block diagonalizing the tube algebra with irreducible central idempotents

We have demonstrated that the tube algebra is a
C^{*}
algebra and hence can be block diagonalized. Since the tubes in Eq.(
IV.1
) span the algebra we have

\displaystyle D=\sum_{pqrs}\delta_{sq}^{p}\delta_{qs}^{r}=\sum_{\underline{a}}D_{{a}}\times D_{{a}}\,,

(308)

where the sum is over all blocks
\underline{a}
of the tube algebra,
D
is the dimension of the tube algebra, and
D_{{a}}
is the dimension of the
\underline{a}
block (which consists of
D_{a}\times D_{a}
-matrices).

The irreducible central idempotents (ICI) of the tube matrix algebra correspond to Hermitian projectors onto each irreducible block.
These ICIs determine the set of inequivalent topological sectors for a disc in the tensor network that may be used to fill in a puncture.
A constructive algorithm to find these ICIs was explained in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
. Alternatively, several exact formulas exist when the input category has additional structure such as a modular braiding
Koenig et al. 2010
;
Aasen
.

The irreducible central idempotents can be expressed as linear combinations of the tube basis elements

\displaystyle\underline{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}t^{pqrs}_{a}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

(309)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIa}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}t^{pqrs}_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tube}\end{array}\,,

note the coefficients satisfy
t^{pqrs}_{a}=\delta_{pr}t^{pqps}_{a}
as only basis elements
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
with
r=p
can occur in the construction of central idempotents. The ICIs are defined by the conditions,
\underline{a},\underline{b}\neq 0

\displaystyle\underline{a}\,\underline{b}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ab}\,\underline{a}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\underline{a}

\displaystyle=\underline{a}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

\displaystyle\underline{a}

\displaystyle\neq\underline{b}+\underline{c}\,,

(312)

for
\underline{a},\underline{b}
ICIs and
\underline{c}
a nonzero central idempotent.
These idempotents realize the superselections sectors of the emergent anyon theory, or modular tensor category (MTC), which is the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
of the input UFC
\mathcal{C}
Muger 2003
. The crossing tensors
\underline{a}
, containing the weights
t^{pqrs}_{a}
, can be directly interpreted as the the simple objects of this Drinfeld center MTC
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
. Furthermore, these crossing tensors yield a construction of the string operators in the Levin-Wen fixed-point models by using them to resolve an external string into the lattice
Levin and Wen 2005
;
Aasen
.

There is always a unique ICI corresponding to the vacuum

\displaystyle\underline{0}=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{C}}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{0s0}\,,

(313)

which is easily verified to be an ICI.

Each irreducible central idempotent that projects onto a block,
a
, of the matrix algebra with dimension
D_{a}
can be written as a sum of
D_{a}
non-central irreducible idempotents that partition the identity within the block

\displaystyle\underline{a}=\sum_{i=1}^{D_{a}}(\underline{a})_{ii}\,.

(314)

These non-central irreducible idempotents satisfy,
(\underline{a})_{ii}\neq 0

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{ii}(\underline{b})_{jj}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ab}\delta_{ij}(\underline{a})_{ii}\,,

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{ii}

\displaystyle\neq(\underline{b})_{jj}+\underline{c}\,,

(315)

for
(\underline{b})_{jj},\underline{c}\neq 0
idempotents and
(\underline{b})_{jj}
irreducible.
There are also
D_{a}(D_{a}-1)
off-diagonal, nilpotent elements
(\underline{a})_{ij}\neq 0
in each degenerate block that satisfy

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{ij}(\underline{a})_{kl}

\displaystyle=\delta_{jk}(\underline{a})_{il}\,,

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{ij}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\underline{a})_{ji}\,.

(316)

The irreducible idempotents are mixed by the conjugation action of the tube algebra
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}(\underline{a})_{ii}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}
.
In particular

\displaystyle(\underline{a})=\sum_{j}(\underline{a})_{ji}(\underline{a})_{ii}(\underline{a})_{ji}^{\dagger}\,.

(317)

We remark that the irreducible idempotents
(\underline{a})_{ii}
, together with the off-diagonal elements
(\underline{a})_{ij}
within each block, define a basis for the tube algebra. Hence we can define the change of basis matrix

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{ij}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}t^{pqrs}_{a_{ij}}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\,,

(318)

which includes expressions for the off-diagonal elements as well as for the irreducible idempotents.
Similarly we have the inverse change of basis matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=\sum_{a_{ij}}{\overline{t}}^{a_{ij}}_{pqrs}(\underline{a})_{ij}\,,

which implies

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}=\sum_{a_{ij}}({\overline{t}}^{a_{ij}}_{pqrs})^{*}(\underline{a})_{ji}\,.

(319)

These coefficients satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}{t}_{a_{ij}}^{pqrs}{\overline{t}}^{b_{kl}}_{pqrs}=\delta_{ab}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{a_{ij}}{\overline{t}}^{a_{ij}}_{pqrs}{t}_{a_{ij}}^{xyzw}=\delta_{px}\delta_{qy}\delta_{rz}\delta_{sw}\,.

(320)

The columns of the inverse change of basis matrix are orthogonal

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}{\overline{t}}^{a_{ij}}_{pqrs}({\overline{t}}^{b_{kl}}_{pqrs})^{*}=\delta_{ab}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\frac{1}{|{a_{ij}}|^{2}}\,,

(321)

for some positive weights
|{a_{ij}}|
.

It follows from Eqs.(
IV.1
) and (
321
) that

\displaystyle\openone=\sum_{r}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{rrr}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=\sum_{a_{ij}}\frac{1}{|{a_{ij}}|^{2}}(\underline{a})_{ij}^{\dagger}(\underline{a})_{ij}\,.

(322)

Multiplying by
(\underline{a})_{ii}
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{1}{|{a_{ij}}|^{2}}=1\,,

and hence

\displaystyle\openone=\sum_{p}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{ppp}=\sum_{a}\underline{a}\,,

(323)

which is a partition of the identity, on the virtual indices of the cylinder tensor network, by ICIs.

IV.3
Minimally entangled states and modular matrices

To diagnose the emergent topological order of the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
described by the ICIs, we want to calculate gauge invariant observable quantities. In this section we describe how to extract the modular
S
and
T
matrices by using the ICIs to define minimally entangled states (MES)
Zhang et al. 2012
. These modular matrices are useful invariants for the identification of an anyon theory, however they are not complete invariants even for nonchiral phases
Mignard and Schauenburg 2017
.

Each irreducible idempotent defines a state on the torus

\displaystyle\ket{a_{ij}}:=\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}t^{pqps}_{a_{ii}}\ket{psq}\,.

(324)

Due to the relation

(\underline{a})_{ii}=(\underline{a})_{ij}(\underline{a})_{ji}\,\sim\,(\underline{a})_{ji}(\underline{a})_{ij}=(\underline{a})_{jj}
,
all irreducible idempotents
(\underline{a})_{ii}
within the same block give rise to the same state, i.e.
\ket{a_{ii}}=\ket{a_{jj}}
.

In particular we take the following set of representatives as our definition

\displaystyle\ket{a}:=\frac{1}{D_{a}}\sum_{i}\ket{a_{ii}}\,,

(325)

which yields a basis of minimally entangled states with a definite anyonic flux
a
threading the
y
-cycle of the torus.
With this choice of normalization we have
\ket{a}=\ket{a_{ii}}
.

Since the ground space on the torus is spanned by states of the form
\ket{psq}
, see Eq.(
II.1.1
), we recover the well known fact that the ground space dimension of the torus equals
|\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})|
the number of emergent anyons or ICIs.

The modular
S
and
T
matrices are defined on the tubes as follows

\displaystyle S(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})

\displaystyle:=\delta_{pr}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{STube1}\end{array}

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}^{p}_{p0p}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime}}\varkappa_{s}B^{q^{\prime}{\overline{s}}}_{p}B^{sp}_{q^{\prime}}(B^{qs}_{p})^{\dagger}(A^{ps}_{q^{\prime}})^{\dagger}{({F^{sp{\overline{s}}}_{pq^{\prime}q}})}^{*}\ \mathcal{T}^{{\overline{p}}}_{sq^{\prime}s}\,.

(327)

For the normalized MES and ICIs they satisfy

\displaystyle\ket{S(a)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b}S_{ab}\,\ket{b}\,,

\displaystyle T(\underline{a})

\displaystyle=\theta_{a}\,\underline{a}\,.

(328)

The quantity
\theta_{a}\in\mathsf{U}(1)
is known as the topological spin of sector
\underline{a}
. The equation defining it follows from Schur’s lemma and the observations

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\mathcal{T}^{a}_{a0a}\,\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}\sum_{a}\mathcal{T}^{a}_{a0a}\,,

and

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{a}\mathcal{T}^{a}_{a0a}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\sum_{a}\mathcal{T}^{a}_{a0a}\right)=\sum_{a}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{aaa}=\openone\,,

(329)

which imply that
T
acts as the identity times a phase on each irreducible block of the matrix algebra.
The
S
and
T
matrices just defined match those derived from state overlaps of MES for fixed point models such as the string-nets.

As a technical aside, note that
S
was defined on MES — rather than directly on ICIs — as the relation
\ket{a_{ii}}=\ket{a}
is necessary to find the correct
S
matrix elements.
An equivalent definition would be to look at the action of
S
on ICIs up to equivalence by conjugation
(\underline{a})_{ii}\sim(\underline{a})_{ji}(\underline{a})_{ii}(\underline{a})_{ij}^{\dagger}=(\underline{a})_{jj}
.
Another useful equivalent definition of the matrix elements for
S
and
T
is given by

\displaystyle S_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[\underline{b}^{\dagger}S(\underline{a})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\underline{b}^{\dagger}\underline{b}]}\,,

\displaystyle\theta_{a}\delta_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[\underline{b}^{\dagger}T(\underline{a})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\underline{b}^{\dagger}\underline{b}]}\,,

(330)

where the trace now ensures that we have the necessary relation

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[(\underline{a})_{ii}]=\text{Tr}[(\underline{a})_{jj}]=\frac{1}{D_{a}}\text{Tr}[\,\underline{a}\,]\,.

(331)

We remark that the formulas in Eq.(
330
) where written with some unnecessary redundancies (such as the hermitian conjugation). We chose to present them in this way as it makes their generalization to the symmetry-enriched case more clear. The trace used in the above equation is the conventional matrix trace applied to elements of the tube algebra.

The
S
and
T
matrices constructed above are known to be unitary
Müger 2003
;
Muger 2003
and hence the emergent theory
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
is modular. Furthermore, since all such theories can be realized by local commuting projector Hamiltonians — via the string-net construction — they have zero chiral central charge and do not support robust gapless edge modes. Consequently
S
and
T
satisfy the modular relations

\displaystyle(ST)^{3}=S^{2}\,,

\displaystyle S^{4}=\openone\,.

(332)

One can also verify that our definition of
S
and
T
must satisfy the above modular relations via direct manipulation of MPOs.
Without using the previously known results it is clear that our definition of
T
must be unitary. It should also be possible to directly show that our definition of
S
is unitary. However we will not labor to reproduce this known result here.

One can find an explicit formula for
S
in terms of matrix elements
t_{a_{ij}}^{pqrs},{\overline{t}}^{a_{ij}}_{pqrs}
of the tube basis change matrix, and
{F^{abc}_{def}}
from the UFC data.
This leads to a convenient formula for the quantum dimensions

\displaystyle d_{a}=\sum_{p}\frac{d_{p}{t}_{a}^{ppp0}}{D_{a}}\,,

(333)

which follows from

\displaystyle\frac{d_{a}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}=S_{a0}=\frac{\text{Tr}[\underline{0}S(\underline{a})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\,\underline{0}\,]}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}{t}_{a}^{pqps}\frac{\text{Tr}[\underline{0}S(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqp})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\,\underline{0}\,]}=\sum_{p}\frac{d_{p}{t}_{a}^{ppp0}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}D_{a}}\,.

(334)

Where we have used the relation
S(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqp})\,\underline{0}=d_{p}\delta_{s0}\delta_{pq}\,\underline{0}
, which is implied by Eq.(
IV.5
).

IV.4
Topological entanglement entropy of the superselection sectors

Our tensor network representation of the ICIs
\underline{a}
, that project onto each superselection sector, can be used to calculate the topological correction to the entanglement entropy for these sectors
Kitaev and Preskill 2006
;
Levin and Wen 2006
;
Qi et al. 2012
.
We first calculate the scaling of the rank of each sector, given by the trace of the relevant ICI, to find the topological correction. We then argue that this correction should be independent of the Rényi index for an RG fixed point model
Flammia et al. 2009
and, furthermore, that the value of topological entanglement entropy (TEE) should be robust throughout a gapped phase
Kitaev and Preskill 2006
.

Using Eq.(
333
), we find that the topological correction to the
0
-Rényi entropy
H_{0}
(i.e. the entanglement rank) for the
\underline{a}
sector is given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}=\log(\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}})-\log(d_{a})\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}^{2}\,,

(335)

\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}
is the total quantum dimension of the emergent theory.
Note, this differs from the TEE of the MES, as reported in Ref.
Zhang et al. 2012
, since we are considering a disk topology rather than a torus.

For gapped, fixed point models — such as the string-nets — the 0-Rényi entropy is equal to the entanglement entropy as the Schmidt spectrum is flat. Hence, for fixed point models, the topological correction
\gamma_{a}
we have calculated corresponds to the topological entanglement entropy (TEE) of the
{a}
superselection sector
Kitaev and Preskill 2006
;
Levin and Wen 2006
. Furthermore, Ref.
Flammia et al. 2009
argued that the result for the TEE calculated at a fixed point, via the 0-Rényi entropy, should hold throughout the gapped phase containing that fixed point model.

The
0
-Rényi entropy
H_{0}
of the
a
superselection sector can be calculated by taking the trace of the ICI
\underline{a}
. To extract the topological correction we need to access the regime of asymptotic scaling in the size of the perimeter
L
. Hence we consider an ICI formed by an MPO of length
L
contracted with the crossing tensor containing the weights
t_{a}^{pqps}

\displaystyle H_{0}=\log\Big(\frac{1}{D_{a}\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}t_{a}^{pqps}\ \text{Tr}\ {\Big[}\raisebox{-11.38092pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TEEMPO}\end{array}$}{\Big]}\Big)\,.

The factor of
D_{a}^{-1}
is included due to an argument presented in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that for a pair of charges
a,\,{\overline{a}}
on a sphere, each basis vector in the degenerate block appears with the same weight. To produce a correctly normalized state, this weight should be
D_{a}^{-1}
.

It was argued in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that the trace of a projector MPO of length
L
(assuming the existence of a unique unit element) is dominated by the leading eigenvector of the
0
block,
\lambda_{0}>\lambda_{s}
for
s\neq 0
. This contributes a factor of
\lambda_{0}^{L}
and picks out the elements of the crossing tensor
t_{a}^{ppp0}
satisfying
s=0
.
To be consistent with a faithful MPO representation of the UFC calculus, the traced crossing tensor must yield the quantum dimension of the closed loop it forms, i.e.
d_{p}
for the entry of the crossing tensor with coefficient
t_{a}^{ppp0}
.
Using Eq.(
333
) we find that
H_{0}
scales as

\displaystyle H_{0}\approx\log(\lambda_{0})L-\gamma_{a}\,,

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}=-\log\left(\sum_{p}\frac{d_{p}{t}_{a}^{ppp0}}{D_{a}\mathcal{D}^{2}}\right)=\log(\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}})-\log(d_{a})\,.

(337)

This provides a general method to extract the TEE of superselection sectors directly on the lattice, including the well known vacuum TEE
\gamma_{0}=\log(\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}})
.

IV.5
Fusion

The fusion spaces of the emergent theory are found by considering the tensor network on a thrice-punctured sphere.
To construct these fusion spaces we make a slight change of notation for the tubes

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Tube}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Annulus}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIa}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIAnnulus}\end{array}\,.

Now the splitting space
V_{c}^{ab}
is constructed from tensor networks of the form

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube3}\end{array}:=\sum_{pqr}c^{pqr}_{\mu}{d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{q}^{\frac{1}{4}}}{d_{r}^{\frac{1}{4}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube4}\end{array}\,.

There is an important subtlety in calculating the basis vectors of the spanning set, due to the local relation induced by growing an
\omega_{0}
-loop over the thrice punctured sphere and fusing it into the skeleton.
Hence the splitting space is spanned by vertex tensors
\mu_{ab}^{c}
in the support subspace of the following projector

\displaystyle\sum_{sijklpq}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{4}}{d_{s}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube5}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube3}\end{array}\,,

where
\mu_{ab}^{c}
indicates
\mu\in{V}^{ab}_{c}
.
More specifically, the relation is induced by creating an
\omega_{0}
-loop between
\underline{c}
and
\underline{a},\underline{b}
and fusing it into the edges around the
\mu_{ab}^{c}
vertex. This also results in a correlated action by
(\underline{a})_{ij},(\underline{b})_{kl}
and
(\underline{c})^{\dagger}_{pq}

on the
\underline{a},\underline{b}
and
\underline{c}
sectors, respectively. These actions permute the internal states within each degenerate block.

Considering Eq.(
316
), and the fact that the
\omega_{0}
-loop is a projector, one finds that the action in Eq.(
IV.5
) is also a projector.
By taking the trace of this projector we find
N_{ab}^{c}
, the dimension of its support subspace. After using the tube
S
-matrix to expand
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
in a complimentary tube basis one can derive the well known Verlinde formula
Pasquier 1987
;
Verlinde 1988

\displaystyle N_{ab}^{c}=\sum_{x}\frac{S_{ax}S_{bx}S_{{\overline{c}}x}}{S_{0x}}\,,

(347)

for example see Ref.
Aasen et al. 2017
.
This formula holds for any modular theory, which includes all the theories we consider as they are Drinfeld centers.

In the case that
\underline{a},\underline{b},
and
\underline{c}
are nondegenerate, i.e. one dimensional,
c_{\mu}^{pqr}
is a delta function that determines a unique fusion vertex,
\mu_{ab}^{c}
.
This fusion vertex,
\mu_{ab}^{c}
, must satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{4}}{d_{s}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube2}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube3}\end{array}\,,

which allows one to absorb the
\underline{c}
ICI into
\underline{a},\underline{b}
and
\mu_{ab}^{c}
.
More explicitly, for nondegenerate ICIs
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube6}\end{array}\,,

for the unique vertex
\mu_{ab}^{c}
that satisfies Eq.(
IV.5
).

Eq.(
IV.5
) follows from the identity

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation5}\end{array}=\sum_{ij}(\underline{\widetilde{a}})_{ij}^{\dagger}\,\underline{a}\,(\underline{{a}})_{ij}\,,

which takes a slightly more illuminating form for a nondegenerate block

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation5}\end{array}={\underline{\widetilde{a}}}^{\dagger}\,\underline{a}\,.

The special case of this equation for
\underline{a}=\underline{0}
, corresponds to sliding an MPO over an
\omega_{0}
-loop

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation8}\end{array}=\sum_{s}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation9}\end{array}\,,

which can be intuitively understood as follows: the
\underline{0}
ICI simply projects onto a disc of unaltered tensor network, through which the MPO is free to move.

The above identities are a consequence of the following slight generalization of Eq.(
322
), which is proved in much the same way,

\displaystyle\sum_{rs}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation0}\end{array}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{pqr}^{s})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}=\sum_{a_{ij}}(\underline{\widetilde{a}})_{ij}^{\dagger}(\underline{a})_{ij}\,,

where

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{pqr}^{s})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation6}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle(\underline{\widetilde{a}})_{ij}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqrs}(t^{pqrs}_{a_{ij}})^{*}\,(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{pqr}^{s})^{\dagger}.

We remark that Eq.(
IV.5
) is somewhat analogous to state teleportation in quantum information, where
\underline{a}
plays the role of the state being teleported and the delta condition arising from multiplication with the idempotent plays the role of the projective measurement.

Similar to Eq.(
IV.5
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ICIteleportation7}\end{array}=\sum_{ij}(\underline{a})_{ij}^{\dagger}\,\underline{a}\,(\underline{\widetilde{a}})_{ij}\,,

with analogous definitions of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{pqr}^{s}
and
(\underline{\widetilde{a}})_{ij}
to those in Eq.(
IV.5
).

IV.5.1
Cups, caps and the Frobenius-Schur indicator

The special case of pair fusion (creation) to (from) the vacuum is captured by the cap (cup) tube

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{tubecap}\end{array}\,,

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{tubecup}\end{array}\,,

respectively. Where
\underline{\bar{a}}
is the ICI for which the above tubes are nonzero.
The FS indicator can be found by composing a cup and a cap
Aasen
, which corresponds to unbending an anyon worldline

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{tubefsind}\end{array}=\varkappa_{a}\,\underline{a}\,.

We remark that the the FS indicator only has an invariant meaning for self inverse particles,
{\overline{a}}=a
, in which case
\varkappa_{a}\pm 1
.

IV.5.2
F
-symbol associators

Assuming no block degeneracy, the
F
-symbols for the doubled theory, denoted
\mathcal{F}
, are given by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TubeF2}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f}\mathcal{F}^{abc}_{def}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TubeF3}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\sum_{pqrst}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TubeF0}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}pqrstu\\
f\rho\sigma\end{subarray}}{F^{pqr}_{stu}}c^{pqt}_{\mu}c^{trs}_{\nu}{\overline{c}}_{qru}^{\rho}{\overline{c}}_{pus}^{\sigma}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{TubeF1}\end{array}\,,

where, due to the no degeneracy assumption,
c_{pqt}^{\mu}
and
{\overline{c}}^{qru}_{\rho}
are delta functions. We remark that the above equation should be interpreted as part of a larger diagram, involving a sphere with four punctures, that have been projected onto idempotents
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
and
\underline{d}
respectively, similar to the thrice punctured sphere depicted in Eq.(
IV.5
). The LHS of the above equation has an implicit
\underline{e}
idempotent between the fusion vertices, which was absorbed into the
\mu
vertex using Eq.(
IV.5
). Similarly, the line connecting the fusion vertices on the RHS has been resolved into a sum over idempotents
\underline{f}
, using Eq.(
323
), which have been absorbed into the
\rho
vertex using Eq.(
IV.5
).

There is a similar, although somewhat more involved equation for
F
for degenerate blocks.
By considering a sphere with five punctures one can check that the emergent
F
-symbols satisfy the pentagon equation.

IV.6
Braiding

The exchange of superselection sectors can be implemented by a deformation of the tensor network

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabmatrix0}\end{array}\xrightarrow{\text{Exchange}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabmatrix1}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ijs}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{2}}{d_{s}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabmatrix2}\end{array}\,,

where we have used Eq.(
IV.5
) to find a tensor corresponding to the
R^{ab}
matrix.
We remark that there is a correlated action
(\underline{a})_{ij}
on the internal states of the
\underline{a}
block, if it is degenerate, which we do not explicitly depict.

IV.6.1
R
-matrix

If the
\underline{a}
ICI is nondegenerate, there is no summation over
i,j
and we simply have the above formula with
\underline{a}^{\dagger}
in place of
(\underline{a})^{\dagger}_{ij}
.
Hence for nondegenerate
\underline{a}
and
\underline{b}
we have a formula for the
R
matrix

\displaystyle R^{ab}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabmatrix3}\end{array}=\sum_{q}\frac{(t_{a}^{s^{a}q\,s^{a}s^{b}})^{*}}{d_{s^{b}}}\,,

where
s^{a}
is defined by the equation
\underline{a}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{ppp}=\delta_{p,s^{a}}\,\underline{a}
.

The
R
matrix can be resolved into sectors of definite charge to find the
{R^{ab}_{c}}
symbols.
For nondegenerate ICIs
\underline{a},\underline{b},\underline{c}
, and the unique fusion vertices
\mu_{ba}^{c}
, and
\nu_{ab}^{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabc0}\end{array}={R^{ab}_{c}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabc1}\end{array}\,,

which should be interpreted as part of a larger fusion diagram such as Eq.(
IV.5
). An explicit formula for
{R^{ab}_{c}}
can be derived from Eq.(
IV.6.1
).
We remark that a similar line of reasoning applies to degenerate ICIs, but the analysis becomes more complicated.

V
Symmetry-enriched topological order from matrix product operators

In Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
, a formalism was introduced for the classification of symmetry-enriched topological order in two spatial dimensions using
\mathcal{G}
-graded matrix product operator algebras.
The formalism was based on finding MPO representations
{\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
of the physical symmetry group
\mathcal{G}
.
These MPOs correspond to
\mathcal{G}
-domain walls. Only unitary, on-site representations of finite groups were explicitly considered, but this turns out to capture the same SET phases without making the on-site assumption
Etingof et al. 2009
and there is a simple extension to also include anti-unitary elements
Cheng et al. 2016
.

In this section we develop the theory of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras, drawing on the previous results derived for MPO algebras in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
.
We find that the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras, with a given underlying
1
-sector MPO algebra, correspond to
\mathcal{G}
extensions of that
1
-sector MPO algebra.
We establish a close analogy between
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras and
\mathcal{G}
-graded UFCs, for which the extension problem has been thoroughly analyzed in Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
.
The
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPOs are used to define a symmetry-enriched pulling through condition for PEPS tensors that lead to symmetric tensor networks under the global symmetry.
Finally we describe how calculations involving the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPOs can be abstracted to a diagrammatic calculus that only keeps track of the relevant topological information.

V.1
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras

We consider MPO representations of a finite group
\mathcal{G}
consisting of translation invariant elements of the form

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{L}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\dots i_{L}\\
j_{1}\dots j_{L}\end{subarray}}\text{Tr}[\Delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{i_{1}j_{1}}B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{i_{L}j_{L}}]\,\ket{i_{1}\cdots i_{L}}\bra{j_{1}\dots j_{L}}\,,

(372)

for arbitrary length
L
. The object
B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{ij}
is a
{\chi}_{\text{\bf{g}}}\times{\chi}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
matrix for each value of
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathsf{D}}
, where
\chi_{\text{\bf{g}}}
is the bond dimension of the MPO and
\mathsf{D}
corresponds to the bond dimension of a PEPS.
\Delta_{\text{\bf{g}}}
is also a
{\chi}_{\text{\bf{g}}}\times{\chi}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
matrix.
In the conventional tensor network notation, we have

\displaystyle(B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{ij})_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gMPOtensor}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gMPO}\end{array}\,,

where dashed lines are used to denote periodic boundary conditions.

We restrict our attention to MPOs that can be brought into canonical form
Pérez-García et al. 2007
;
Cirac et al. 2017
after insertion of
\Delta_{\text{\bf{g}}}
. Hence the tensors have a block diagonal structure

\displaystyle B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{ij}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}B_{a}^{ij}\,,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\bigoplus_{a\in\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}w_{a}\openone_{\chi_{a}}\,,

(375)

where
B_{a}^{ij}
are a set of
\chi_{a}\times\chi_{a}
matrices that generate the full matrix algebra,
w_{a}
are nonzero complex numbers, and
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
refers to the collection of MPOs appearing in the decomposition of
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
. These MPOs are referred to as
single block
, since they cannot be decomposed further. The full collection of single block MPOs in all sectors is denoted
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
We remark that
\Delta_{\text{\bf{g}}}
decomposes into a direct sum of multiples of the identity since it must commute with the full matrix algebra on each block, as the MPO is translationally invariant.

In tensor network notation Eq.(
375
), left, is written as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gMPOtensor2}\end{array}=\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOtensor2}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOtensor}\end{array}=(B_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{ij})_{\alpha\beta}\,,

and the shorthand notation
a_{\text{\bf{g}}}
indicates
\text{MPO}_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
. We also use the shorthand
\lfloor{a}\rfloor\in\mathcal{G}
to indicate
\text{MPO}_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lfloor{a}\rfloor}
, i.e.
\lfloor{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\rfloor={\text{\bf{g}}}
. We remark that each
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
MPO tensor is only nonzero within the relevant
\chi_{a}\times\chi_{a}
block of the virtual index, hence a direct sum is implicit in the equation above, left.

The
single block
MPOs are then

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPO}\end{array}\,,

which satisfy

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\sum_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}w_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,\text{MPO}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,.

Since the
g
MPOs form a representation of
\mathcal{G}
we have
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
.
The MPO algebra must have a faithful
\mathcal{G}
-grading, i.e.
|\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|>0
for all
g
, otherwise application of the physical symmetry to a tensor network can annihilate the state.
If
|\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}|=1
there is no underlying topological order, and the formalism recovers the cohomology classification of SPT phases
Chen et al. 2013
;
Chen et al. 2011b
;
Levin and Gu 2012
in
({2}+1)
D, this case was thoroughly analyzed in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
.
In general this representation yields a Hermitian MPO projector onto the symmetric subspace

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\in\mathcal{G}}\text{MPO}^{L}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,,

(380)

for all lengths
L
.
In Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
such MPO projectors where analyzed extensively, and it was found that many concepts from the theory of fusion categories naturally emerge. At an intuitive level, such MPO projectors correspond to a representation of a fusion category.
In particular, the theory of MPOs and the fact that we have a representation, implies

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}\text{MPO}_{b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{c}\text{MPO}_{c}=\sum_{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{gh}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\text{MPO}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\,,

(381)

\displaystyle\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}w_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}w_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}=w_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\,,

(382)

since
N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{c}
vanishes unless
c\in\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
.
Remarkably it also implies a local version of this condition on the level of individual MPO tensors. That is, there exists a set of independent
fusion tensors
, and their left inverses

\displaystyle[X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu}]_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOfusion}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle[(X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu})^{-1}]^{\alpha\beta}_{\gamma}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOsplitting}\end{array}\,,

where
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{N_{ab}^{c}}
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agbhMPOfusion}\end{array}=\delta_{ab}^{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{cghMPOfusion}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{cMPOsplittingfusion}\end{array}=\delta_{ab}^{c}\delta_{\mu\nu}\delta_{cd}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{cMPOline}\end{array}\,,

where the
{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}
line on the right indicates the identity on the
{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}
block of the virtual index.
We remark that the fusion tensor are only defined up to an invertible gauge transformation on the degeneracy index,
\mu
,

\displaystyle X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu}\,\mapsto\,\widetilde{X}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\nu}:=\sum_{\mu}(Y_{ab}^{c})_{\nu\mu}X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu}\,.

(389)

For a symmetric PEPS on a sphere, a second type of MPO arises that has a reversed orientation of the virtual bond. These MPO are not independent from those introduced earlier, and lead to an arrow reversing gauge transformation. Consider a sphere and partition the on-site site representation into an action on the northern and southern hemispheres,

\displaystyle U({\text{\bf{g}}})=U_{\text{N}}({\text{\bf{g}}})\otimes U_{\text{S}}({\text{\bf{g}}})\,,

(390)

respectively. Applying
U_{\text{S}}({\text{\bf{g}}})
to a symmetric PEPS — see Section
V.4
— leads to a domain wall along the equator, represented by
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
. Applying
U_{\text{N}}({\text{\bf{g}}})
instead, leads to a different MPO

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{-}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}w_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\text{MPO}^{-}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,,

(391)

where

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{-}_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agminusMPO}\end{array}\,,

and

\displaystyle(B_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{-})^{ij}_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agminusMPOtensor}\end{array}\,.

This is simply because the boundary of the southern disc has the opposite orientation to the boundary of the northern disc.
Hence, by applying
U_{\text{N}}({\text{\bf{g}}})=(\openone\otimes U_{\text{S}}({\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})\,)\,U({\text{\bf{g}}})
to a symmetric PEPS on the sphere we find

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{-}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,.

(394)

The theory of MPS implies that there is a unique label
{{{\overline{a}}}_{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}}
, for each
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
, such that

\displaystyle\text{MPO}^{-}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{{{\overline{a}}}_{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{and}\,,

\displaystyle w_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=w_{{{{\overline{a}}}_{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}}}\,,

(395)

which also satisfies
{\overline{{\overline{a}}}}=a
.
Furthermore, there exists a local gauge transformation

\displaystyle Z_{a}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Za}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle Z_{a}^{-1}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Zainv}\end{array}\,,

such that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agminusMPOtensororientationreversal}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agplusMPOtensor}\end{array}\,,

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agminusMPOtensororientationreversal2}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agplusMPOtensor2}\end{array}\,.

Note the same gauge transformation must work for both orientations in a consistent MPO representation
Williamson et al. 2016b
.
By applying Eq.(
V.1
) twice, we find that
Z_{a}(Z_{{\overline{a}}}^{-1})^{T}
must commute with the set of matrices
B_{a}^{ij}
. Hence
{Z_{a}(Z_{{\overline{a}}}^{-1})^{T}=\varkappa_{a}\openone_{\chi_{a}}}
, for some complex number
\varkappa_{a}
.
This implies

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Za}\end{array}=\varkappa_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Zainvconj}\end{array}\,,

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Zaconj}\end{array}=\varkappa_{{\overline{a}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Zainv}\end{array}\,,

we also find
\varkappa_{{\overline{a}}}=\varkappa_{a}^{-1}
. The magnitude
|\varkappa_{{\overline{a}}}|
can be absorbed into the definition of
Z_{a}
and the phase
\frac{\varkappa_{a}}{|\varkappa_{{a}}|}
can also be absorbed unless
a={\overline{a}}
, in which case we have
\varkappa_{a}=\pm 1
. It will be shown later that this quantity corresponds to the familiar Frobenius-Schur indicator from the theory of fusion categories.

V.2
Classification via group extensions of MPO algebras

\mathcal{G}
-graded matrix product operator algebras can be classified by
\mathcal{G}
extensions of an underlying MPO algebra, that corresponds to the
1
-sector of the resulting graded algebra.

V.2.1
Summary of the problem and comparison to previous work

\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
occur as group extensions of an ungraded MPO algebra
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
that forms the
1
sector. This is a generalization of the more familiar group extension problem, where
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
plays the role of the normal subgroup, and
\mathcal{G}
the quotient group. For the class of MPO algebras that correspond to fusion categories, which we focus on, one can apply the thorough analysis of the group extension problem for fusion categories given in Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
.

The first step in the search for faithful
\mathcal{G}
extensions of an MPO algebra
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
can intuitively be thought of as a hunt for
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras
\mathcal{C}_{G}
that make

\displaystyle 1\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\rightarrow 1\,,

(406)

into a short exact sequence.
From left to right, the arrows correspond to: a map from unit to the
0
MPO (defined more precisely below), an injection that includes
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
as the trivial sector in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, a surjection that maps
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
down to
g
, and a map from all elements of
\mathcal{G}
to
1
, respectively.

This extension problem includes the group extension problem as a special case in the following way: for
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}=\text{Vec}_{N}
and
\mathcal{G}=Q
, any group
G
that is an extension of
Q
by
N
yields a solution
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{Vec}_{G}
.
There are solutions beyond these group extensions, as demonstrated by the example
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}=\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, for which we find solutions where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
has Ising fusion rules, along with solutions that have
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
fusion rules. This example is explored further in Section
IX
.

There is a potential obstruction to finding an associative set of
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion rules which lies in the third cohomology group
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, explained in detail below. If this obstruction vanishes, we have a consistent set of
\mathcal{G}
-extended fusion rules. These form a torsor over
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
as shifting by such a 2-cocycle leads to another consistent solution.

A consistent
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion algebra alone does not solve the extension problem. Since the fusion of open MPOs is only associative up to some boundary operator, we need to solve a categorification of the underlying algebra problem. Hence we must also check whether a consistent set of associators can be found, to relate the fusion of open MPOs in different orders. This amounts to finding
F
-symbols that solve the pentagon equation for the previously constructed
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion algebra. Any such solution can be realized by a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra via the fixed point construction in Section
III
.
It was shown in Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
that there is a potential obstruction to finding a consistent set of associators which lies in
\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
. We find that this obstruction vanishes for the class of MPOs considered here, and the resulting solutions form
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
torsors. That is, multiplying any solution by a 3-cocycle results in another — possibly distinct — solution. Physically this corresponds to the fact that a classification of SET phases depends upon the choice of on-site symmetry with respect to which they are classified.

For the above examples, where
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}=\text{Vec}_{N}
,
\mathcal{G}=Q
, and the fusion rules are given by
G
— a group extension of
Q
by
N
— the potential
\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
obstruction disappears as the trivial
F
-symbols are a valid solution to the pentagon equation that yields
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{Vec}_{G}
.
Shifting by an element
[\alpha]\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
corresponds to twisting the extension by a 3-cocycle to obtain
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{Vec}_{G}^{\alpha}
.
More generally, for
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}=\text{Vec}_{N}^{\omega}
with
[\omega]\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(N,\mathsf{U}(1))
and
\mathcal{G}=Q
we find several valid extensions with
G
fusion rules. These extensions are given by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}=\text{Vec}_{G}^{\hat{\omega}}
for
[\hat{\omega}]\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(G,\mathsf{U}(1))
that satisfy
\hat{\omega}\big|_{N}\equiv\omega
.

This class of examples can be obtained by gauging normal subgroups of SPT phases, see Appendix.
A
. They capture the formalisms of Refs.
Wen 2002
;
Mesaros and Ran 2013
;
Jiang and Ran 2017
;
Garre-Rubio et al. 2017
;
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
as follows:

•

The unitary finite group case of Wen’s projective symmetry group classification
Wen 2002
is captured by restricting
\omega
and
\hat{\omega}
to be trivial.

•

Similarly, Ref.
Garre-Rubio et al. 2017
is captured by restricting
\omega
and
\hat{\omega}
to be trivial.

•

Conversely, Ref.
Mesaros and Ran 2013
is captured by restricting to trivial direct product extensions
G\cong N\times Q
, but with arbitrary
\omega
and
\hat{\omega}
.

•

Interestingly, Ref.
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
is captured by restricting to
\text{Vec}_{G}^{\hat{\omega}}
— with possibly nontrivial
\omega
and
\hat{\omega}
— that are Morita equivalent to some
\text{Vec}_{A}
, for
A
an abelian group. For further explanation see Sections
VIII.3
and
IX.2
.

•

The unitary finite group case of Ref.
Jiang and Ran 2017
is captured with no further restrictions.

It is clear that only considering extensions
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
whose fusion rules are given by a finite group is very restrictive and misses many interesting cases, such as the Ising fusion rules needed to describe the EM duality enriched toric code, see Sections
IX.1
,
II.2.2
. Furthermore, all such MPOs can be generated by an on-site representation times a diagonal local unitary circuit, see Refs.
Chen et al. 2011b
;
Williamson et al. 2016b
;
Wang et al. 2015
, while the more general MPOs may require a linear depth unitary circuit, for instance the Ising MPO.

V.2.2
MPO representations satisfying the group-zipper condition

At this point we assume that a stronger version of the local fusion condition holds for the MPO group representation. This allows us to make contact with the theory of group extensions of fusion categories
Etingof et al. 2009
. The MPO representation of a
\mathcal{G}
domain wall correspond to an invertible bimodule in the language of Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
.
We restrict to the case where the full group MPOs satisfy the
group-zipper condition
: that there exist tensors, together with left inverses

\displaystyle X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gzipperMPOfusion}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{-1}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gzipperMPOsplitting}\end{array}\,,

such that

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ghzipperMPOsplitting1}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ghzipperMPOsplitting2}\end{array}\,.

These tensors define a left and right group action on the
\chi_{g}\times\chi_{g}
matrix algebra of the
g
virtual bond

\displaystyle{\text{\bf{h}}}\circ M_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{hleftaction}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle M_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\circ{\text{\bf{h}}}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{hrightaction}\end{array}\,.

The subset of matrices — modulo multiples of the identity on each block — that commute with
B_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{ij}
for all
ij
, define the group of autoequivalences of the
g
domain wall,
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
.
The right and left actions in Eq.(
V.2.2
) imply isomorphisms
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{hg}}}}\cong\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\cong\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
, due to the zipper condition, and hence
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\cong\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.
It was shown in Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
that
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\cong\mathcal{A}
, the group of abelian anyons of the emergent topological order
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}})
.
It is beyond the scope of this paper to rigorously establish that our definition of
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
matches that of Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
, but they are analogous.

The equivalence
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\cong\mathcal{A}
can be understood physically by considering a topological order
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}})
with a gapped boundary to vacuum
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
. Pushing abelian anyons from the bulk onto the boundary excitations induces an autoequivalence of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
for each element of
\mathcal{A}
. This picture suggests that only the abelian anyons that do not condense on the boundary induce interesting autoequivalences of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
, which lines up with the results of the example in Section
IX.1
.

The
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
tensors define a matrix

\displaystyle T({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{anomaly3cocycle}\end{array}\,,

that lies in
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{ghk}}}}
and satisfies the 3-cocycle equation

\displaystyle{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0}\circ T({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\,T({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\,T({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2}})\circ{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}=T({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\,T({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\,.

(414)

If we modify our choice of
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
, by an element
M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\in\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
, to
X^{\prime}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}=X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
we find that

\displaystyle T^{\prime}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})=M_{{\text{\bf{gh}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}}^{-1}\,(M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{-1}\circ{\text{\bf{k}}})\,T({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\,({\text{\bf{g}}}\circ M_{{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})\,M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{hk}}}}\,,

(415)

which corresponds to a transformation by a coboundary.

Hence
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
yields an element
[T]\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, that is invariant under modifying the choice of
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
by an element of
\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
. This element
[T]
constitutes an obstruction to a consistent MPO representation of a set of
\mathcal{G}
domain walls.
Intuitively, the obstruction can be thought of as an anomalous anyonic charge appearing from the fusion of domain walls.
It is unclear at this point whether an MPO group representation can be found with
[T]\neq 0
. If they were to be found, these MPOs would be relevant to the understanding of tensor network representations of SETs with anomalous symmetry fractionalization, such as those occurring on the boundary of a
({3}+1)
D higher form SPT
Thorngren and von
Keyserlingk 2015
;
Fidkowski and Vishwanath 2015
.

Consistent fusion of the MPO representation requires
[T]=0
and that
T
has been transformed, by a coboundary, into a multiple of the identity.
Tensor solutions
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
that satisfy
[T]=0
, form
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
torsors. That is, we can modify any solution
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
by a 2-cocycle
M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\in\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
satisfying

\displaystyle(M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\circ{\text{\bf{k}}})\,M_{{\text{\bf{gh}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}}=({\text{\bf{g}}}\circ M_{{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})\,M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{hk}}}}\,,

(416)

to find a new solution
X^{\prime}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}=X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}M_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}

Hence we have
T({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})=\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\openone_{{\text{\bf{ghk}}}}
, where we may take
\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\in\mathsf{U}(1)
by normalizing the
X_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
tensors.
This yields an associator

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{3cocycle1}\end{array}=\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{3cocycle2}\end{array}\,.

Considering the reduction of
{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}
, one finds a relation

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{4cocycletrees}\end{array}\,,

leading to the phase

\displaystyle\nu({\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}):=\frac{\alpha({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\alpha({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\alpha({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2}})}{\alpha({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})\alpha({{\text{\bf{g}}}_{0}{\text{\bf{g}}}_{1},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{2},{\text{\bf{g}}}_{3}})}\,,

(420)

which is a 4-cocycle. This constitutes an
\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
obstruction
Etingof et al. 2009
to finding a solution to the pentagon equation for the MPO representation.
We find that this obstruction always vanishes for the MPOs we consider, due to the zipper condition we have assumed. It would be interesting to generalize our framework to capture a nontrivial
\mathcal{H}^{4}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
obstruction, which is relevant for the understanding of tensor network representations of anomalous SETs such as those occurring on the boundary of a
({3}+1)
D SPT
Fidkowski et al. 2013
;
Burnell et al. 2014
;
Chen et al. 2015
.

Since this obstruction vanishes, the possible solutions to the pentagon equation form an
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
torsor. That is, any
F
-symbol solution may be multiplied by a 3-cocycle to obtain another solution which may or may not be distinct.

V.3
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras satisfying the stronger zipper condition and
\mathcal{G}
-graded unitary fusion categories

We move on by narrowing our focus to the class of MPOs that are relevant for the description of anomaly free SET phases in (2+1)D. The properties thus introduced allow for an identification of these MPOs with
\mathcal{G}
-graded UFCs.
In the process, we recount many results shown in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
and put them into the context of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras, which are relevant for the description of domain walls in SET ordered states.

From this point on we assume that the stronger
zipper condition
holds for the single block MPOs, and the fusion tensors defined in Eq.(
V.1
)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agbhzipperMPOsplitting1}\end{array}=\sum_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\mu}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agbhzipperMPOsplitting2}\end{array}\,.

The class of MPOs satisfying the zipper condition is sufficiently general to capture representations of all
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion categories, via the fixed point construction explained in Section.
III
.

The associativity of the product
(\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\text{MPO}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})\text{MPO}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\text{MPO}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\text{MPO}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}})
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{{{e}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{e}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}N_{{{e}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}^{{{d}_{{\text{\bf{ghk}}}}}}=\sum_{{{f}_{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}}}N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{f}_{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}}}^{{{d}_{{\text{\bf{ghk}}}}}}N_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}^{{{f}_{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}}}\,.

(423)

The addition of the zipper condition further implies the existence of
F
-symbol
associators
Bultinck et al. 2017a

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gfmove1}\end{array}=\sum_{f\mu\nu}{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e\alpha\beta}^{f\mu\nu}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gfmove2}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle{[F^{abc}_{d}]_{e\alpha\beta}^{f\mu\nu}}=\frac{1}{\chi_{d}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gFysmbol}\end{array}\,,

that satisfy the
pentagon equation

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{gPentagon}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}{[F^{pcd}_{e}]_{q\beta\gamma}^{r\mu\nu}}{[F^{abr}_{e}]_{p\alpha\nu}^{s\sigma\tau}}=\sum_{x\kappa\lambda\eta}{[F^{abc}_{q}]_{p\alpha\beta}^{x\kappa\lambda}}{[F^{axd}_{e}]_{q\lambda\gamma}^{s\eta\tau}}{[F^{bcd}_{s}]_{x\kappa\eta}^{r\mu\sigma}}\,.

(428)

Hence all obstructions vanish for MPO representations satisfying this zipper condition, and we have a consistent fusion theory.
It was shown in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that MPO algebras satisfying the zipper condition correspond closely to fusion categories.
Hence the structure of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras that satisfy the zipper condition correspond closely to
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion categories.

The classification of
\mathcal{G}
SET phases for a given underlying topological order is given by
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras containing the MPO algebra of the underlying topological order as
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
.
It was shown in Ref.
Edie-Michell 2017a
that seemingly different
\mathcal{G}
-extensions of a UFC
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
that has nontrivial monoidal auto-equivalences, can actually coincide. This leads to a possible overcounting of
\mathcal{G}
-extensions and we expect a similar result to hold for
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras.
There is a further subtlety in the classification of SET phases, as substantially different MPO algebras may give rise to the same emergent SET. We say that a pair of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras are
\mathcal{G}
-graded Morita equivalent
if they lead to the same emergent SET. A method to calculate the emergent SET from the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra is described in Section
VI
. Hence, for the SET phase classification, one should consider
\mathcal{G}
extensions of all representatives in the Morita equivalence class of the underlying topological order, up to
\mathcal{G}
-graded Morita equivalence. We remark that this is a looser equivalence relation than the graded monoidal equivalence considered in Ref.
Edie-Michell 2017a
.

V.3.1
Imposing further restrictions to isolate the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras that describe SET orders

Thus far we have seen the structure of associative fusion rules arise from MPO algebras.
We proceed to restrict our attention to MPO algebras that satisfy a range of further conditions, derived from structures in a unitary fusion category.

Firstly, we restrict to irreducible projector MPOs, i.e. those where
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
cannot be written as a sum of inequivalent nonzero projector MPOs (PMPOs)

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\neq\text{PMPO}+\text{PMPO}^{\prime}\,.

(429)

This excludes the possibility of cat state projectors that occur for spontaneous symmetry breaking phases. It corresponds to restricting to stable TQFTs, and we note the nonreducible case can be reconstructed by taking a sum of irreducible PMPOs.

Next we further restrict our attention to MPO representations that satisfy
\text{MPO}^{\dagger}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
. Equivalently, MPO representations that are unitary within the support subspace of a Hermitian unit MPO. This implies the MPO projector in Eq.(
380
) is also Hermitian.
For a consistent unitary MPO representation we must have
{\text{MPO}^{\dagger}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{{{\overline{a}}}_{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}}}=\text{MPO}^{-}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
, that is, the conjugate particle defined by Hermitian conjugation must match that defined by orientation reversal. In this case the left handed tensor is defined by reflecting and complex conjugating the right handed tensor
(B_{a}^{-})^{ij}_{\alpha\beta}=(B_{a}^{ij})^{*}_{\beta\alpha}
. Graphically

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agminusMPOtensor}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agplusMPOtensor3}\end{array}\,,

i.e. complex conjugation is equivalent to reflection of the tensor. This implies

\displaystyle N_{ab}^{c}=N_{{\overline{a}}{\overline{b}}}^{{\overline{c}}}\,,

(432)

and additionally we have
w_{\bar{a}}=w_{a}^{*}
. Moreover, it was shown in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that the
w_{a}
are real numbers and that
w_{a}>0
follows from Eq.(
382
), and the fact that
N_{ab}^{c}
has only nonnegative entries.

We further assume that the single block MPOs have been brought into a canonical form
Pérez-García et al. 2007
;
Cirac et al. 2017
that admits unitary gauge transformation matrices
Z_{a}
and isometric fusion matrices
X_{ab}^{c}
.

It was shown in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that, for projector MPOs satisfying our assumptions, each block
a
has an element
e_{a}
that satisfies
N_{a{\overline{a}}}^{e_{a}}=1
. We further assume that there is a unique, single block
unit MPO

0
satisfying
N_{a{\overline{a}}}^{0}=1
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
. It was shown
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that, with this assumption,
{\overline{0}}=0
and
\varkappa_{0}=1
. Furthermore,
N_{a{\overline{b}}}^{0}=\delta_{ab}
and

\displaystyle N_{ab}^{c}=N_{b{\overline{c}}}^{{\overline{a}}}=N_{{\overline{c}}a}^{{\overline{b}}}\,,

which implies

\displaystyle\delta_{ab}=N_{a0}^{b}=N_{0a}^{b}\,,

(433)

and hence
0
plays the role of the trivial element under fusion, as expected.
We do not expect all irreducible projector MPOs to be unital, as the existence of a unit is posited as an assumption in the theory of fusion categories.
