Given the definition of the unit element, we require that the
F
-symbols have been brought into a gauge where

\displaystyle\delta_{ab}^{c}\delta_{\mu\nu}={[F^{a0b}_{c}]_{a0\mu}^{b0\nu}}={[F^{ab0}_{c}]_{b\mu 0}^{a0\nu}}={[F^{0ab}_{c}]_{b0\mu}^{a\nu 0}}\,,

(434)

where the first equality corresponds to the
triangle equation
and the latter equalities follow by combining it with the pentagon equation.
Then it was shown
Bultinck et al. 2017a
that the
F
-symbols can be brought into a compatible gauge satisfying

\displaystyle{[F^{a{\overline{a}}a}_{a}]_{000}^{000}}=\frac{\varkappa_{a}}{d_{a}}\,,

(435)

with
d_{a}=d_{{\overline{a}}}>0
, and
\varkappa_{a}
matching the definition given in Eq.(
V.1
).

Consistent fusion of the MPOs within a tensor network requires that removing an infinitesimal loop of
\text{MPO}_{a}
results in a weight
d_{a}
. A local sufficient condition for this is

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{bubblepopping0}\end{array}=d_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{bubblepopping1}\end{array}\,,

where
P
corresponds to the projector onto the support subspace of the PEPS virtual index.
This implies that
d_{a}
matches the definition of quantum dimension via the Perron-Frobenius vector, i.e.

\displaystyle d_{a}d_{b}=\sum_{c}N_{ab}^{c}d_{c}\,.

(438)

For a consistent MPO representation of
\mathcal{G}
domain walls within a tensor network, we furthermore require that an infinitesimal loop of domain wall
\mathcal{G}
can be removed. This corresponds to a local sufficient condition

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{bubblepopping2}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{bubblepopping3}\end{array}\,,

which implies

\displaystyle w_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\frac{d_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{g}}}^{2}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{2}:=\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}d_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{2}\,.

(441)

Hence the MPO representation of
\mathcal{G}
is given by

\displaystyle\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\frac{d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{2}}\,\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(442)

in particular,
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
corresponds to what is known as the
\omega_{0}
-projector for
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.

At this point we renormalize the fusion and splitting tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOfusion}\end{array}=d_{a}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{b}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{c}^{-\frac{1}{4}}[X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu}]_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOsplitting}\end{array}=d_{a}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{b}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{c}^{-\frac{1}{4}}([X_{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}b_{\text{\bf{h}}}}^{c_{\text{\bf{gh}}}\,\mu}]_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma})^{*}\,.

This is to ensure that removing a loop of
\text{MPO}_{a}\text{MPO}_{{\overline{a}}}
fusing to the vacuum
\text{MPO}_{0}
, matches the removal of an
\text{MPO}_{a}
loop within a tensor network

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agbhzipperMPOsplitting1}\end{array}=\sum_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\mu}\sqrt{\frac{d_{c}}{d_{a}d_{b}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agbhzipperMPOsplitting2}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{cMPOsplittingfusion}\end{array}=\delta_{ab}^{c}\delta_{\mu\nu}\delta_{cd}\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{cMPOline}\end{array}\,.

We focus on the class of MPOs admitting a set of unitary matrices
A^{ab}_{c},B^{ab}_{c}
that satisfy
pivotal identities
relating the fusion and splitting tensors

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOAlinebending}\end{array}=\sum_{\nu}[A^{ab}_{c}]^{\nu}_{\mu}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOAlinebending2}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOBlinebending}\end{array}=\sum_{\nu}[B^{ab}_{c}]^{\nu}_{\mu}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{agMPOBlinebending2}\end{array}\,.

This requires
N_{ab}^{c}=N_{{\overline{a}}c}^{b}
, which follows from Eqs.(
432
) and (
433
).
These pivotal identities are related to the existence of a pivotal structure in the theory of fusion categories.
We believe that the above pivotal identities can be derived for the class of MPOs we are considering, however a proof is beyond the scope of this paper. We remark that such a proof was given in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
for the SPT case where
|\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}|=1
.

For the fixed point examples in Section
III
we found

\displaystyle[A^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}({[F^{{\overline{a}}ab}_{b}]_{000}^{c\mu\nu}})^{*}\,,

\displaystyle[B^{ab}_{c}]_{\mu}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{a}d_{b}}{d_{c}}}{[F^{ab{\overline{b}}}_{a}]_{c\mu\nu}^{000}}\,,

(453)

and we suspect that these formulas still hold for the full class of MPOs considered here.

V.3.2
Implications of the
\mathcal{G}
-grading

The existence of a
\mathcal{G}
-grading for the fusion structure implies further restrictions on the algebraic data of an MPO algebra, similar to results shown in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
.

Since each element
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
satisfies
N_{a{\overline{b}}}^{0}=N_{a0}^{b}=N_{0a}^{b}=\delta_{ab}
, for all
{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
there must exist some
{{c}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}},c^{\prime}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
such that
N_{a{\overline{b}}}^{c}=N_{{\overline{c}}a}^{b}\neq 0
, and
N_{{\overline{b}}a}^{c^{\prime}}=N_{a{\overline{c^{\prime}}}}^{b}\neq 0
.
That is, for any single block
\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
, any other single block
\text{MPO}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
appears in the product
\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\text{MPO}_{{\overline{c^{\prime}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}}
and also
\text{MPO}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}\text{MPO}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
for some
{{c}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}},c^{\prime}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.
However, we do not generally have
|\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|=|\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}|
for
{\text{\bf{g}}}\neq{\text{\bf{h}}}
.

The MPO algebras we consider are faithfully
\mathcal{G}
-graded, hence Eq.(
438
) implies that
\mathcal{D}^{2}_{0}=\mathcal{D}^{2}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
for all
g
. The argument for this is identical to that given in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
. Consequently we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}=|\mathcal{G}|\mathcal{D}^{2}_{{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,.

(454)

Hence the MPO projector onto the symmetric subspace in Eq.(
380
) corresponds to the
\omega_{0}
-loop of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, since

\displaystyle\frac{w_{a}}{|\mathcal{G}|}=\frac{d_{a}}{\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\mathcal{G}}}\,.

(455)

The
\omega_{0}
-MPO loop of
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
satisfies an equation, generalizing Eq.(
IV.5
), that allows us to move a
g
-MPO through an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
loop by transforming it to an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
loop. That is,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru0}\end{array}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\\
{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\mu\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{b}d_{s}}{d_{a}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru1}\end{array}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\nu\mu\end{subarray}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{b}d_{s}}{d_{a}}}\,([B^{sa}_{b}]^{\nu}_{\mu})^{*}[B^{sa}_{b}]^{\nu}_{\mu}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru2}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\nu\end{subarray}}d_{b}\sqrt{\frac{d_{s}}{d_{a}d_{b}}}\,\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{{\overline{a}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru5}\end{array}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}}\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}d_{b}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru3}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0pullthru4}\end{array}\,,

where we have used the zipper condition, the pivotal identities, and properties of the FS indicator.
A similar equation holds for a
1
MPO of arbitrary length and with arbitrary orientations of the black indices. By applying orientation reversing gauge transformations,
Z_{g}
, on the open red indices and noting that reversal of the orientation of the
1
MPO has no effect,
\text{MPO}^{-}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
, the equation can also bee seen to hold for all orientations of the red indices. Furthermore, the same argument applies for pulling through an open MPO from any contiguous subregion of a circle to its complement.

V.4
SET tensor network states and the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation

The
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras that were just introduced yield representations of domain walls in SET tensor network states. We proceed to introduce a condition on the local tensors of a PEPS that ensures it is symmetric under an on-site representation of
\mathcal{G}
, and furthermore that the domain walls are described by a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra.

The local tensors of a two dimensional PEPS on a directed trivalent lattice, dual to a triangulation with branching structure, are specified by

\displaystyle(T_{+})^{ijk}_{p}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setPEPStensor0}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle(T_{-})^{ijk}_{p}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setPEPStensor1}\end{array}\,.

We assume that this PEPS is MPO-injective and hence describes a state with some underlying topological order. The MPO w.r.t. which the PEPS is MPO-injective corresponds to
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
in the MPO representation of
\mathcal{G}
.

The PEPS is symmetric under an on-site representation of
\mathcal{G}
if it satisfies the
symmetry-enriched pulling though equation
Williamson et al. 2016b

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setpullthruPEPStensor0LHS}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setpullthruPEPStensor0}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setpullthruPEPStensor1}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{setpullthruPEPStensor1LHS}\end{array}\,.

Where we have used the following graphical notation for the on-site group action

\displaystyle\raisebox{-2.84544pt}{$\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugaction0}\end{array}$}=\,U_{\text{\bf{g}}}\,,

hence

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugPEPStensor0}\end{array}:=\sum_{q}[U_{\text{\bf{g}}}]_{p}^{q}\,(T_{+})^{ijk}_{q}\,.

By the properties of the MPOs described above, Eq.(
V.4
) implies that all the similar pulling through equations, involving
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
acting on a different choice of indices, also hold.
While the tensors considered above can be used to build a PEPS on any triangulation with branching structure. Similar considerations apply for regular lattices, such as the square lattice, or more generally for locally planar directed graphs.

Due to Eqs.(
V.3.1
) and (
V.3.2
), the symmetry-enriched pulling though equation is equivalent to the following group intertwiner property

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugPEPStensor0}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{closedgroupactionPEPStensor0}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugPEPStensor1}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{closedgroupactionPEPStensor1}\end{array}\,,

which we expect to be more practical for numerical purposes.

V.4.1
\omega_{0}
-loop fixed-point tensors

The
\omega_{0}
-loop of
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
,
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
, defines an MPO-injective PEPS tensor
Bultinck et al. 2017a
which we refer to as an MPO fixed-point PEPS tensor

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{PEPStensor0}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0PEPS0}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{PEPStensor1}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{omega0PEPS1}\end{array}\,.

The physical degree of freedom is identified with a subspace of the three MPO indices that enter the circle, given by the support of
\text{MPO}^{L=3}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.
The on-site group action is given by
U_{g}=\text{MPO}^{L=3}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
, which is unitary on the support subspace of
\text{MPO}^{L=3}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
, corresponding to the local physical degree of freedom. The application of the group action to the PEPS tensors results in

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugPEPStensor0}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugomega0PEPS0}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugPEPStensor1}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{ugomega0PEPS1}\end{array}\,.

Hence it follows from Eq.(
V.3.2
) that the MPO fixed-point PEPS tensors in Eq.(
V.4.1
) satisfy the symmetry-enriched pulling through equation (
V.4
).

We remark that this MPO fixed-point construction yields the same PEPS tensor for all
\mathcal{G}
-extensions of an underlying MPO algebra
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
. This same tensor represents different SET phases, depending upon the choice of the on-site symmetry action. A similar phenomenon has previously been noted for the restricted case of SPT phases
Chen et al. 2011b
.

V.5
Abstracting the calculus of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras to diagrams

At this point we have developed the theory of
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras, and seen that they represent
\mathcal{G}
domain walls of symmetric tensor networks.
The characteristics of the domain wall MPOs within a tensor network are topological and do not have a strong dependence on local details, such as the number of sites within a certain region.
In fact, the calculus of these MPOs within a tensor network can be abstracted further by not explicitly keeping track of the underlying lattice. This was described in detail in Eqs.(
II.1.1
) and (
II.2.1
).

To carrying out this abstraction, we first identify the tensor network ground state with the vacuum. This corresponds to the empty diagram

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCbackground1}\end{array}\,.

A loop on top of this background represents an MPO

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{anyonlineonbackground}\end{array}\,,

and the application of the on-site symmetry to all sites within a region is depicted as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{groupactiononbackground}\end{array}\,.

Hence

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{anyonlineonbackground2}\end{array}=d_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTCbackground1}\end{array}\,,

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{anyonlineonbackground}\end{array}=d_{a}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{groupactiononbackground}\end{array}\,.

When passing to the abstract diagrammatic calculus, we switched convention for the colour of MPO loops from red to black.

All of the properties satisfied by the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras that were discussed throughout this section can be carried over to the abstract diagrammatic calculus.
This realizes all consistent fusion diagrams, modulo local relations from the
\mathcal{G}
-graded unitary fusion category, and planar isotopy. In particular, restricting to the
1
-sector recovers the diagrammatic calculus
Joyal and Street 1991
;
Joyal and Street 1993
;
Kitaev 2006
of the unitary fusion category
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.
The full
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
leads to a more general diagrammatic calculus, related to a 2-category, that was recently introduced in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2016
.

In this section we have adapted the theory of MPO algebras, presented in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
, to include a
\mathcal{G}
-grading. These MPOs form a representation of the domain walls of an SET ordered phase. Classifying the emergent SET order that arises from
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras corresponds to grouping them into graded Morita equivalence classes. Two
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebras are equivalent if they give rise to the same symmetry-enriched
\mathcal{G}
-graded double, which is described by a UGxBFC. In the next section we will describe how to construct the symmetry-enriched double, and extract the gauge invariant physical data of the emergent SET.

This contains the classification of the underlying emergent topological order as a subproblem. This emergent topological order is given by the Drinfeld double (or center) of the fusion category corresponding to the MPO algebra and hence is nonchiral. Moreover, this construction can realize all nonchiral topological orders
Davydov et al. 2013a
;
Davydov et al. 2013b
.
The classification is then given by collecting MPO algebras into Morita equivalence classes that lead to the same topological order
Müger 2003
;
Muger 2003
.

It was shown in Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
, and later used in a condensed matter context by Refs.
Cheng et al. 2016
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
, that the symmetry-enriched double construction can realize all non-anomalous symmetry actions on nonchiral emergent anyons in an on-site manner. In the next section we will go further to construct the full SET on the lattice, including the theory of emergent symmetry defects.

VI
The defect tube algebra and emergent symmetry-enriched topological order

In this section we generalize Ocneanu’s tube algebra to an algebra of topological symmetries acting on each nontrivial monodromy
g
-defect, appearing at the termination point of a
g
-domain wall.
We construct tensor network representations of the defect superselection sectors by block diagonalizing each
g
-sector of this defect tube algebra.
Furthermore, we find tensor network representations of the projective group action upon each defect superselection sector.
From these representations we extract physical data of the emergent theory, including the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular
S
and
T
matrices, the permutation action of the symmetry upon the defect sectors, the cohomology class of the projective representation carried by each defect sector, and the topological entanglement entropies of the defect sectors.
We go on to describe a construction of the full
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular U
\mathcal{G}
xBFC of the emergent SET order from the defect ICIs.
In particular, this defect tube algebra construction can be applied to the symmetry-enriched string-net examples of Section
III
.

VI.1
Definition of the dube algebra

Similar to the conventional tube algebra, the defect tube (
dube
) algebra is found by considering the action of MPOs in the
1
-sector upon a puncture at the end of a
g
-domain wall.
For this, we look at a symmetry-twisted cylinder, which is equivalent to a domain wall on a sphere with a puncture at each of its end points.
Again, for simplicity of presentation, we restrict our attention to the case of no fusion multiplicity. The results we find directly generalize to the case with nontrivial fusion multiplicity.

By a slight generalisation of Eq.(
II.1.1
) the
g
-twisted cylinder is spanned by tensor networks that have been closed with an MPO from the
g
-sector

\displaystyle\sum_{{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,\mu}c_{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETpuncturedsphere}\end{array}\,.

We can repeat the analysis that lead to Eq.(
IV.1
) by considering the purely virtual MPO symmetries that act on the punctures, these come from the
1
-sector of the MPO algebra

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETCylinder0}\end{array}=\sum_{{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{q}}{d_{r}d_{s}^{3}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETCylinder1}\end{array}=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\frac{1}{d_{s}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{p}}{d_{r}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETCylinder2}\end{array}\,.

Elements of the dube algebra are defined in the same way as for the tube algebra, except they now split into sectors according to the label of the horizontal MPO

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}:=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTube}\end{array}\,,

corresponding to the minimal tensor network

\displaystyle d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{tensornetworktube}\end{array}\,.

We often refer to elements of the dube algebra simply as dubes
1
1

1

The process of constructing the defect tube algebra from a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra is called
rolling a dube
.
. We remark that there is a gauge freedom in the definition of the dube elements, similar to a 1-cochain.

Hence Eq.(
VI.1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\openone_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}:=\sum_{{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(495)

where
\openone_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
is the identity on the virtual level of the
g
-twisted cylinder tensor network.

The definition of multiplication is given by stacking dubes, and is identical to the one given in Eq.(
IV.1
). Similarly, the definition of Hermitian conjugation is given in Eq.(
IV.1
).
We remark that the
\mathcal{G}
-grading of the MPOs ensures that the dube algebra breaks into
|\mathcal{G}|
orthogonal sectors, each of which is a
C^{*}
algebra.
In particular, the
1
-sector of the dube algebra recovers the tube algebra of
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
.

VI.2
Block diagonalizing sectors of the dube algebra with ICIs

Since the dube algebra breaks up into
|\mathcal{G}|
decoupled
C^{*}
algebras, we can separately block diagonalize each of these sectors.

The sector generated by tubes of the form
\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
is referred to as the
g
-dube algebra. It has dimension

\displaystyle D_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}\delta_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\delta_{{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}^{{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\sum_{\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}D_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\times D_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,,

(496)

where the sum is over the irreducible blocks
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
of the
g
-dube algebra, and
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
is the dimension of the
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
block (consisting of
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\times D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
-matrices).

The ICIs of each
g
-sector are given by Hermitian projectors onto each irreducible
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
block.
The ICIs of the
g
-sector determine the inequivalent superselection sectors for a disc filling a puncture at the termination point of a
g
-domain wall.
The ICIs can be found constructively by following the approach in Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
.
Expressions for the defect ICIs of the
\mathcal{G}
-graded dube algebra for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
can be derived from the ICIs of the tube algebra for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, this is discussed further in Section
VIII
.

The
g
-defect ICIs can be written as a linear combination of dubes from the
g
-sector

\displaystyle\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\end{subarray}}t^{pqrs}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(497)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETICIag}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\end{subarray}}t^{pqrs}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTube}\end{array}\,,

where the coefficients satisfy
t^{pqrs}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\delta_{pr}t^{pqps}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
, as only dubes
\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
with
{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}={{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
can appear in the ICIs.

The
1
-sector of the dube algebra recovers the tube algebra of the underlying fusion category
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
. Hence the emergent topological order is described by
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, see Section
IV
.
The full set of
g
-sector ICIs lead to a description of the emergent SET order, which is denoted
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
. The specifics of this construction, in particular how to extract the physical characteristics of the emergent theory, are described below.

In the mathematics literature
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
is known as a relative center. It was shown in Ref.
Gelaki et al. 2009
that the relative center
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
has a canonical
\mathcal{G}
-crossed braided structure. Our construction realizes this U
\mathcal{G}
xBFC, henceforth referred to as
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
, on the lattice as a tensor network.
Similar to the ungraded case, the crossing tensors of the defect ICIs
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
can be directly interpreted as the simple objects of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
.
We also expect the crossing tensors of these defect ICIs
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
to yield a construction of the defect string operators in the symmetry-enriched Levin-Wen models
Heinrich et al. 2016
;
Cheng et al. 2016
by using them to resolve an external string into the lattice.

More generally, one can define the relative double
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({A})
for an arbitrary
\mathcal{C}
-
\mathcal{C}
-bimodule category
A
. There is an obvious extension of the tube algebra to this case, although it is unclear whether there is any notion of a modified braided structure on
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}}({A})
when
A
is not invertible. In Ref.
Kitaev and Kong 2012
a related extension of the tube algebra for a pair of
\mathcal{C}
-modules was used to find boundary excitations.

For irreducible blocks of dimension
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}>1
the
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
ICI further decomposes into a sum of
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
orthogonal irreducible idempotents
(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ii}
.
Furthermore, there are
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}-1)
nilpotent off diagonal elements
(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}
for
i\neq j

\displaystyle(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}\end{subarray}}t^{pqrs}_{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,.

(500)

These elements span the
D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\times D_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
-matrix algebra of the
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
-block, hence the dubes in the
g
-sector can be expanded in this basis

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}}{\overline{t}}_{pqrs}^{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}\,.

(501)

The columns of this change-of-basis matrices are orthogonal

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}}\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}{\overline{t}}^{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}_{pqrs}({\overline{t}}^{({{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}})_{kl}}_{pqrs})^{*}=\delta_{ab}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\frac{1}{|{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}|^{2}}\,,

(502)

for some positive weights
|{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}|
.

It follows from Eqs.(
495
) and (
502
) that

\displaystyle\openone_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\sum_{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}\frac{1}{|{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}|^{2}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}^{\dagger}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ij}\,,

(503)

where
\openone_{\text{\bf{g}}}
denotes the identity on the virtual level of the
g
-twisted cylinder.
Multiplying by
(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})_{ii}
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\frac{1}{|{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})_{ij}}|^{2}}=1\,,

and hence

\displaystyle\openone_{\text{\bf{g}}}=\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(504)

which is a partition of the identity on the virtual level of the
g
-twisted cylinder by the
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
ICIs.

VI.3
Projective group actions on the defect ICIs

Beyond the topological symmetries acting on each
g
-defect, there is also an action of each element of the physical symmetry group.
This can be found by considering the action of the global symmetry on a
g
-twisted cylinder tensor network

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SEThactionCylinder0}\end{array}=\frac{1}{d_{s}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SEThactionCylinder2}\end{array}=\sum_{{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{q}}{d_{r}d_{s}^{3}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SEThactionCylinder3}\end{array}=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}}\frac{1}{d_{s}^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d_{p}}{d_{r}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SEThactionCylinder4}\end{array}\,,

where the darker shaded region denotes the application of the on-site physical action of
{\text{\bf{h}}}\in\mathcal{G}
.

The action of
h
on a
g
-defect involves tube elements of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}:=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTube1}\end{array}\,.

These tubes match the set of elements of the tube algebra for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, however they are interpreted differently in light of the
\mathcal{G}
-graded structure.

Hence Eq.(
VI.3
) can be written as

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}[\openone_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}]=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}(\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(510)

where
\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}
denotes the action of the global symmetry
{\text{\bf{h}}}\in\mathcal{G}
to the physical level of the tensor network.

The action of an
h
domain wall on an ICI
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
is defined by projecting the collection of tubes with
s\in\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}
onto the ICI

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}:=K\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}c_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{pqrs}\,\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\right)\,\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(511)

for arbitrary constants
c_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{pqrs}\neq 0
, and some normalization
K\neq 0
that depends on the
c_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{pqrs}
.
For simplicity of presentation we ignore some subtleties that may arise in the case of a degenerate block, the analysis presented can still be applied to the degenerate case with some care.
We remark that the RHS of the above equation may be
0
for certain fine-tuned choices of
c_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{pqrs}
, we intend it to hold for generic choices, i.e. after adding a random perturbation to any given fine-tuned values.

The normalization is fixed, up to a multiplicative phase factor, by the unitarity requirement

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}=\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,.

(512)

The domain walls are only defined up to a multiplicative 1-cochain
\varepsilon_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\in\mathsf{U}(1)
. We always work with a choice of 1-cochain such that
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}=\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
and
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{$\text{\bf{g}}$}}}}}_{{0}}=\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
.

The domain walls on each sector can be expanded in the tube basis

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}t^{pqrs}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(513)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETDW}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}t^{pqrs}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTube1}\end{array}\,.

Due to Eqs.(
504
), and (
512
) we have

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}[\openone_{\text{\bf{g}}}]=\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,,

(516)

which corresponds to the intertwining of a physical
h
action to the virtual level by the
g
-twisted cylinder tensor network.

VI.3.1
Permutation action on the defects

The action of the domain wall on the set of defects may result in a nontrivial permutation
\rho_{h}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})={}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})=\underline{{{({}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a)}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}}
.
Which can be found as follows

\displaystyle{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}):=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\,,

or equivalently

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}={}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,.

(517)

To reiterate, the permuted defect satisfies
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})\in\mathcal{C}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}
, and even in the case
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
there may still be a nontrivial permutation action of
h
upon the defects within sector
g
, captured by
\underline{{{({}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a)}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.
In particular, the potentially nontrivial permutation action of the global symmetry
\mathcal{G}
upon the emergent anyons (or
1
-defects) can be extracted.

By definition (
511
), we have
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}=C\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
, for some constant
C
. Hence
\rho
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{G}
to the permutation group on
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}))={}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})\,.

(518)

We remark that the action of the domain walls commutes with the
T
matrix, see Eqs.(
IV.3
), and (
532
). Hence the topological spin of all superselection sectors in an orbit of the permutation action must match.

We refer to the set of all group elements that do not permute a given defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
as its centralizer

\displaystyle{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}:=\{{\text{\bf{h}}}\in\mathcal{G}\,|\,{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}={{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\}\,,

(519)

which is a subgroup of
\mathcal{G}
. Note
{\text{\bf{g}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
as the
T
matrix, see Eq.(
329
), commutes with
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
.

The orbit of a defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
under the
\mathcal{G}
-action is denoted by

\displaystyle{[{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}]}:=\{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,|\,{\text{\bf{h}}}\in\mathcal{G}\}\,,

(520)

and a set of representatives for each right coset of
{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
is written as
\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
, hence

\displaystyle{[{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}]}=\{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,|\,{\text{\bf{h}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\}\,.

(521)

VI.3.2
2-cocycle of the projective representation on a defect

We can go beyond the analysis of the previous section to work out precise composition rules of the domain walls. Since

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\right)\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\left(\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\right)\,,

(522)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\right)^{\dagger}\left(\sum_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\right)=\openone_{\text{\bf{g}}}\,,

(523)

by an application of Schur’s lemma we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}={\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,,

(524)

for some phase
{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}\in\mathsf{U}(1)
. Due to the associativity of matrix multiplication,
\eta_{a}
must satisfy the twisted 2-cocycle equation

\displaystyle{\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}({{\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{fh}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}={\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{hk}}}})}{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}\,.

(525)

Since
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
is only defined up to a multiplicative 1-chain,
{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}
is only defined up to a 2-coboundary and hence each defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
carries a
\rho
-twisted projective representation in cohomology class
[\eta_{a}]\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
.
Furthermore, each defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
transforms under a conventional projective representation of its own centralizer
{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
with cohomology class
{[\eta_{a}]\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({Z}_{a},\mathsf{U}(1))}
.
We assume these cocycles have been normalized such that

\displaystyle{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{1}}}})}={\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}=1\,.

(526)

We remark that a previous work demonstrated how to extract this symmetry fractionalization label for anyons, or
1
-defects, using tensor networks
Huang et al. 2014
.

All defects in a
\mathcal{G}
-orbit
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\in{[{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}]}
have isomorphic centralizer groups
{{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\cong{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
, via the obvious isomorphism
{\text{\bf{k}}}\mapsto{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{k}}}
.
It was shown in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
that the 2-cocycle of the projective rep of
{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}
acting on
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
is related to the projective rep of
{Z}_{a}
acting on
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
by a coboundary

\displaystyle\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}({}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{x}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{y}}})=d\varepsilon_{a}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}})\,\eta_{a}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}})\,,

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{a}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{x}}}):=\frac{\eta_{a}({\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{x}}})}{\eta_{a}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}\,,

(527)

hence
[\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}({}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{x}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{y}}})]=[\eta_{a}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}})]
give the same element in both
\mathcal{H}^{2}({Z}_{a},\mathsf{U}(1))
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}({Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a},\mathsf{U}(1))
.

Furthermore, this gives a canonical isomorphism between the
\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}
-projective irreps of
{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}
and the
\eta_{a}
-projective irreps of
{Z}_{a}
, for a chosen representative
a\in[a]
, as follows

\displaystyle\pi^{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\mu}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}a}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$k}}})=\varepsilon_{a}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\pi^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,.

(528)

Where
\pi_{a}^{\mu}
is an irreducible projective representation of
{Z}_{a}
with cocycle
\eta_{a}
, labeled by
\mu
.

VI.4
Symmetry-twisted minimally entangled states and
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices

The representations of
h
on the
g
-defects
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
yield all the
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})
symmetry-twisted, minimally entangled states
Barkeshli et al. 2014
on the torus

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}=\frac{1}{D_{a}\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}t^{pqps}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\ket{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}}\,.

(529)

Only tubes
\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
with
p=r
lead to nonzero states, and hence nonzero symmetry-twisted sectors must satisfy
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
.
Furthermore, only group elements with nonzero matrix trace
\text{Tr}[\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}]\neq 0
lead to nonzero states.
By using Eq.(
517
), and the orthogonality of defect ICIs, this implies that nonzero symmetry-twisted MES must satisfy
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}={{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
.
Hence the dimension of the
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})
-sector equals the number of
h
-invariant
g
-defects.

Similarly, one can use the crossing tensors from the domain wall tensor networks
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
— containing the weights
t^{pqps}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}
— to construct general symmetry-twisted states on arbitrary oriented 2-manifolds, i.e. higher genus tori.
Here we focus on the torus, as it already allows us to construct the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
and
T
matrices that generate a representation of the modular group.

The action of the global, on-site symmetry upon the symmetry-twisted MES can be calculated by using Eq.(
516
)

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}=\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}}=\frac{{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}{\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}({{\text{\bf{kh}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}})}}{{\eta_{a}({{\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}$h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\,.

(530)

Where, by Eq.(
524
), and Eq.(
525
), we have

{\eta_{a}({{\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}$}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
.

For calculational purposes it is often convenient to derive the permutation action on the defects
{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
directly from the MES. This can be done by intertwining the global
\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}
action on the physical indices to
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}
on the virtual indices, and fusing it into the 1-skeleton of the torus.

For
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{h}
, by Eq.(
512
), we have

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}=\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})=\frac{{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}}{{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}}\,,

(531)

which is the slant product.
This phase is only gauge invariant for
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}
, which is defined to be the elements of
{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
that also commute with
h
. In that case we find a group homomorphism
\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}:{Z}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\rightarrow\mathsf{U}(1)
, due to the 2-cocycle Equation (
525
).

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular
S
and
T
matrices are defined by the same action on tubes as the topological modular matrices, see Eq.(
IV.3
). They satisfy

\displaystyle\ket{S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}S_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\ket{T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}_{{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(532)

where

\displaystyle S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]}\,,

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\delta_{a,b}

\displaystyle=\frac{D_{b}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{{${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}}}}_{{b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]}\,,

(533)

and we have used that
{}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}={{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
, since the
T
matrix tube commutes with
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
, see Eq.(
329
). The trace used above corresponds to the conventional matrix trace.

This includes the special case of the topological spin of defect superselection sectors

\displaystyle\ket{T({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}

\displaystyle=\theta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{a_{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(534)

and due to Eq.(
517
) we have

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}={\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}\theta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,.

(535)

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices are broken down into sectors, outside of which they are identically zero. That is,

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}}}\,|\,S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})}=:\delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{y}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}S^{({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}}}\,|\,T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})}=:\delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{y}}},{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}\delta_{a,b}T^{({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,.

(536)

Since
S
and
T
are clearly invertible by definition, the dimension of the
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})
and
({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})
sectors must match. This implies the number of
h
-invariant
g
-defects equals the number of
g
-invariant
h
-defects, where
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
.
In particular, the number of
g
-defects equals the number of
g
-invariant anyons (
1
-defects).
We also have that the
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})
and
({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{gh}}})
sectors must have the same dimension.

Similar to the case without symmetry, the
S
and
T
matrices are unitary. This is because the emergent theory describes a modular non-chiral topological order that has been enriched by a non-anomalous symmetry, and remarkably it has been shown that
\mathcal{G}
-extensions of modular theories are
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Etingof et al. 2005
;
Barkeshli et al. 2014
.

Unitarity of the
T
matrix follows directly from our definition, and we expect that unitarity of
S
can also be derived in similar fashion to the ungraded tube algebra. However, we will not give such a proof here.

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
and
T
matrices satisfy modular relations, similar to the ungraded case. For technical reasons
Barkeshli et al. 2014
we first rescale
S
by a complex phase
U_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({a,{\overline{a}};0})\in\mathsf{U}(1)
that arises from the group action on the fusion space, see Eq.(
VI.6.1
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}:=[U_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({a,{\overline{a}};0})]^{*}S_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,.

(537)

Since the underlying topological order is a Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, the chiral central charge is trivial, and hence we have the modular relations

\displaystyle(\widetilde{S}T)^{3}=\widetilde{S}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}^{4}=\openone\,.

(538)

One can also verify these formulas by direct manipulation of MPOs, using the definitions of
S
and
T
given in Eq.(
IV.3
).

We remark that
S_{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}
and
\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
are not gauge-invariant, although they do contain gauge invariant data
Barkeshli et al. 2014
. In particular, the
S
and
T
matrices of the underlying topological order are given by
S^{({\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{1}}})}
and
T^{({\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{1}}})}
.

Furthermore, with the normalizations we have fixed for the
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}},\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{0}}
domain walls, we can extract the normalized quantum dimensions of the defects using a similar approach to that which led to Eq.(
333
). The result

\displaystyle d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\frac{d_{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{t}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}0}}{D_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

(539)

follows from

\displaystyle\frac{d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}=S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}0}=\frac{\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{0}})^{\dagger}S(\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\,(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{0}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{0}}\,]}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}{t}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}\frac{\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{0}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqp})]}{D_{a}\text{Tr}[\,\underline{0}\,]}=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\frac{d_{p}{t}_{a}^{ppp0}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}D_{a}}\,,

(540)

where we have used
S(\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})\,\underline{0}=d_{p}\,\delta_{s0}\delta_{pq}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{0}}
, which is implied by Eq.(
VI.6
).

Since we have a
\mathcal{G}
-extension of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, and the total quantum dimension of the
1
-defect topological superselection sectors is given by
\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{2}
, the total quantum dimension of all defects is given by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G},\text{out}}=\sqrt{|\mathcal{G}|}\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}
.

VI.5
Topological entanglement entropy of the defect superselection sectors

The topological entanglement entropy (TEE) of the defect superselection sectors can be calculated via the topological correction to the 0-Rényi entropy, similarly to the purely topological case considered in Section
IV.4
.
The 0-Rényi entropy for the
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
defect superselection sector is calculated by taking the trace of the
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
ICI, of varying MPO length
L
, similar to Eq.(
IV.4
).

By using Eq.(
539
) we recover a similar result to Eq.(
337
)

\displaystyle H_{0}\approx\log(\lambda_{0})L-\gamma_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=-\log\left(\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\frac{d_{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{t}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}0}}{D_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\right)=\log(\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}})-\log(d_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})\,,

(541)

and
\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}
is the quantum dimension of the underlying emergent topological order.

We argued in Section
IV.4
that the topological correction to the entropy is independent of the Rényi index for an RG fixed point model, and that the topological entanglement entropy is a robust quantity throughout a gapped phase of matter. The same arguments apply for the topological correction to the entropy of a defect superselection sector, and hence the quantity
\gamma_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
in Eq.(
541
) does indeed correspond to the TEE of a defect.
Hence Eq.(
541
) provides a general method to calculate the TEE of defect superselection sectors directly on the lattice
Brown et al. 2013
.

VI.6
\mathcal{G}
-graded fusion

The fusion structure of the defect ICIs proceeds almost identically to the purely topological case, explained in Section
IV.5
. We briefly summarize the points of similarity, before moving on to discuss the interplay of fusion with the group action and domain walls, which are aspects that do not occur in the topological case.

We use a similar notation to that established in Section
IV.5

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETTube}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectAnnulus}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{SETICIag}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIAnnulus}\end{array}\,,

where the group elements on the far right indicate the sector of the strings appearing in the definition of
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.

The fusion of defects
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
and
\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
to sector
\underline{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}
is captured by

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube}\end{array}\,,

where

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Nabctube3}\end{array}:=\sum_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}c^{pqr}_{\mu}{d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{q}^{\frac{1}{4}}}{d_{r}^{\frac{1}{4}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectNabctube4}\end{array}\,,

are fusion vertex tensors that lead to nonzero tensor networks.
Due to the structure of the defect ICIs,
c^{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}_{\mu}
contains the constraint
\delta_{gh,k}
and hence the fusion is
\mathcal{G}
-graded.

The fusion space
{V}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
is spanned by states
\mu_{ab}^{c}
in the support subspace of the projector described in Eq.(
IV.5
). This is found using the same approach as in the purely topological case, by creating an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
loop projector between
\underline{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
and
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}},\,\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
and fusing it into the edges surrounding
\mu_{ab}^{c}
.

By taking the trace of the projector in Eq.(
IV.5
) we find
N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
, the dimension of its support subspace.
After using the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
-matrix to expand
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}},\,\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}},\,\underline{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
in a complimentary basis one can derive the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed Verlinde formula, recently introduced in Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014

\displaystyle N_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}^{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}=\sum_{{{x}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}\frac{S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{x}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}S_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{x}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}S^{*}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}{{x}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}}{S_{0{{x}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}}{\eta_{x}({{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}})}\,.

(549)

This holds for any
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular theory, which includes all the theories in our formalism as they are symmetry-enriched Drinfeld centers.

Further properties of defect fusion that follow from a direct application of the methods described in Section
IV.5
are briefly summarized below:

•

If the ICIs
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}},\,\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}},\,\underline{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
are all nondegenerate, there is a unique fusion vertex
\mu_{ab}^{c}
and one can absorb the
\underline{c}
ICI into
\underline{a},\,\underline{b}
and
\mu
, similar to Eq.(
IV.5
).

•

The antiparticle
\underline{{\overline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}
for each
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
is found using the same approach as in the topological case. That is, by finding the ICI
\underline{{\overline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}
that leads to nonzero cup and cap tubes, as defined in Eq.(
IV.5.1
). The
\mathcal{G}
-grading of the fusion implies that
{\overline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
lies in the
{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
-sector.

•

The FS indicator
\varkappa_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
can then be calculated by multiplying a cup and cap tube as in Eq.(
IV.5.1
). This is only gauge invariant for
{\overline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}={{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
, which requires
{\text{\bf{g}}}^{2}=1
, and implies
\varkappa_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\pm 1
.

•

The
F
-symbols of the emergent theory are calculated using the same approach as in the topological case, see Eq.(
IV.5.2
).

Similar to Eq.(
516
) we have

\displaystyle\sum_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation5}\end{array}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,

For the special case
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}=0
we recover a version of Eq.(
V.3.2
) for tubes

\displaystyle\sum_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation8}\end{array}=\sum_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation9}\end{array}\,,

which corresponds to sliding a
g
domain wall over an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
loop, thus transforming it into an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
loop. This is similar to Eq.(
V.3.2
).

Eq.(
VI.6
) follows from the following slight generalization of Eq.(
516
)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\sum_{{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation0}\end{array}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=d_{p}^{\frac{1}{4}}d_{r}^{-\frac{1}{4}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation6}\end{array}\,,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}\\
{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\end{subarray}}(t^{pqrs}_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}})^{*}\,(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{{{p}_{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}$g}}}}}{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}{{r}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})^{\dagger}.

We also have the similar result

\displaystyle\sum_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectICIteleportation7}\end{array}=(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})^{\dagger}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,.

VI.6.1
Group action on the fusion space

The action of the on-site representation of
\mathcal{G}
on the fusion pair-of-pants tensor network can be calculated as follows

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{UactionNabctube}\end{array}=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{UactionNabctube2}\end{array}\,.

Where the darker shaded region indicates the on-site action of
k
on the fusion tensor network, and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectNabctube3}\end{array}:=\sum_{{s}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{4}}{d_{s}^{2}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectNabctube2}\end{array}=\sum_{\nu}[U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})]^{\mu}_{\nu}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectNabctube5}\end{array}\,,

defines the group action
U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})
on the fusion space.

We denote the pair-of-pants tensor network in Eq.(
VI.6
) by
\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
, hence

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]=(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{c}})^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{V}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}}\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{b}}\,.

(561)

Since
\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}
forms a representation and
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}}
forms a projective representation we find that
U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})
forms a projective representation

\displaystyle U_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}({{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}a,{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}b;{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}c})U_{{\text{\bf{y}}}}({a,b;c})={\omega_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}}({a,b;c})}U_{{\text{\bf{xy}}}}({a,b;c})\,.

(562)

Where

\displaystyle{\omega_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}}({a,b;c})}=\frac{{\eta_{c}({{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}})}}{{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}})}{\eta_{b}({{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}})}}\,,

(563)

is a
\mathsf{U}(1)
phase that satisfies the twisted 2-cocycle equation

\displaystyle{\omega_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{yz}}}}({a,b;c})}\,{\omega_{{\text{\bf{y}}},{\text{\bf{z}}}}({a,b;c})}={\omega_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}}}({{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}a,{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}b;{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}c})}\,{\omega_{{\text{\bf{xy}}},{\text{\bf{z}}}}({a,b;c})}\,.

(564)

For
{\text{\bf{x}}},\,{\text{\bf{y}}}\in{Z}_{a,b,c}
we find
[{\omega({a,b;c})}]\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({Z}_{a,b,c},\mathsf{U}(1))
as it is only defined up to a coboundary, corresponding to rephasing
U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})
.

Eq.(
VI.6.1
) included an action of the symmetry within the
\underline{a},\,\underline{b},
and
\underline{c}
superselection sectors.
To this end we consider closing the pair-of-pants tensor network via defect tensors
T_{a}^{-},\,T_{b}^{-},\,T_{c}
, where the
-
indicates that the boundary of the puncture is negatively oriented. The defect tensors must transform under the on-site physical symmetry as follows

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{a}]=T_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T^{-}_{a}]=(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}})^{\dagger}T^{-}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}\,,

(565)

if they are to correctly capture the behaviour of the defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
.
For the special case
T_{a}=\underline{a}
, we have
\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{a}]=\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}}
.

Including the action of the physical symmetry upon the defect superselection sectors yields the following modification of Eq.(
VI.6.1
)

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{c}\,\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,T_{a}^{-}\otimes\openone\otimes T_{b}^{-}]

\displaystyle=\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{c}](\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{c}})^{\dagger}\,\mathcal{V}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{a}}\,\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{a}^{-}]\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{b}}\,\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[T_{b}^{-}]

(566)

\displaystyle=T_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}c}\,\mathcal{V}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,T^{-}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({a,b;c})\otimes T^{-}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}b}\,.

(567)

That is, the physical action of the symmetry compensates the action on each defect sector appearing in Eq.(
VI.6.1
).

VI.7
\mathcal{G}
-crossed braiding

The process of exchanging defects differs from the exchange of anyons, as we must now also move the domain walls attached to the defects, which requires the application of the physical symmetry

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectRabmatrix0}\end{array}\xrightarrow{\text{Exchange}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectRabmatrix1}\end{array}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{{s}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}{d_{s}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectRabmatrix2}\end{array}\,.

During the exchange step we have applied the physical symmetry to the dark shaded region, and to the superselection sector, which resulted in the application of
(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a}})^{\dagger}
to the ICI
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.

For nondegenerate
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
and
\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
we have

\displaystyle R^{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}:=\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectRabmatrix3}\end{array}=\sum_{{{q}_{{\text{\bf{g${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}}}\frac{(t_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a}}}^{s^{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{a}}q\,s^{a}s^{b}})^{*}}{d_{s^{b}}}\,,

where
s_{a}
is defined by the equation
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\mathcal{T}^{0}_{ppp}=\delta_{ps^{a}}\,\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
R
-symbols
{R^{{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{hg}}}}}}}
are defined by resolving the
R
-matrix into defect superselection sectors of definite topological charge. For nondegenerate defect ICIs
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}},\,\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}},\,\underline{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{hg}}}}}},
the fusion vertices
\mu_{ba}^{c}
and
\nu_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}ab}^{c}
are unique, and we have

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{DefectRabc0}\end{array}={R^{{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{hg}}}}}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[]{Rabc1}\end{array}\,,

as part of a larger fusion pair-of-pants tensor network
\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
. An explicit expression for
{R^{{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{hg}}}}}}}
can be derived directly from Eq.(
VI.7
).
These results can be generalized to degenerate defect ICIs by following a similar line of reasoning.

VI.8
Shifting by 2 and 3 cocycles

In Section
V.2
we found that consistent fusion rules for a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra correspond to an
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
torsor, while
F
-symbol associators that satisfy the pentagon equation correspond to
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
-torsors.
Hence one might consider the effect on the emergent theory, of shifting the MPO algebra by an element of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
or
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
.
Shifting the fusion rules by an element of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{Z}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}})
can totally change the structure of the
g
-defect tube algebras for
{\text{\bf{g}}}\neq{\text{\bf{1}}}
. Beyond the
1
-sector, which remains the same, there is no obvious relation between the emergent theories before and after the shift. This is demonstrated in Example
IX.1
.

Shifting the
F
-symbol associators by an element of
\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))
is more subtle, as it preserves the set of emergent defects, but it may change some of their properties. Except for the underlying emergent topological order, corresponding to the
1
-sector, which is left invariant.
Specifically, we consider shifting by a nontrivial cocycle
[\alpha]\in\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))

\displaystyle{F^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}_{def}}\mapsto\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\,{F^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}_{def}}\,,

(574)

which leads to an obvious change in the
F
-symbols of the emergent theory, via Eq.(
IV.5.2
).
The effect on the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
R
matrices can also be derived.

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices are modified as follows

\displaystyle S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\mapsto\varkappa_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{*}\,\alpha^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})^{*}\frac{\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}{\alpha({\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}\,S_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\mapsto\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})^{*}\frac{\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}{\alpha({\text{\bf{gh}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{gh}}})}\,\theta_{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,.

(575)

The effects of the cocycle shift on the physically observable data are more apparent after gauging the symmetry, see Section
VII
. These effects are largely a consequence of the following transformations.

The Frobenius-Schur indicator transforms as follows

\displaystyle\varkappa_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\mapsto\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})\,\varkappa_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,.

(576)

The 2-cocycle of the projective representation acting on the
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
defect transforms as

\displaystyle{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}\mapsto\omega_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\,{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}\,,

(577)

where

\displaystyle\omega_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\,d\varepsilon^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}})\,,

\displaystyle\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}{\alpha({}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})}\,,

(578)

\displaystyle d\varepsilon^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\varepsilon^{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}{\varepsilon^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{hk}}})}\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}})

\displaystyle=\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\,.

(579)

Hence the group action on the symmetry-twisted MES becomes

\displaystyle\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\mapsto\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,,

where

\displaystyle\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})=\frac{\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}{\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}\,,

(580)

and
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
, with
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
.
Finally the group action on the fusion vertices transforms as follows

\displaystyle U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}})\mapsto\omega_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}})\,,

(581)

for
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
.

We remark that shifting by a 3-cocycle cannot, in general, be absorbed by a change of gauge of the MPO data.
For instance it is apparent that the projective representations acting on the defects are shifted when the 2-cocycle
\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
lies in a nontrivial cohomology class.
Hence one must assess whether shifting by a 3-cocycle actually changes the emergent UGxBFC on a case by case basis.
In Section
VIII
we present some examples for which a 3-cocycle shift can be absorbed by a change of gauge, and some for which it cannot.

VII
Gauging a global symmetry

In this section, we describe the effect of applying the state gauging procedure — developed in Refs.
Haegeman et al. 2015a
;
Williamson et al. 2016b
— to SET tensor networks.
This corresponds to equivariantization in the category theory language used in Refs.
Turaev 2000
;
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Etingof et al. 2009
;
Gelaki et al. 2009
.
Our analysis focuses on the transformation of the
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry algebra. Gauging effectively promotes the nontrivial
g
-sectors into purely topological symmetries.
From this we derive formulas for the the ICIs of the gauged topological order.
These gauged ICIs lead to expressions for the resulting
S
and
T
matrices, as well as the fusion degeneracies and quantum dimensions.
Before addressing the general case, we focus on a simple example that captures many features of the gauging procedure.

VII.1
Example: Gauging SPT orders

We begin by applying the gauging procedure to the simplest class of examples where a unitary, on-site SPT order is gauged to a twisted quantum double (Dijkgraaf-Witten) topological order
Levin and Gu 2012
;
Williamson et al. 2016b
;
Kitaev 2003
;
Dijkgraaf and Witten 1990
.

VII.1.1
MPO description of an SPT order

We consider a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra with
|\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}|=1
. It was shown in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
that this corresponds to a non-chiral, invertible topological order. Furthermore it was shown that
|\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}|=1,\,\forall{\text{\bf{g}}}\in\mathcal{G},
for a faithful MPO representation with
|\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}|=1
.

In this case the single block MPOs are simply labeled by group elements
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
, and their fusion is given by the group product
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}=\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}
.
Consequently, the associators are simply
\mathsf{U}(1)
phases
