{{F^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{\text{\bf{ghk}}},{\text{\bf{gh}}},{\text{\bf{hk}}}}}=\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}
that satisfy the 3-cocycle equation

\displaystyle\alpha({\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\alpha({\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{gh}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})=\alpha({\text{\bf{fg}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\alpha({\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{hk}}})\,,

(582)

due to the pentagon equation.
The algebraic data of the MPO algebra is described by the UFC
\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}
, considered as a
\mathcal{G}
-graded category. The classification of such MPO algebras recovers the third cohomology classification of SPTs
Chen et al. 2011b
;
Chen et al. 2012
;
Else and Nayak 2014
. In this case the
\mathcal{G}
-graded Morita equivalence class is given by a representative, up to a 1-cochain, since each sector consists of only one single block MPO.
Fixed point representatives of these SPT phases are captured by a special case of the
\mathcal{G}
-graded string-net example, see Section
III
.

The significance of the fact that
F
-symbol solutions are torsors can be understood in this case as follows: the classification of SPT phases is dependent upon the precise choice of physical symmetry, with respect to which the classification is done. As an explicit example, the well known CZX model
Chen et al. 2011b
is in the trivial phase w.r.t. an on-site
X^{\otimes N}
symmetry, but is in a nontrivial phase for the more complicated on site symmetry chosen in Ref.
Chen et al. 2011b
.

Since
|\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}|=1
the dube algebras are all one dimensional. There is a single defect ICI for each sector, which is simply given by
\underline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\mathcal{T}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}}
.

When considering the group action on the defects we use the following choice of gauge

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}=\alpha^{*}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\mathcal{T}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}}=:\mathcal{T}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,.

(583)

The permutation action is simply given by group conjugation, i.e.
{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\underline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\underline{{\text{\bf{hg${\overline{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}$}}}}
.

Multiplication of domain walls is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}=\frac{\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}{\alpha({}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\alpha({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})}\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{hk}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,,

hence

\displaystyle{\eta_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}=\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})\,.

(584)

The group action of
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{h}
on the MES is given by

\displaystyle\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}=\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})=\frac{\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})}{\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})}\,.

(585)

The
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices are given by

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}\,|\,S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}=\delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{y}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,\varkappa_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{*}\,\alpha^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})^{*}\,,

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{x}}}}}\,|\,T(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})}=\delta_{{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{g}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{y}}},{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})^{*}\,.

(586)

Fusion of the defect ICIs is again given by the group multiplication rule, and it is multiplicity free. Hence the group action on the fusion space is simply a
\mathsf{U}(1)
phase, which is only defined up to multiplication with a 1-cocycle. One can verify that the following is a valid choice
Bultinck et al. 2017a

\displaystyle U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}};{\text{\bf{gh}}}})=\alpha^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\,.

(587)

Even though
U
is a phase in this case, it cannot generally be absorbed into the gauge of the projective representation
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
as it is a function of three group variables.

The
F
-symbols of the defects are given by the 3-cocycle
\alpha
of the underlying MPO algebra. Hence the FS indicators of the defects are given by
\varkappa_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}=\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{\text{\bf{g}}})
.

VII.1.2
Gauging the global symmetry of an SPT order

It was shown in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
that applying the lattice gauging procedure introduced in Ref.
Haegeman et al. 2015a
, for the full symmetry group
\mathcal{G}
, results in a tensor network with an MPO algebra where the
\mathcal{G}
-grading is essentially forgotten. That is, the
1
-sector of the resulting MPO algebra is given by the full
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra of the ungauged model. The algebraic data describing this is again the UFC
\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}
, but without including the
\mathcal{G}
-grading. Hence the emergent topological order is
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}})
, which is a twisted quantum double model.
See Section
III
for a description of the gauging map applied to the fixed point PEPS tensors.

After gauging, the former domain walls
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
become purely virtual operators that do not require a physical group action. These MPOs can now be thought of as fluctuating in the vacuum given by the tensor network representation of the ground state.

This produces a tube algebra for
\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}
from the dube algebra of the
\mathcal{G}
-graded
\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}
, together with the former domain walls
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
.
Hence all the sectors in a conjugacy class
{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
of the dube algebra are coupled by the domain wall tubes
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
and result in a
|{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|\times|\mathcal{G}|
block of the gauged tube algebra.

An individual defect
g
supports a projective representation of
{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
with 2-cocycle
\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
. This contributes a block of size
|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|
to the gauged tube algebra, which is block-diagonalized by idempotents that project onto the projective irreps
\pi^{\mu}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}

\displaystyle\underline{{({\text{\bf{g}}},\mu)}}:=\frac{d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,,

(588)

where
{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}}):=\text{Tr}[\pi^{\mu}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}})]^{*}
is a complex conjugated character.
Each
\underline{{({\text{\bf{g}}},\mu)}}
projects onto a block of size
d_{\mu}
, and since

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}d_{\mu}^{2}=|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|\,,

(589)

we see that the decomposition is complete.

These irreducible idempotents can be extended to the full
|{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|\times|\mathcal{G}|
block, by summing over the orbit of
({\text{\bf{g}}},\mu)
under conjugation

\displaystyle\underline{{[{\text{\bf{g}}},\mu]}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\underline{{({}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\mu)}}\,,

(590)

where the canonical isomorphism between projective irreps, given in Eq.(
528
), is used to define
\underline{{({}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\mu)}}
.
The resulting
\underline{{[{\text{\bf{g}}},\mu]}}
are ICIs that project onto
d_{\mu}\times|{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|
dimensional blocks, and by counting one can verify that they give a complete decomposition.

After gauging, some states on the torus vanish. As discussed in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
only those states
\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
satisfying
\alpha^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\equiv 1
survive the gauging process.

The gauged
S
and
T
can be derived from the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices, leading to the formulas

\displaystyle S^{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}})}_{{[{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\end{subarray}}\delta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}$h}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}})\,\varkappa_{\,{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{*}\,\alpha^{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}({}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}})\,,

\displaystyle T^{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\alpha}})}_{{[{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}=\delta_{{{[{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}}\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})},

(591)

where
{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}\chi_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{\mu}:=\chi_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\mu}
.
From the special case of the
S
matrix with
{[{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}={[{\text{\bf{1}}},1]}
, we find

\displaystyle d_{[{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}=\frac{|\mathcal{G}|d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}|}\,.

(592)

The fusion multiplicities can be derived from
S
via the Verlinde formulas, but they can also be obtained directly by considered the intertwining property of a defect pair-of-pants tensor network

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[\mathcal{V}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}]=\mathcal{V}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}^{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,\alpha^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{g}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{h}}\,,

(593)

for
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
. After gauging there is no global group action
\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}
remaining, hence the projector onto the
\pi^{\kappa}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})
projective rep is intertwined by the gauged pair-of-pants tensor network as follows

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\kappa}}{|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\kappa}_{{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}\mapsto\frac{d_{\kappa}}{|{Z}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\kappa}_{{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\alpha^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,,

(594)

which projects onto the diagonal
\pi^{\kappa}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\big|_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}}
irrep within
{\left((\alpha^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}})\,\pi^{\mu}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\otimes\pi^{\nu}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\right)\big|_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
.
To calculate the fusion multiplicity one must also sum over conjugacy classes
{C}_{g},\,{C}_{h},
and
{C}_{gh}
. This is implemented by summing over conjugates
({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}})
, where
{\text{\bf{x}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{g},\,{\text{\bf{y}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{h}
, modulo left multiplication
({\text{\bf{zx}}},{\text{\bf{zy}}})
, since that leads to conjugation
{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}})
which remains within
{C}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}g{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}h}
. The set of elements
\mathcal{G}/{C}_{g}\times\mathcal{G}/{C}_{h}
, modulo left multiplication, is known to be isomorphic to the double coset
{Z}_{g}\backslash\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{h}
.

After including the summations over conjugacy classes we find that the fusion multiplicity is given by

\displaystyle N_{{{[{\text{\bf{g}}},\mu]}}{{[{\text{\bf{h}}},\nu]}}}^{{{[{\text{\bf{k}}},\kappa]}}}=\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}})\in{Z}_{g}\backslash\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{h}\\
{\text{\bf{z}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{k}\end{subarray}}\delta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{\text{\bf{k}}}}\ m\left({}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}\pi^{\kappa}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}(\cdot)\Big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}},\ \alpha^{({\cdot})}({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}})\Big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\pi^{\mu}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}(\cdot)\Big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\otimes\,{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}\pi^{\nu}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}(\cdot)\Big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\right)\,,

(595)

where

\displaystyle m(\pi,\rho)=\dim\left[\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(\pi,\rho)\right]\,,

(596)

counts the dimension of the space of intertwiners between the
\pi
and
\rho
reps. For the special case of
\pi
an irrep. it counts the number of times
\pi
appears in the irrep decomposition of
\rho
. For a further discussion of the
m
function, and how it can be computed in terms of projective characters, see Ref.
Barkeshli et al. 2014
.
In Ref.
Bultinck et al. 2017a
this multiplicity was evaluated explicitly in terms of projective characters

\displaystyle N_{{{[{\text{\bf{g}}},\mu]}}{{[{\text{\bf{h}}},\nu]}}}^{{{[{\text{\bf{k}}},\kappa]}}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{x}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,\sum_{{\text{\bf{y}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,\sum_{{\text{\bf{z}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}\,\delta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{\text{\bf{k}}}}\sum_{{\text{\bf{w}}}\in{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,\alpha^{{\text{\bf{w}}}}({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\text{\bf{h}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}({\text{\bf{w}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\nu}_{{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}({\text{\bf{w}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}\chi^{\kappa}_{{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}({\text{\bf{w}}})\,.

(597)

VII.2
The general case

Gauging a global symmetry can be implemented explicitly on a tensor network state using the prescription of Ref.
Haegeman et al. 2015a
. It was shown in Ref.
Williamson et al. 2016b
that gauging a symmetric tensor network results in the removal of the
\mathcal{G}
-grading from the MPO algebra. That is, the
1
-sector of the gauged MPO algebra consists of all sectors of the ungauged
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra.
The application of the gauging map to the fixed point SET string-net tensors is described explicitly in Section
III
.

Since the
1
-sector of the gauged MPO algebra is
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, the emergent superselection sectors are given by
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
and can be constructed via the tube algebra.
The total quantum dimension is given by
\mathcal{D}_{\text{out}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}=|\mathcal{G}|\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}
, see Eq.(
454
).

Rather than calculating the gauged ICIs directly with the tube algebra, we can derive them from the defect ICIs
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
and the domain walls
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
of the dube algebra. In this section, and the next, we demonstrate that the problem of finding the gauged ICIs is equivalent to finding the ungauged defect ICIs and domain walls.

In Ref.
Gelaki et al. 2009
the authors show that
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
is canonically equivalent to a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariantization of the relative center
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
. Physically
\mathcal{G}
-equivariantization corresponds to gauging a global
\mathcal{G}
symmetry, and hence our results can be understood as a tensor network realization of the theorem in Ref.
Gelaki et al. 2009
.

After gauging, the former domain walls
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
are now included in the tube algebra.
Hence the sectors of the dube algebra within a conjugacy class
{C}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
are coupled by the domain walls
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}
, and contribute a block to the resulting tube algebra.

The projective representation of
{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
, acting on each
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
defect, was constructed in Section
VI
. Idempotents of the gauged theory are given by Hermitian projectors onto projective irreps
\pi^{\mu}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
with 2-cocycle
\eta_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}

\displaystyle\underline{{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu)}}:=\frac{d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{h}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\,.

(598)

The projector
\underline{{({{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu)}}
correspond to the identity on a block of dimension
d_{\mu}\times D_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
.

The ICIs are then found by summing over the orbit of the defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
under the group action

\displaystyle\underline{{[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu]}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\underline{{({}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\mu)}}\,,

(599)

which project onto blocks of dimension
d_{\mu}\times D_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\times|\mathcal{G}|/|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|
.
Similar to the SPT example
Williamson et al. 2016b
, only states satisfying
\eta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\equiv 1
lead to nonzero gauged states.

The
S
and
T
matrices of the gauged theory can be calculated in terms of the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed modular matrices by using Eqs.(
330
),(
532
),(
599
)

\displaystyle S_{ab}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{b}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{f}}}\in{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\\
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\end{subarray}}\frac{D_{[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},\nu]}\,d_{\mu}d_{\nu}\,{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{f}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{f}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})]}{D_{[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu]}\,|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|\,|{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}|\,\text{Tr}\underline{{[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},\mu]}}}

(600)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{b}\end{subarray}}\frac{D_{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,d_{\nu}^{2}\,{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}${$\text{\bf{h}}$}}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}})\,\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}${{$\text{\bf{h}}$}}}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})]}{D_{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,|{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}|^{2}\,\text{Tr}\underline{{[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},\mu]}}}

(601)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{b}\end{subarray}}\frac{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}${$\text{\bf{h}}$}}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}})\,S_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}{|\mathcal{G}|}\,.

(602)

Where we have used that
\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})^{\dagger}S(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{f}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}})]
contains the delta condition
\delta_{{\text{\bf{k}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\delta_{{\text{\bf{f}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}}
, and

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\underline{{[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},\nu]}}=\sum_{{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}\frac{d_{\nu}}{|{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\nu}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\text{Tr}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})=\frac{|\mathcal{G}|d_{\nu}^{2}}{|{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}|^{2}}\text{Tr}(\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}})\,,

(603)

which follows from
{\overline{\chi}}^{\nu}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})=d_{\nu}
,
\text{Tr}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})=\delta_{{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{1}}}}\text{Tr}(\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}})
and
\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}=\underline{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
.

Since it was shown in Eq.(
328
) that the
T
matrix simply results in a phase, it can be calculated by keeping track of the relative phase of the underlying defect ICI
\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}

\displaystyle T(\underline{{[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu]}})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}},{{s}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}\mathcal{T}^{s}_{s0s}\right)\frac{d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|}\left({\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})\,\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}+\dots\right)

(604)

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{\mu}}{|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|}\left({\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\left(\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\right)^{*}\,\theta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{g}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}+\dots\right)

(605)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\,\theta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,\underline{{[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},\mu]}}\,,

(606)

where we have used that
\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\in\mathsf{U}(1)
.

Hence we have
Barkeshli et al. 2014

\displaystyle S^{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})}_{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\text{\bf{r}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{a}\\
{\text{\bf{s}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{b}\end{subarray}}{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}$h}}})\,{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}\chi^{\nu}_{{b}}({\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}$${\overline{\text{g}}}$}}})\,S_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle T^{\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})}_{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}}}\frac{\chi^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{g}}})}{\chi^{\mu}_{{a}}({\text{\bf{1}}})}\theta_{a}\,.

(607)

These quantities are gauge invariant, as the variation of
\theta_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
and
S_{{}^{{\text{\bf{r}}}}a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{s}}}}b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}
under a 2-coboundary is canceled by that of
\chi^{\mu}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{g}}})
. In particular, the topological spins of the anyons (
1
-defects), after gauging, remain the same.
Setting
{[b_{{\text{\bf{h}}}},\nu]}={[0,0]}
in Eq.(
607
) we find the quantum dimensions of the gauged superselection sectors to be

\displaystyle d_{{[a_{{\text{\bf{g}}}},\mu]}}=\frac{|\mathcal{G}|d_{\mu}d_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}{|{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}|}\,.

(608)

The fusion multiplicities can be derived form the
S
matrix, calculated in Eq.(
607
), by using the Verlinde formula. It is also possible to derive them directly, by using the intertwining property of the pair-of-pants tensor network

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}\,\text{\bf{U}}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}[\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}]=\mathcal{V}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}\,,

(609)

for
{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
.
After gauging, there is no longer a physical group action, and hence the projector onto the
\pi^{\kappa}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}
rep is intertwined by the gauged pair-of-pants fusion tube as follows

\displaystyle\frac{d_{\kappa}}{|{Z}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\kappa}_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}\mapsto\frac{d_{\kappa}}{|{Z}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}|}\sum_{{\text{\bf{k}}}\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}{\overline{\chi}}^{\kappa}_{{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}({\text{\bf{k}}})\,\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}})\otimes\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}_{{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}}\,.

(610)

This projects onto the
\pi^{\kappa}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}\big|_{{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}
rep within
\left(\pi^{\mu}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\,\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{$(\,\cdot\,)$}}}}({{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}})\,\otimes\pi^{\nu}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\right)\big|_{{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}}}
.
To calculate the fusion multiplicity one must also include a summation over defect orbits
[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}],

[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}],

[{{c}_{{\text{\bf{gh}}}}}]
.
We implement this by summing over pairs
({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}})
, for
{\text{\bf{x}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}},\,{\text{\bf{y}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}
, modulo left multiplication
({\text{\bf{zx}}},{\text{\bf{zy}}})
, since that leads to defects
{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}({}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\times{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}})
in the same orbit. Hence

\displaystyle N_{{{[{{a}_{{\text{\bf{ g}}}}},\mu]}}{{[{{b}_{{\text{\bf{ h}}}}},\nu]}}}^{{{[{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}},\kappa]}}}=

(611)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{y}}})\in{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\backslash\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\\
{\text{\bf{z}}}\in\mathcal{G}/{Z}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}\end{subarray}}\delta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{ h}}}}}}^{{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}\ m\left({}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}\pi^{\kappa}_{{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}\big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}\pi^{\mu}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}}\,\otimes U_{{\text{\bf{$(\,\cdot\,)$}}}}({{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}};{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}})\big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}}\,\otimes{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}\pi^{\nu}_{{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}}\big|_{{Z}_{{}^{{\text{\bf{x}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{y}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}},{}^{{\text{\bf{z}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}}\right)\,,

where
m
is the projective rep multiplicity counting function defined in Eq.(
596
).

VIII
Anyon condensation phase transitions dual to gauging

In this section, we study the
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
anyon condensation phase transition induced by breaking a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry down to the
1
-sector subalgebra
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
;
Garre-Rubio et al. 2017
. This corresponds to de-equivariantization in the category theory language used in Ref.
Turaev 2000
;
Kirillov Jr 2004
;
Etingof et al. 2009
;
Gelaki et al. 2009
.
These phase transition are dual to those induced by gauging a global
\mathcal{G}
symmetry, which were studied in the previous section.
We recount how symmetry breaking perturbations to a local tensor lead to tensor network representations of an anyon condensate
Chen et al. 2010a
;
Haegeman et al. 2015b
;
Shukla et al. 2016
. Furthermore, we present a decomposition of these perturbations into topological charge sectors that identify which anyons are condensed by a given perturbation.
A procedure to extract the defect superselection sectors and domain walls of the condensed theory from the topological superselection sectors of the original theory is explained. This allows one to identify which anyons split, which are identified, and which are confined during the condensation phase transition, as well as the full U
\mathcal{G}
xBFC description of the resulting SET.
We also outline how different — Morita equivalent — MPO algebras, that lead to the same emergent topological order, can be used to find different anyon condensation phase transitions. It is conjectured that this approach will recover all possible boson condensation transitions from a given nonchiral topological order.

VIII.1
MPO symmetry breaking perturbations and anyon condensation

We first explain how anyon condensation phase transitions, induced by a reduction of the topological MPO symmetry algebra, can be analyzed with superselection sector ICIs. The reduction of the topological MPO symmetry algebra can either originate from explicit symmetry breaking
Chen et al. 2010a
;
Shukla et al. 2016
;
Garre-Rubio et al. 2017
, due to a non-symmetric perturbation to the local tensor, or from spontaneous symmetry breaking
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
;
Haegeman et al. 2015b
;
Mariën et al. 2016
, where the local tensor is varied symmetrically until the boundary theory undergoes a phase transition.

For the case of explicit symmetry breaking, it was argued in Ref.
Shukla et al. 2016
that perturbations to the local tensor can be classified according to the MPO symmetry they respect.
The perturbations that are not even symmetric under the vacuum
\text{MPO}_{0}
do not change the tensor network, while perturbations respecting
\text{MPO}_{0}
lead to fluctuating anyons on top of the tensor network ground state vacuum. The full MPO algebra symmetry of the unperturbed tensor network implies that the fluctuating anyons satisfy a global charge constraint.

The perturbations respecting
\text{MPO}_{0}
can be further divided into sectors corresponding to the subset of irreducible idempotents of the tube algebra that come attached to a vacuum string
\text{MPO}_{0}
.
The set of ICI sectors on which a perturbation has support identify the emergent anyons that it condenses.

We denote the set of irreducible idempotents that can be realized at the end of a vacuum
\text{MPO}_{0}
string by
\{\underline{a_{0}}\}
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\underline{a_{0}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{s}t^{0s0s}_{a_{0}}\,\mathcal{T}^{s}_{0s0}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{s}t^{0s0s}_{a_{0}}\,\text{MPO}_{s}\,.

(612)

Each
\underline{a_{0}}
corresponds to an internal state of a topological superselection sector satisfying
\underline{a}=(\underline{a_{0}}+\dots)\,
.
In particular the vacuum sector
\underline{0}
always appears in this set of idempotents that occur at the end of
\text{MPO}_{0}
.

Due to Eq.(
323
) any tensor perturbation
V
can be partitioned into a sum over
\underline{a_{0}}
sectors

\displaystyle V=V_{\times}+\sum_{a_{0}}V_{a_{0}}\,,

(613)

where
V_{\times}
is orthogonal to
\text{MPO}_{0}
and hence leads to a zero state, while
V_{a_{0}}
satisfies
V_{a_{0}}\underline{a_{0}}=V_{a_{0}}
, and hence lies in the
\underline{a}
charge sector. The perturbations
V_{0}
, satisfying
V_{0}\,\underline{0}=V_{0}
, are symmetric under the full MPO algebra.

Let
\ket{\Psi[T^{(i)}]}
denote the tensor network resulting from the contraction of tensors
T^{(i)}
on every site
i
of some lattice. We assume the local tensor is symmetric under the full MPO algebra i.e.
T\,\underline{0}=T
.
A perturbed tensor network is given by

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi[(T+\varepsilon_{\times}V_{\times}+\sum_{a_{0}}\varepsilon_{a_{0}}V_{a_{0}})^{(i)}]}=\ket{\Psi[T]}

\displaystyle+\sum_{a_{0}}\varepsilon_{a_{0}}\sum_{v}\ket{\Psi[T^{(i\neq v)},V_{a_{0}}^{(v)}]}

\displaystyle+\sum_{a_{0},b_{0}}\varepsilon_{a_{0}}\varepsilon_{b_{0}}\sum_{u,v}\ket{\Psi[T^{(i\neq u,v)},V_{a_{0}}^{(u)},V_{b_{0}}^{(v)}]}+\dots\,,

(614)

where
i,u,v
are lattice sites. We remark that the first order perturbations, aside from
V_{0}
, generically contribute a zero state for a closed manifold due to a global charge constraint.
The resulting tensor network states can be interpreted as anyon condensates, as they involve a fluctuation of the charges
a_{0}
with
\varepsilon_{a_{0}}\neq 0
on top of the tensor network ground state vacuum.

We expect the case of spontaneous MPO symmetry breaking to follow a similar picture. That is, once the phase transition has been crossed the physical state becomes a cat (GHZ) state given by a sum of explicit MPO symmetry breaking tensor networks, each of which can be interpreted as an anyon condensate.

VIII.2
Extracting the SET order that results from
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
anyon condensation

Due to the additional structure appearing in the ICIs of a tube algebra derived from a graded MPO algebra
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
, it is possible to infer the defect ICIs and domain walls of the
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
dube algebra from the anyon ICIs of the
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
tube algebra. This procedure allows us to calculate the effect of condensing a bosonic
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
subtheory of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
that induces a phase transition to
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
. While the anyon theory that results from this condensation transition is known
Barkeshli et al. 2014
to be
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
, which can simply be constructed by finding the ICIs of the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
tube algebra, we go beyond this to explicitly derive the full SET defect theory
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
. In particular we calculate which ICIs split, which become identified, and which become confined defect ICIs during the condensation phase transition
Bais and Slingerland 2009
. The resulting dube ICIs and domain walls can be used to construct the full U
\mathcal{G}
xBFC description of the resulting SET, by following the procedure explained in Section
VI
.

We assume that the MPO algebra
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
of the model under consideration admits a
\mathcal{G}
-grading. Hence there is a group element assigned to each of the single block MPOs
\lfloor{a}\rfloor\in\mathcal{G}
.
The analysis of Refs.
Chen et al. 2010a
;
Shukla et al. 2016
can be extended to the case where the full virtual MPO symmetry
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
is broken down to a nontrivial residual MPO symmetry, given by the
1
-sector subalgebra
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
.
This corresponds to an anyon condensation transition from
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
to a nontrivial topological phase
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
.
To calculate the relationship between the
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
theory and the condensate
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
we examine the implications of a
\mathcal{G}
-grading for the
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
tube algebra ICIs.
For an ICI of the
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
tube algebra

\displaystyle\underline{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}t^{pqps}_{a}\,\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqp}\,,

(615)

we can separate out sectors according to the group elements
\lfloor{p}\rfloor
and
\lfloor{s}\rfloor

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{pqs}\delta_{\lfloor{p}\rfloor,{\text{\bf{g}}}}\,\delta_{\lfloor{s}\rfloor,{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,t^{pqps}_{a}\,\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqp}\,,

(616)

we must have
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
for a nonzero
(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}
.

Each ICI
\underline{a}
can be written as a sum of subidempotents

\displaystyle\underline{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\in{C}}\,{{(\underline{a})}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,,

where

\displaystyle{{(\underline{a})}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}:=\sum_{{\text{\bf{h}}}\in{Z}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,

(617)

for some conjugacy class
{C}
.

Since the MPO symmetry has been broken down to
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
, the MPOs from the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{g}}}
sectors for
{\text{\bf{g}}}\neq{\text{\bf{1}}}
now correspond to immobile domain walls in the tensor network. Hence, tubes
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
with
\lfloor{s}\rfloor\neq 1
should be dropped from the tube algebra. Furthermore, the remaining tubes
\mathcal{T}^{s}_{pqr}
generate a
\mathcal{G}
dube algebra, that is graded by the sector
\lfloor{p}\rfloor=\lfloor{q}\rfloor=\lfloor{r}\rfloor
.
By dropping the immobile domain walls from the topological symmetry algebra, the subidempotents of an ICI
\underline{a}
are mapped as follows

\displaystyle{{(\underline{a})}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}\,\mapsto\,(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\,.

(618)

The resulting idempotents
(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
are attached to an MPO string in the
g
-sector. For
{\text{\bf{g}}}\neq{\text{\bf{1}}}
the idempotents correspond to confined defects, as they are connected to a physically observable
g
-domain wall that is detected by the local terms of the PEPS parent Hamiltonian
Pérez-García et al. 2008
, and hence the energy penalty of a defect pair scales with their separation.

For a nontrivial conjugacy class
|{C}|>1
the ICI
\underline{a}
splits into multiple defects

\displaystyle\underline{a}\,\mapsto\,\{\,K\,(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\,\}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}\in{C}}\,,

(619)

for some positive constant normalization
K>0
such that
K(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
is a projector.
We can also calculate which ICIs condense into the same defect, by looking for all
\underline{a}
and
\underline{b}
that satisfy

\displaystyle(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}=\widetilde{K}\,(\underline{b})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\neq 0\,,

(620)

for some positive constant
\widetilde{K}
.

The centralizer
{Z}_{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
of a defect
{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}
is given by the set of group elements
h
that lead to a nonzero
(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}
, and the orbit of a defect
[{(\underline{{a}})}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}_{\text{\bf{g}}}]
is given by the set of defects
(\underline{a})_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
that are nonzero.

The
(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
idempotents give the anyon ICIs of the condensed topological order. The procedure explained above allows one to determine the splitting and condensation relations between the anyons of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
and those of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}})
.
In particular, we can calculate the anyons that condense into the vacuum

\displaystyle\underline{{{0}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}=\sum_{s}\delta_{\lfloor{s}\rfloor,{\text{\bf{1}}}}\,\frac{d_{s}}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{1}}}^{2}}\,\mathcal{T}^{s}_{0s0}\,,

(621)

by looking for those
\underline{a}
that satisfy
(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}^{{\text{\bf{1}}}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\underline{{{0}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}}}
.
For condensation induced by symmetry breaking down to the
1
-sector of a
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO algebra, the ICIs that condense to vacuum are precisely given by
\underline{{[{{0}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}},\mu]}}
, see Eq.(
599
). These superselection sectors correspond to bosonic
\mathcal{G}
-charges, which generate a
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
subtheory of
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
that condenses to yield the resulting SET
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}}({\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}})
.

Hence symmetry breaking perturbations to the local tensor can be divided into irreps, or
\mathcal{G}
-charges,

\displaystyle V=\varepsilon_{\times}V_{\times}+\sum_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\mu}V_{\mu}\,,

(622)

similar to Eq.(
613
).
When a tensor network is built from a symmetric local tensor
T
with such a perturbation
V
— for
\varepsilon_{\mu}\neq 0
— the
\mathcal{G}
-charges fluctuate and induce a
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
anyon condensation, see Eq.(
VIII.1
).

To construct a tensor network for the condensed SET, where the symmetry is realized on-site, we can modify the local tensor with a perturbation that is correlated to a newly introduced physical group variable

\displaystyle T\,\mapsto\,\sum_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}\ket{{\overline{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}\otimes(T+V_{0}\,\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}})\,.

(623)

One can verify that this tensor has the correct symmetry transformation under a physical group action, given by the left regular representation, acting on the newly introduced variable. This corresponds to ungauging a
\mathcal{G}
-gauge symmetry, or equivalently gauging a 1-form symmetry
Williamson 2016
;
Gaiotto et al. 2015
;
Kapustin and Thorngren 2013
.
We remark that the perturbations
V_{0}\,\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}
can be constructed from a linear combination of the
V_{\mu}
perturbations.

Beyond the defect ICIs, we can also extract the
\mathcal{G}
domain walls of the SET resulting from
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{h}}}=K\,(\underline{a})_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}\,,

(624)

where
{\text{\bf{gh}}}={\text{\bf{hg}}}
, and
K>0
is a normalization fixed by the equation
({\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{h}}})^{\dagger}{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{h}}}=\underline{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}
.
These domain walls can be used to extract the second cohomology label of each defect
{[\eta_{a}]\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathsf{U}(1))}
that describes its projective transformation under the global symmetry.

Furthermore, the domain walls define the full set of symmetry-twisted MES on the torus
\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}$}}}}_{{h}}}
, from which the
\mathcal{G}
-crossed
S
and
T
matrices can be extracted, see Section
VI.4
.
To calculate the permutation action on the defects, and symmetry twisted states, one can simply fuse an
\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}
loop into the skeleton of the torus

\displaystyle\ket{\text{MPO}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}\circ\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{h}}}}_{{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\,\mapsto\,\frac{{\eta_{a}({{\text{\bf{k}}},{\text{\bf{h}}}})}{\eta_{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}a}({{\text{\bf{kh}}},{\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}})}}{{\eta_{a}({{\overline{{\text{\bf{k}}}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}})}}\ket{\mathcal{B}^{{\text{\bf{${}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}$h}}}}_{{{}^{{\text{\bf{k}}}}{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}}}}\,,

(625)

see Eq.(
530
).

VIII.3
Using Morita equivalences to find all anyon condensation phase transitions

The concept of Morita equivalence plays an important role in the search for all possible boson condensation transitions in the framework we have developed.
Two MPO algebras
\mathcal{C},\,\widetilde{}\mathcal{C}
are Morita equivalent, denoted
\mathcal{C}\sim\widetilde{}\mathcal{C}
, precisely when they lead to the same emergent anyons
Müger 2003
;
Muger 2003
,
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})\cong\mathcal{Z}({\widetilde{}\mathcal{C}})
. That is, when the physical properties derived from the ICIs of the tube algebras match.

Different, Morita equivalent, MPO algebras that lead to the same emergent topological order can exhibit different
\mathcal{G}
-gradings and different
1
-sectors
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
. Hence, by considering such different presentations of the same emergent topological order we can find different
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation transitions from
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
.
An example of this phenomenon is given in Section
IX
.

By Ref.
Etingof et al. 2009
all
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation transitions from a given MTC
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
, which are dual to gauging an on-site
\mathcal{G}
symmetry on a nonchiral topological order, can be realized by considering Morita equivalent MPO algebras that lead to the same
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
emergent topological order.
To find Morita equivalent representations more explicitly would require a framework for invertible domain walls between different MPO algebras. This is more general than the
\mathcal{G}
-extension problem considered in Section
V.2
, which only considered invertible domain walls between the same MPO algebra. We plan to address this in future work.

The process we have outlined for calculating the effects of anyon condensation, by breaking the MPO symmetry down to a nontrivial subalgebra, is straightforwardly generalized beyond the case of a
\mathcal{G}
-grading. The most general case corresponds to a “grading” by an algebra, note it is trivial that every MPO symmetry is “graded” in this way by its own fusion algebra.

Motivated by the
\mathcal{G}
-graded case,
we conjecture
that all possible boson condensation phase transitions from a nonchiral topological phase
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
can be realized by symmetry breaking
down to the
1
-sector of some algebra-“graded”
MPO symmetry algebra in the
\mathcal{Z}({\mathcal{C}})
Morita equivalence class.
Further results in this direction will be presented in a forthcoming work
J. Williamson et al.
.

Beyond computing the effects of anyon condensation, the MPO symmetry breaking procedure leads to a simple method to derive the ICIs of a UFC
\mathcal{C}
from the ICIs of a modular extension of
\mathcal{C}
. This is because
\mathcal{C}
naturally forms a subcategory of any modular extension of
\mathcal{C}
.
We expect this to prove useful as explicit formulas exist for the ICIs of a modular theory, making them particularly easy to find
Koenig et al. 2010
;
Aasen
.

IX
Further examples

In this section, we present several examples that demonstrate different aspects of the constructions explained throughout the paper.
First, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-extension problem for
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
is solved explicitly.
Then, a Morita equivalent description of
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})
is used to study a
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
condensation phase transition to the doubled semion model
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{I}}}})
, induced by MPO symmetry breaking.
Finally, we study a condensation phase transition from
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{S_{3}}})
to
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}})
which increases the number of anyon types.

IX.1
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-extension of
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
and symmetry-enriched toric codes

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-extension problem for an underlying UFC
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}=\{0,\psi\}
provides a simple and illustrative example of the extension procedure described in Section
V.2
.
It corresponds to classifying anomaly-free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SET orders, where the underlying topological order is the toric code
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}})
.
One of these extensions corresponds to the EM duality-enriched toric code, which was analyzed in detail in Section
II
.

An extension is calculated in two stages, firstly we search for consistent
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded fusion rules containing
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
as the trivial sector. Note the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{{\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{x}}}\}
action on the objects of
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
must be trivial, as there is only a single nontrivial object.
Since the total quantum dimension of each
g
-sector must be the same we have
\mathcal{D}_{\text{\bf{x}}}^{2}=\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\text{\bf{1}}}=2
. Hence the
x
-sector can either consist of a single object
\sigma
with quantum dimension
\sqrt{2}
, or a pair of inequivalent objects
\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}
both with quantum dimension
1
.
We find consistent fusion rules for both of these cases, for which the potential obstructions in
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2})
disappear.

In the former case the fusion rules must be

\displaystyle\sigma\times\sigma=0+\psi\,,

(626)

and the action of the
H^{2}
torsor, permuting
0
and
\psi
, is trivial. Hence the unique choice is given by the Ising fusion rules.

In the later case there is a trivial solution to the fusion rules

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}\times\sigma_{\pm}=0\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}\times\sigma_{\mp}=\psi\,,

(627)

which corresponds to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fusion rules.

In this case the action of the
H^{2}
torsor changes the fusion rules to an inequivalent choice

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}\times\sigma_{\pm}=\psi\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}\times\sigma_{\mp}=0\,,

(628)

which corresponds to
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
fusion rules. This exhausts the different choices of consistent fusion rules.

The possible obstruction at the second stage also vanishes since
H^{4}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathsf{U}(1))=0
. For each choice of fusion rule we can now look for solutions to the pentagon equation. These are
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathsf{U}(1))
torsors, which can be realized through multiplication of a representative
F
-symbol with the nontrivial 3-cocycle function
\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}})=(-1)^{{\text{\bf{g}}}{\text{\bf{h}}}{\text{\bf{k}}}}
. That is

\displaystyle{F^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}_{def}}\mapsto\alpha({\text{\bf{g}}},{\text{\bf{h}}},{\text{\bf{k}}}){F^{{{a}_{{\text{\bf{g}}}}}{{b}_{{\text{\bf{h}}}}}{{c}_{{\text{\bf{k}}}}}}_{def\,,}}

(629)

note the only nontrivial value of
\alpha
is
\alpha({\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{x}}},{\text{\bf{x}}})=-1
.

Solutions to the pentagon equation for each choice of graded fusion rules are listed below, where we use the notation of Ref.
de Wild Propitius 1995
;
de Wild Propitius 1997
for the 3-cocycles
\omega_{\text{I}},\,\omega_{\text{II}}
.

Ising

–

For Ising fusion rules, the
F
-symbols in Eq.(
81
) solve the pentagon equation. In this case the FS indicator of
\sigma
is given by
\varkappa_{\sigma}=1
. As discussed in Section
II.1.2
this corresponds to the EM duality enriched toric code that gauges to the doubled Ising model,
\text{Ising}^{(1)}\boxtimes\overline{\text{Ising}}^{(1)}
.

–

Multiplication with the 3-cocycle
\alpha(\sigma,\sigma,\sigma)=-1
flips the phase of
F^{\sigma\sigma\sigma}_{\sigma 00}
and hence yields
\varkappa_{\sigma}=-1
.
This corresponds to the EM duality enriched toric code with a pair of self-inverse defects with negative FS indicators. Gauging this leads to the Drinfeld center of the modified Ising model with
\varkappa_{\sigma}=-1
,
\text{Ising}^{(3)}\boxtimes\overline{\text{Ising}}^{(3)}
.

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}

–

For
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fusion rules, there is a trivial solution
F^{ghk}_{abc}=\delta_{ghk,a}\delta_{gh,b}\delta_{hk,c}
which corresponds to a toric code tensor producted with a trivial paramagnet. This gauges to two copies of the toric code.

–

Multiplying by
\alpha(\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm})=-1
leads to a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-extension of
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
with two self inverse defects that have negative FS indicator
\varkappa_{\sigma_{+}}=\varkappa_{\sigma_{-}}=-1
. This corresponds to a symmetry-enriched toric code that gauges to
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{I}^{(2)}}}})
.

–

There is another distinct solution to the pentagon equation with these fusion rules, corresponding to a 3-cocycle
\omega_{\text{I}^{(1)}}\omega_{\text{II}}
, which gives a toric code tensor producted with a nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT. This gauges to a toric code tensor producted with a doubled semion model.

Multiplying by
\alpha(\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm})=-1
in this case corresponds to relabeling
\sigma_{\pm}
, and hence yields the same solution.

The general
F
-symbol solutions for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fusion rules are given by 3-cocycles representatives of the elements
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathsf{U}(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
, which are generated by
\omega_{\text{I}^{(1)}},\,\omega_{\text{I}^{(2)}},\,\omega_{\text{II}}
. For a fixed choice of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup, corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{\text{\bf{1}}}
, there are four inequivalent 3-cocycles with a trivial restriction. Two of these 3-cocycles are captured by the first two cases above, while the other two are both captured by the third case.

\mathbb{Z}_{4}

–

For
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
fusion rules, again there is the trivial solution, which corresponds to a toric code with two pairs of topologically distinct defects that are mutual inverses. This gauges to the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
quantum double model
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})
.

–

Multiplying by
\alpha(\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm},\sigma_{\pm})=-1
corresponds to a different symmetry-enriched toric code with two pairs of mutual inverse defects. This gauges to the
\omega_{\text{I}}^{2}
-twisted
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
quantum double model
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}^{\omega_{\text{I}}^{2}}})
.

The general
F
-symbol solutions for
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
fusion rules are given by 3-cocycles representatives of the elements
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},\mathsf{U}(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
. There are two 3-cocycles that have trivial restriction to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroup. These correspond to the two cases above.

We remark that similar treatments of this example have appeared in Refs.
Cheng et al. 2016
;
Heinrich et al. 2016
.

IX.2
Morita equivalent MPO algebras
{\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\sim\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}}
and condensation to the doubled semion model

In Ref.
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
a condensation phase transition from
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})
to the doubled semion model
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{I}}}
was described in terms of a boundary SPT phase under the MPO symmetry. Here we revisit this condensation with our framework, and by using a Morita equivalent description of the topological order
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})\cong\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}})
we find a reduction of the MPO symmetry that induces the same
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
condensation phase transition without any reference to a boundary SPT. This exemplifies the general fact, that all
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation phase transitions can be realized through
\mathcal{G}
-graded MPO symmetry breaking.
Hence any condensation phase transition corresponding to a boundary SPT phase in the language of Ref.
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
, can be induced by symmetry breaking in some Morita equivalent MPO algebra using our framework.
Our approach has the advantage that it captures all
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
condensation phase transition, while no such proof was given in Ref.
Duivenvoorden et al. 2017
.

There is a well known Morita equivalence
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}\sim\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}
, see Ref.
de Wild Propitius 1995
, and hence

{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})\cong\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}})}
.
Therefore, to describe an emergent
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})
topological order we may use an MPO algebra
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}
.

let us first construct the tube algebra for
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}
and find its ICIs, from which one can verify the equivalence
{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}}})\cong\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}})}
. Since the fusion rules are Abelian and all group elements are self inverse, the tubes appearing are of the form
\mathcal{T}^{h}_{g,g+h,g}
and we use the shorthand notation
\mathcal{T}^{h}_{g}:=\alpha(h,g+h,h)\mathcal{T}^{h}_{g,g+h,g}
, established in Eq.(
583
).
This tube algebra has multiplication rules

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{h}_{f}\mathcal{T}^{k}_{g}=\delta_{f,g}\,\alpha^{g}(h,k)\mathcal{T}^{h+k}_{g}\,,

(630)

where
\alpha^{g}
is the slant product defined in Eq.(
578
). The 3-cocycle is given by
\alpha\equiv\omega_{\text{II}}
where

\displaystyle\omega_{\text{II}}(A,B,C)=(-1)^{a_{0}b_{1}c_{1}}\,,

and

\displaystyle A=(a_{0},a_{1})\,.

(631)

Using the ICI formula for twisted gauge theory, given in Eq.(
590
), we find

the table gives the elements

of the ICIs and should be read as follows

\displaystyle\underline{a_{i}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}^{2}}\sum_{j}t_{i}^{j}(\mathcal{T}^{g}_{h})_{j},

(649)

where
\mathcal{D}^{2}=4
in this case.

There are several
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-gradings of
\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{\omega_{\text{II}}}
that we could consider for
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{x}}}}

\displaystyle\{00,01\}\oplus\{10,11\},\,

\displaystyle\{00,10\}\oplus\{01,11\},\,

\displaystyle\{00,11\}\oplus\{01,10\}\,.

(650)

The first two are equivalent and lead to a condensation to the toric code phase, while the third leads to a condensation to the doubled semion phase. This is because the restriction of the 3-cocycle
\omega_{\text{II}}
to the
\{00,11\}
subgroup lies in the nontrivial cohomology class of
{\mathcal{H}^{3}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathsf{U}(1))=\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
.
We remark that shifting by a
\omega_{I}
cocycle on any of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroups rearranges which of the above
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-gradings corresponds to the doubled semion phase.

We choose the
\{00,11\}\oplus\{01,10\}
grading for the MPO symmetry algebra. Then breaking the MPO symmetry down to
\mathcal{C}_{{\text{\bf{1}}}}
leads to a
\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})
condensation to a symmetry enriched doubled semion phase described in the table below.

).

Denote the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generator on defect
a
by
X_{a}
, then
X_{a}^{2}=1
for
a\in\{0,s{\overline{s}},\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}\}
and

for
a\in\{s,{\overline{s}},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}
. We remark, in this case, the projective phase
X_{a}^{2}=-1
can be removed by a choice of coboundary. It is important, however, to keep track of the choice when calculating the effect of gauging the symmetry.

This example demonstrates that the topological data obtained by gauging an SET is not a faithful label.
In particular one finds the same gauged data for the symmetry-enriched doubled semion, described in Eq.(
IX.2
), and the symmetry-enriched toric code, described as the first case under
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
in Section
IX.1
. For this example, the gauge invariant
S^{({\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{1}}})},T^{({\text{\bf{1}}},{\text{\bf{1}}})}
matrices of the SETs clearly distinguish the two SET phases, as they have different underlying topological orders.

We remark that even when the underlying topological order is the same, the gauged data still does not provide a faithful label. A well known example of this occurs for the trivial
D_{4}
SPT and the type-III
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
SPT, since
{\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{D_{4}}})\cong\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}}^{\omega_{\text{III}}}})}
and the underlying topological orders are both trivial
de Wild Propitius 1995
;
Wang et al. 2015
;
Bridgeman and Williamson 2017
. In this case the SPTs are clearly distinguished by their different symmetry groups.

IX.3
A phase transition from
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{S_{3}}})
to
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}})

For a final example, we use the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading of
\text{Vec}_{S_{3}}
to study a phase transition to
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}})
. We remark that one can start from the Morita equivalent
\text{Rep}(S_{3})
and follow a similar approach to study a phase transition to
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Rep}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})})
that is not induced by a
\text{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
boson condensation. We plan to explore this further in a future work
J. Williamson et al.
.

We work with the following presentation of
S_{3}

\displaystyle\left\langle s,r\ |\ s^{2}=r^{3}=(sr)^{2}=1\right\rangle\,,

(679)

hence the set of group elements is given by
\{1,\,r,\,{\overline{r}},\,s,\,sr,\,s{\overline{r}}\}
.
Furthermore, we denote the irreducible representations of
S_{3}
by
\{0,\,\psi,\,\pi\}
, correspond to the trivial, sign, and two-dimensional irrep, respectively.

The fusion rules of
\text{Vec}_{S_{3}}
are given by
S_{3}
multiplication, and the
F
-symbols are trivial.
The anyons of
\mathcal{Z}({\text{Vec}_{S_{3}}})
can be labeled by a conjugacy class, together with an irreducible representation of the centralizer of a representative from the conjugacy class. We use the notation
{[g,\rho]}
to indicate the conjugacy class
{C}_{g}
and an irrep
\rho
of the centralizer
{Z}_{g}
.
A formula for the ICIs is given in Eq.(
590
).

in the above table.

The full two dimensional
\underline{{[1,\pi]}}

\displaystyle\underline{{[1,\pi]}}_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\left(\mathcal{T}^{1}_{1}+e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{3}}\mathcal{T}^{r}_{1}+e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}\mathcal{T}^{{\overline{r}}}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\underline{{[1,\pi]}}_{01}
